Three parts cover nonlinear exchange dynamics, partition-related coefficient inequalities,
and partial results on involution log concavity. This edition integrates the full contents

\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}
This manuscript brings together eight studies of nonlinear exchange dynamics and
coefficient inequalities. The exchange chapters establish quantitative convergence
and entropy estimates in their stated parameter regions, and separately exhibit a
tail obstruction to an infinite-state exponential-convergence conjecture. The
partition chapters present complete manuscript claims concerning reciprocal-subsum
denominator log concavity, unimodality of shifted Nekrasov--Okounkov polynomials,
and uniform log concavity of regular partition numbers. Their analytic proofs,
finite coverage arguments and executable certificates are retained in full. The
involution chapter proves infinite edge strips and eventual fixed linear-window
log concavity, while retaining the unresolved status of the full Dukes conjecture.
The eight studies have related probabilistic, analytic and coefficient methods,
but they do not constitute one overarching theorem. A common structure, uniquely
namespaced references, a consolidated bibliography and an exact recoverable source
archive make their results and qualifications available in one standalone document.

\chapter*{Preface and Scope}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface and Scope}
\section*{How to read this edition}
The chapters preserve all theorem statements, proofs, substantive discussions,
limitations, numerical certificate specifications and code listings in the eight
source manuscripts. Each original abstract appears as a chapter overview.
Repeated title pages and local tables of contents are replaced by common front
matter, while the source bibliographies are gathered at the end with reference
labels of the form chapter.entry. Repeated works retain their source descriptions. Mathematical
notation remains local to its chapter: identical symbols in different chapters
need not denote identical objects. Section, equation and theorem references are
made unique for this edition. Appendices remain attached to their originating
chapters and are numbered with that chapter prefix.

The organization does not claim a new theorem connecting all eight subjects.
Part I concerns exchange dynamics, Part II concerns partition-related coefficient
inequalities, and Part III contains partial involution results. The commonality
is methodological and editorial, not a claim that one proof settles all the
questions treated here.

\section*{Status of the results}
Chapters 1--3 are quantitative convergence studies with different hypotheses and
different levels of explicitness. Chapter 1 retains a nonexplicit discrete-time
prefactor and separates continuous-time entropy decay from discrete-time
convergence. Chapter 2 fixes a closed marginal or density region strictly below
$1/(\Delta+1)$; it does not assert uniformity up to that endpoint. Chapter 3 gives
an explicit entropy contraction under its stated local condition, including the
uniform range $\beta\leq1/(2\Delta+1)$, and does not settle the remaining
conjectured region.

Chapter 4 is an explicit application of a known slow-tail mechanism, not a claim
to have discovered that mechanism. Its obstruction targets a concluding
conjecture under broad hypotheses, not the stronger proved exponential-decay
theorem discussed in the chapter. Chapters 5--7 retain the original manuscripts'
full claims in their stated scopes, with computer-assisted finite components.
Chapter 5 classifies reciprocal-subsum denominator log concavity; Chapter 6 claims
unimodality, not global coefficient log concavity; Chapter 7 includes the sharp
$k\geq30$, $n\geq26$ regular-partition assertion and its stated extensions.
Chapter 8 remains explicitly partial: the full all-$n$, all-$k$ Dukes conjecture
is unresolved, the linear-window threshold is existential, and the Bernstein and
four-step candidates are not promoted to proved statements.

These are the claims and qualifications of the source research manuscripts, not
an assertion of independent peer review, publication priority or formal
proof-assistant verification. Finite certificate execution checks only the
specified computational components. Infinite conclusions still depend on the
analytic and algebraic arguments and the exact coverage of finite and infinite
regions given in the chapters. This consolidation performs no new proof search
and does not enlarge any theorem's domain.

\section*{Source and reproducibility policy}
All eight manuscripts are attributed to KEIO. The source manifest below identifies
the exact input files by SHA-256. The consolidated \texttt{.tex} file is sufficient
to typeset the complete manuscript: it requires no external input files,
bibliography databases, images or PDFs. The original code payloads are unchanged.
Their source-specific extraction markers and original filenames are retained in
an exact archive after the document terminator. The recovery instructions below
restore those originals, so the chapter-specific replay drivers can be used
without modifying their assumptions or mixing similarly named files across studies.

\section*{Compiling and recovering the exact sources}
Typeset this file with ordinary \texttt{pdflatex}; run it three times to settle
the contents and cross-references. Compilation does not execute any verification
code. To restore the exact source files, save the following listing as
\texttt{recover\_sources.py} and run it in a writable directory:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=collectedbase,language=bash]
python3 recover_sources.py KEIO_Collected_Research_01_08_2026.tex originals
\end{lstlisting}
The destination must not already exist. The recovery program validates every
source hash before writing files. Then use each original manuscript's replay
instructions in its own fresh working directory. In particular, do not run a
chapter-specific extractor against the complete consolidated file: the exact
originals are the intended inputs to those unchanged drivers.

\begin{lstlisting}[style=collectedbase,language=Python]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Recover the exact eight source manuscripts from the consolidated TEX.
Usage: python3 recover_sources.py CONSOLIDATED.tex NEW_DIRECTORY
"""
import base64
import hashlib
import json
from pathlib import Path
import re
import sys

if len(sys.argv) != 3:
    raise SystemExit(__doc__)
text = Path(sys.argv[1]).read_text(encoding="utf-8")
pattern = (r"^% BEGIN ORIGINAL SOURCE (\d{2}) (\S+) "
           r"([0-9a-f]{64})\n(.*?)"
           r"^% END ORIGINAL SOURCE \1$")
blocks = re.findall(pattern, text, flags=re.M | re.S)
if [int(b[0]) for b in blocks] != list(range(1, 9)):
    raise SystemExit("Expected exactly eight ordered source blocks")
records = []
decoded = []
for number, name, expected, payload in blocks:
    if Path(name).name != name:
        raise SystemExit("Unsafe source filename")
    lines = payload.splitlines()
    if not lines or any(not line.startswith("% ") for line in lines):
        raise SystemExit("Malformed base64 archive")
    raw = base64.b64decode("".join(line[2:] for line in lines),
                          validate=True)
    digest = hashlib.sha256(raw).hexdigest()
    if digest != expected:
        raise SystemExit("SHA-256 mismatch: " + name)
    decoded.append((name, raw))
    records.append(dict(chapter=int(number), filename=name,
                        bytes=len(raw), sha256=digest))
dest = Path(sys.argv[2])
dest.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=False)
for name, raw in decoded:
    (dest / name).write_bytes(raw)
(dest / "manifest.json").write_text(
    json.dumps(records, indent=2) + "\n", encoding="utf-8")
print("PASS: recovered eight exact sources in", dest)
\end{lstlisting}
Chapters 1--4 contain analytic proofs, not computational certificates.
Chapters 5--8 print their executable sources and specify requirements, replay
modes and expected outputs. The separate verification bundle is not required
to compile or recover this standalone source.

\chapter{Uniform Convergence and Entropy Bounds for Nonlinear Exchange Dynamics}\label{chapter:1}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\thechapter.\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\theHchapter.\arabic{section}}
\lstset{style=collectedbase}

\begin{chapterabstract}
For each fixed finite interaction matrix, we establish uniform exponential convergence in total variation for the discrete-time nonlinear Glauber and block dynamics of Caputo--Sinclair. The constants are uniform over every initial law, including laws whose one-site marginals approach or lie on the boundary. A bounded-perturbation argument supplies explicit asymptotic exponents; a separate entropy reweighting argument supplies global continuous-time entropy decay. We rederive their qualitative convergence statement by minorization and entropy monotonicity. The proof uses spectral stability of the linearization on the fixed-marginal tangent space at every equilibrium, including boundary equilibria. Compactness then gives a common local contraction and a uniform entrance time. The discrete-time prefactor remains nonquantitative. The argument is self-contained apart from the cited zero-interaction entropy-production inequalities; its relationship to earlier work is discussed explicitly.
\end{chapterabstract}

\section{Background and scope}
The dynamics below are exactly the discrete-time maps (2.6) and (2.9) of Caputo--Sinclair \cite{p1:CS}. Their Theorem 2.7 establishes qualitative convergence for arbitrary finite interactions; Remark 2.11 covers pinned spins. Section 5, question 1 asks for exponential decay for arbitrary interactions, even with a very bad dependence on dimension. The result below proves existence of constants depending only on the fixed interaction matrix. The quantitative section supplies an explicit lower bound on the asymptotic decay exponent, but not an explicit discrete-time prefactor or mixing-time bound.

A closely related 2025 question of Caputo--Morellini asks for uniform covariance bounds for constrained replicas, as a route to continuous-time entropy decay. A 13 October 2026 GitHub manuscript by Kaiwen Jin \cite{p1:Jin} claims that covariance bound; the consulted registry labels it an unverified full-resolution claim. The covariance statement, combined with the Kac-program and linearization arguments of \cite{p1:CM,p1:CS18}, supplies a prior route to the present qualitative all-interaction conclusion. The present working paper gives an independent proof and explicit perturbation bounds; it does not claim priority for the qualitative conclusion. The covariance preprint does not itself derive entropy-decay estimates.

\section{Definitions and statement}
Fix $n\geq1$, $\Omega=\{-1,1\}^n$, and a finite real symmetric matrix $J$. Put
\[
 w(x)=\exp\{\tfrac12x^{\mathsf T}Jx\}>0.
\]
For $A\subseteq[n]$, let $S_A(x,y)=(y_Ax_{A^c},x_Ay_{A^c})$. We use either of the following Markov kernels on ordered pairs:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The block kernel selects $A$ with probability proportional to $w((S_A(x,y))_1)w((S_A(x,y))_2)$ and outputs $S_A(x,y)$.
\item The single-site kernel chooses $i$ uniformly and replaces $(x,y)$ by $S_{\{i\}}(x,y)$ with probability
\[
 a_i(x,y)=\frac{w((S_i(x,y))_1)w((S_i(x,y))_2)}{w(x)w(y)+w((S_i(x,y))_1)w((S_i(x,y))_2)};
\]
otherwise it leaves the pair unchanged.
\end{enumerate}
Let $Q$ be the selected pair kernel and $Q_1(z\mid x,y)$ its first-output marginal. Define the polynomial map
\[
 T(p)(z)=\sum_{x,y}p(x)p(y)Q_1(z\mid x,y).
\]
The kernel commutes with exchange of the two replicas. Consequently the two output marginals agree when the input law is $p\otimes p$. Every one-site marginal of $p$ is conserved by $T$.

Write $m(p)=(\sum_xp(x)x_i)_{i=1}^n$. For $m\in[-1,1]^n$, denote by $\mu_m$ the unique maximizer, among probabilities of mean vector $m$, of
\[
 \mathcal F(p)=H(p)+\sum_xp(x)\log w(x).
\]
On the subcube allowed by the pinned coordinates $m_i=\pm1$, this maximizer has full support and the Gibbs form $\mu_m(x)\propto w(x)e^{h\cdot x}$ with finite fields on the unpinned coordinates. It is stationary for $T$.

\begin{p1theorem}\label{p1:thm:main}
For either kernel above, there exist finite constants $C=C(J)\geq1$ and $c=c(J)>0$ such that, simultaneously for all $p\in\pOnePp(\Omega)$ and all integers $t\geq0$,
\[
 \|T^t(p)-\mu_{m(p)}\|_{\pOneTV}\leq C e^{-ct}.
\]
In particular, the constants do not depend on the initial marginals or on their distance to deterministic marginals.
\end{p1theorem}

\section{Continuity and the fixed tangent space}
\begin{p1lemma}\label{p1:lem:equilibria}
The map $m\mapsto\mu_m$ is continuous on the closed cube. The set $\mathcal M=\{\mu_m:m\in[-1,1]^n\}$ is compact. For every $p$,
\[
 T^t(p)\longrightarrow\mu_{m(p)}.
\]
\end{p1lemma}
\begin{proof}
Existence follows from compactness, and uniqueness from strict concavity of entropy. On the allowed subcube a full-support feasible law exists (the product law). Mixing any putative maximizer with that law shows, by the infinite positive one-sided entropy derivative at a zero cell, that the maximizer is positive. Lagrange multipliers on that subcube give its Gibbs form.

For continuity, let $m^{(k)}\to m$. Any subsequential limit $\nu$ of $\mu_{m^{(k)}}$ has mean $m$. For any law $p$ of mean $m$, there exist $p^{(k)}\to p$ with means $m^{(k)}$: independently on each coordinate, flip the required fraction of $-1$ spins to $+1$ or vice versa to correct its marginal. A correction at coordinate $i$ changes total variation by at most $|m_i^{(k)}-m_i|/2$ and preserves every other marginal. Thus all corrections change total variation by at most half the sum of these differences. Optimality and continuity of $\mathcal F$ give $\mathcal F(\nu)\geq\mathcal F(p)$. Uniqueness implies $\nu=\mu_m$, proving continuity. Compactness follows. The last assertion is established in \cite[Theorem 2.7 and Remark 2.11]{p1:CS}. For a self-contained argument, we prove it again below using the same classical positivity and entropy principles.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[A self-contained proof of qualitative convergence]
Let $Q^0$ be the pair kernel that chooses one coordinate uniformly and exchanges its two spins with probability $1/2$. The positive transitions of $Q^0$ are the identity and the one-site exchanges. Every such transition has positive probability for each of our two kernels. Since the pair space is finite, there exists $a=a(J)>0$, with $a\leq1$, such that $Q\geq aQ^0$ entrywise. This comparison is made with the original finite weight $w$ on the full cube, so $a$ does not depend on the equilibrium fields or marginals.

Fix the initial one-site marginals and write $\pi=\bigotimes_i p_i$. On this marginal fiber the nonlinear map for $Q^0$ is the positive linear map
\[
 L_\pi q=\tfrac12q+\frac1{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n\pi_i\otimes q_{-i}.
\]
Consequently $T(q)\geq a L_\pi q$ for every probability $q$ in the fiber. Conservation of marginals and positivity of $L_\pi$ imply inductively
\[
 T^k(p)\geq a^k L_\pi^k p.
\]
In $n$ steps of $L_\pi$, the term that refreshes coordinates $1,2,\ldots,n$ in that order is exactly $(2n)^{-n}\pi$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{p1:eq:minorization}
 T^t(p)\geq b\pi\quad(t\geq n),\qquad b=a^n(2n)^{-n}>0.
\end{equation}
For $t>n$, apply the same estimate to $T^{t-n}(p)$, which has the same marginals. On the allowed subcube $S$, $\pi$ is strictly positive; therefore the trajectory after time $n$ lies in a compact subset of the strictly positive laws on $S$.

Write $\mu=\mu_{m(p)}$, which is positive on $S$, and let $D$ denote relative entropy. For a positive law $q$ on $S$, pair reversibility and the log-sum inequality give
\[
 D((q\otimes q)Q\Vert\mu\otimes\mu)\leq2D(q\Vert\mu).
\]
The two output marginals are both $T(q)$, so entropy subadditivity also gives
\[
 2D(T(q)\Vert\mu)\leq D((q\otimes q)Q\Vert\mu\otimes\mu).
\]
The first inequality is strict unless $q(x)q(y)/(\mu(x)\mu(y))$ is constant on every communicating class of $Q$. To see the equality condition directly, apply strict convexity of $u\log u$ to the reversed transition probabilities at each output state. The positive diagonal forces the density to agree across every positive transition, and hence throughout every class. Since all one-site swaps are positive transitions, equality implies that $h=\log(q/\mu)$ is additive, by the coordinate-difference argument in Lemma~\ref{p1:lem:interior}. Therefore $q$ is a Gibbs law with the same weight and marginals as $\mu$, and uniqueness of the free-energy maximizer gives $q=\mu$.

It follows that $D(T(q)\Vert\mu)<D(q\Vert\mu)$ unless $q=\mu$. The entropy along the trajectory decreases to a limit. If a subsequence $T^{t_j}(p)$ converges to $q$, then \eqref{p1:eq:minorization} makes $q$ positive on $S$. Continuity implies $D(T(q)\Vert\mu)=D(q\Vert\mu)$, because both the time-$t_j$ and time-$(t_j+1)$ entropies have the same limit. Hence $q=\mu$. Compactness shows that the entire trajectory converges to $\mu$.
\end{proof}

Let the fixed vector space
\[
 E=\left\{v\in\mathbb R^{\Omega}:\sum_xv(x)=0,\quad \sum_xv(x)x_i=0\ (1\leq i\leq n)\right\}
\]
be the tangent space of each affine fixed-marginal fiber. For $\mu\in\mathcal M$, write $A_\mu=DT(\mu)$. Explicitly,
\begin{equation}\label{p1:eq:derivative}
 (A_\mu v)(z)=\sum_{x,y}\{v(x)\mu(y)+\mu(x)v(y)\}Q_1(z\mid x,y).
\end{equation}
The space $E$ is invariant under $A_\mu$: differentiating mass and marginal conservation along the simplex tangent space proves this, or one can check it directly in \eqref{p1:eq:derivative}.

\section{Interior spectral stability}
The linearization and conserved-mode framework has a direct precursor in \cite[Section 2.3]{p1:CS18}; these ingredients are not claimed as new. The following statement also applies to a resident subcube, with the other coordinates pinned. A single-site update at a pinned coordinate is then the identity.
\begin{p1lemma}\label{p1:lem:interior}
Suppose $\mu$ is a Gibbs equilibrium with full support on a subcube $S$. On vectors supported on $S$ with zero mass, $A_\mu$ is self-adjoint in the density representation $v=\mu h$ and has spectrum in $[0,1]$. Its eigenspace for eigenvalue $1$ consists exactly of the vectors $\mu h$ for which $h$ is an additive one-site function with $\mu[h]=0$. Hence on vectors additionally having zero one-site marginals its spectral radius is strictly below $1$. On all vectors supported on $S$, the remaining mass eigenvalue is $2$.
\end{p1lemma}
\begin{proof}
Work on $S$, where $\mu>0$. The pair kernel is reversible for $\mu\otimes\mu$. In the block case it is conditional expectation onto the equivalence classes of coordinatewise pair sums. Each one-site kernel is conditional expectation onto its two-point exchange orbits, and the Glauber kernel is an average of these conditional expectations. Thus in both cases $Q$ is a self-adjoint positive-semidefinite contraction on $L^2(\mu\otimes\mu)$.

Put $Mh(x,y)=h(x)+h(y)$ and let $Lh=(A_\mu(\mu h))/\mu$. Reversibility and replica-exchange symmetry yield
\begin{equation}\label{p1:eq:compression}
 \langle h,Lk\rangle_\mu=\tfrac12\langle Mh,Q Mk\rangle_{\mu\otimes\mu}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, reversibility expresses $Lk(x)$ as the $\mu$-average in $y$ of $Q Mk(x,y)$; this function is symmetric in the two replicas, allowing the average of $h(x)$ and $h(y)$. If $\mu[h]=0$, then $\|Mh\|^2=2\|h\|^2$. Formula \eqref{p1:eq:compression} proves self-adjointness and the spectral interval $[0,1]$ on the zero-mean space.

Equality in its upper bound holds precisely when $Q Mh=Mh$. Since every single-site exchange has positive transition probability, this is equivalent to
\[
 h(x)+h(y)=h(y_i x_{-i})+h(x_i y_{-i})
\]
for every free $i$ and every $x,y\in S$. Taking $x_i=+1$, $y_i=-1$ shows that $h(+1,u)-h(-1,u)$ is independent of the complementary configuration $u$. Applying this for each coordinate shows $h$ is additive. Conversely additive $h$ makes $Mh$ collision-invariant and, if centered, gives $Lh=h$.

If $v=\mu h$ has zero mass and all zero one-site marginals, then $h$ is orthogonal to every additive function. It therefore has no eigenvalue-$1$ component. Finite dimension proves strict spectral stability. Finally, $A_\mu\mu=2\mu$ by homogeneity, while the zero-mass space is invariant, establishing the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\section{Boundary spectral stability}
\begin{p1proposition}\label{p1:prop:boundary}
For every $\mu\in\mathcal M$, including every boundary equilibrium,
\[
 \rho(A_\mu|_E)<1.
\]
\end{p1proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $R$ be the pinned coordinates of $\mu$, with pinned values $b_i$, and let $F=[n]\setminus R$. The support $S$ of $\mu$ is the full subcube $x_R=b_R$, and $\mu$ is positive on $S$. For each configuration $x$, define its mutant set
\[
 B(x)=\{i\in R:x_i\ne b_i\}.
\]
Decompose a vector into blocks $v_B$ according to $B(x)=B\subseteq R$. In the derivative \eqref{p1:eq:derivative}, one parent is a resident drawn from $\mu$ and the other has mutant set $B$. The two offspring partition those mutant sites between them. Consequently each offspring has a mutant set contained in $B$. The matrix $A_\mu$ is therefore block triangular when subsets are ordered by inclusion (or decreasing cardinality). Write $D_B$ for its diagonal block.

For $B\ne\varnothing$, the column sum of $D_B$ is the expected number of offspring whose mutant set is exactly $B$ when one parent is the specified mutant and the other is an independent resident. This interpretation follows directly from replica symmetry in \eqref{p1:eq:derivative}. This number is at most one.

If $|B|\geq2$, there is strictly positive probability of exchanging just one site of $B$. This produces two nonempty proper mutant subsets, so neither offspring retains the full set $B$. Every input mutant and resident has positive probability for such an exchange, and there are finitely many inputs. Thus all column sums of $D_B$ are strictly below one, and $\rho(D_B)<1$.

If $B=\{i\}$, exactly one offspring always carries that mutant site, so $D_B$ is column-stochastic. It is in fact strictly positive: for any desired free-coordinate configuration $z_F$, choose a resident $y$ with $y_F=z_F$, which has positive $\mu$-probability, and exchange only site $i$. The resulting mutant carrier has free coordinates $z_F$. This event has positive probability for both kernels. Perron--Frobenius therefore gives a simple eigenvalue $1$, a strictly positive eigenvector, and all other eigenvalues strictly inside the unit circle. An eigenvector for eigenvalue $1$ necessarily has nonzero total mass.

For $B=\varnothing$, $D_\varnothing$ is the resident linearization of Lemma~\ref{p1:lem:interior}: its spectrum is contained in $[0,1]\cup\{2\}$, its $2$-eigenvectors are multiples of $\mu$, and its $1$-eigenvectors have centered additive densities.

Suppose now $v\in E$ is a nonzero complex eigenvector of $A_\mu$ with $|\lambda|\geq1$. The triangular equations, starting with maximal mutant sets, give $v_B=0$ for all $|B|\geq2$. If $\lambda\ne1$, they also give $v_B=0$ for singleton $B$. If $\lambda=1$, the constraint that the opposite-to-pinned marginal at site $i$ vanishes is
\[
 0=\sum_{B\ni i}\sum_{x:B(x)=B}v_B(x)=\sum_{x:B(x)=\{i\}}v_{\{i\}}(x).
\]
The preceding Perron--Frobenius observation again gives $v_{\{i\}}=0$. Thus in every case $v$ is supported on the resident subcube and has zero mass and zero free-site marginals. Lemma~\ref{p1:lem:interior} contradicts $|\lambda|\geq1$. Hence the restricted spectral radius is strictly below one.
\end{proof}

\section{Compactness upgrades spectral stability to uniform exponential decay}
\begin{p1lemma}\label{p1:lem:upgrade}
Let $K$ be a compact convex subset of a finite-dimensional affine space, $T$ a polynomial map carrying $K$ into itself, and $r:K\to K$ a continuous retraction onto fixed points. Assume $r(Tp)=r(p)$, $p-r(p)\in E$ for a fixed vector space $E$, and that the affine fibers $r^{-1}(\mu)$ lie in $\mu+E$. Assume also $T^t(p)\to r(p)$ for every $p\in K$ and $\rho(DT(\mu)|_E)<1$ at every fixed point $\mu\in r(K)$. Then there are $C<\infty,c>0$ with
\[
 \|T^t(p)-r(p)\|\leq C e^{-ct}
\]
for all $p\in K$ and integers $t\geq0$, provided $E$ is derivative-invariant along the relevant affine fibers.
\end{p1lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use a Euclidean norm. The family of restricted derivatives $A_\mu$ is compact. Continuity of spectral radius gives $\sup_\mu\rho(A_\mu)<1$. Their powers converge to zero uniformly: choose a circle of radius $a<1$ enclosing every spectrum, bound the resolvents $(zI-A_\mu)^{-1}$ uniformly on that circle by compactness, and use the Cauchy integral formula for $A_\mu^k$. Choose an integer $s$ such that $\sup_\mu\|A_\mu^s\|<1/4$.

Since $T(\mu)=\mu$, $D(T^s)(\mu)|_E=A_\mu^s$. Uniform continuity of the derivative gives $\eta>0$ such that
\[
 \|T^s(p)-\mu\|\leq\tfrac12\|p-\mu\|
\]
whenever $p\in K$, $r(p)=\mu$, and $\|p-\mu\|\leq\eta$. This follows by integrating the derivative along the segment from $\mu$ to $p$; in the present application $K$ is convex and the segment remains in the fixed affine fiber. More generally convexity of these local segments is sufficient.

Set $U=T^s$. For every $p$, pointwise convergence gives some integer $k(p)$ with $\|U^{k(p)}p-r(p)\|<\eta/2$. Continuity gives an open neighborhood $V_p$ on which $\|U^{k(p)}q-r(q)\|<\eta$. A finite subcover of $K$ and the local contraction imply, for $k_0=\max k(p)$ among that subcover,
\[
 \sup_{q\in K}\|U^{k_0+j}q-r(q)\|\leq \eta 2^{-j}\qquad(j\geq0).
\]
Indeed each $q$ enters the local contraction neighborhood at its own selected time no later than $k_0$, and remains there at all later $U$-times. Finally, the finitely many maps $T^a$, $0\leq a<s$, are uniformly Lipschitz on $K$ (or on its compact convex hull). Writing $t=sk+a$ establishes the result with $c=(\log2)/s$ and a finite prefactor absorbing the entrance time.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{p1:thm:main}]
Take $K=\pOnePp(\Omega)$, $r(p)=\mu_{m(p)}$, and the fixed space $E$ defined above. Conservation of marginals gives all fiber properties and derivative invariance. Lemma~\ref{p1:lem:equilibria} gives continuity and pointwise convergence. Proposition~\ref{p1:prop:boundary} verifies strict spectral stability on the entire compact equilibrium set, not merely its interior. Lemma~\ref{p1:lem:upgrade}, followed by equivalence of finite-dimensional norms, proves the asserted total variation bound.
\end{proof}

\section{An explicit bounded-perturbation spectral estimate}
Let $R=\max_xw(x)/\min_xw(x)$. Define $g_0=1/n$ for the single-site kernel and $g_0=1/2$ for the block kernel. For either kernel, the following bound holds at every Gibbs equilibrium, including boundary equilibria:
\[
 \rho(DT(\mu)|_E)\leq1-\varepsilon,
 \qquad \varepsilon=R^{-2}g_0.
\]
Here the spectral radius statement uses the positive semidefiniteness of the heat-bath kernels.

To prove the estimate first suppose $\mu>0$. Write $\mu=w\gamma/Z$, where $\gamma$ is the product probability determined by its external fields and $Z=\gamma[w]$. Put $m=\min w$, $M=\max w$, $l=m/Z$, and $u=M/Z$. Then $l\gamma\leq\mu\leq u\gamma$. The product law $\gamma$ need not have the same marginals as $\mu$.

For each exchange orbit partition, the heat-bath operator is the orthogonal projection onto the space of orbit-invariant functions. Although the projection depends on the reference weights, this invariant subspace does not. Thus the variational formula for squared distance gives, for every pair function $F$,
\[
 \langle F,(I-Q_J)F\rangle_{\mu\otimes\mu}
 \geq l^2\langle F,(I-Q_0)F\rangle_{\gamma\otimes\gamma}.
\]
For the block kernel this is one projection comparison; for the single-site kernel average this comparison over sites.

Let $\mathrm{Add}$ denote all additive one-site functions. The orthogonal product decomposition of $L^2(\gamma)$ gives
\[
 \tfrac12\langle Mh,(I-Q_0)Mh\rangle_{\gamma\otimes\gamma}
 \geq g_0\inf_{a\in\mathrm{Add}}\|h-a\|_\gamma^2.
\]
Indeed, on a product component involving $k\geq2$ coordinates, the linearized single-site eigenvalue is $1-k/(2n)$ and the block eigenvalue is $2^{1-k}$. Additive components have zero Dirichlet energy. If $\mu h\in E$, then $h$ is $\mu$-orthogonal to $\mathrm{Add}$, so
\[
 \inf_{a\in\mathrm{Add}}\|h-a\|_\gamma^2
 \geq u^{-1}\inf_{a\in\mathrm{Add}}\|h-a\|_\mu^2
 =u^{-1}\|h\|_\mu^2.
\]
The density compression identity therefore yields
\[
 \langle h,(I-L)h\rangle_\mu
 \geq(l^2/u)g_0\|h\|_\mu^2
 \geq R^{-2}g_0\|h\|_\mu^2,
\]
because $l^2/u=m^2/(ZM)\geq m^2/M^2$. Interior equilibria are dense in the closed equilibrium set, and the restricted matrix depends continuously on $\mu$, so the same spectral-radius bound extends to the boundary.

The perturbation and product spectral arguments are classical in character. In particular the linearization/conserved-mode framework appears in Caputo--Sinclair, arXiv:1609.06897, Section 2.3. This comparison is not represented as a new general mathematical principle.

\subsection{Explicit asymptotic rate; nonexplicit prefactor}
The uniform spectral gap supplies an explicit exponential exponent without an explicit entrance time. Put $a=1-\varepsilon/2$ and, on $E$, define
\[
 N_\mu(v)=\sum_{k\geq0}a^{-k}\|A_\mu^k v\|_1.
\]
Uniform resolvent estimates on the compact family $\{A_\mu|_E\}$ show that there is $B<\infty$ with
\[
 \|v\|_1\leq N_\mu(v)\leq B\|v\|_1,
 \qquad N_\mu(A_\mu v)\leq aN_\mu(v)
\]
uniformly in $\mu$. Since $T$ is quadratic with a stochastic pair kernel,
\[
 T(\mu+v)-\mu=A_\mu v+T(v),\qquad \|T(v)\|_1\leq\|v\|_1^2.
\]
The remainder belongs to $E$. If $N_\mu(v)\leq\eta=\varepsilon/(4B)$, then
\[
 N_\mu(T(\mu+v)-\mu)
 \leq(1-\varepsilon/4)N_\mu(v).
\]
Every trajectory eventually enters the smaller ordinary ball $\|v\|_1<\eta/B$. Continuity of the equilibrium section and a finite-cover argument give a common entrance time on the simplex, exactly as in the compact-upgrade lemma. Consequently, for some finite $C(w)$,
\[
 \|T^t(p)-\mu_{m(p)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \leq C(w)e^{-\varepsilon t/4}.
\]
Thus one can take $c=R^{-2}/(4n)$ for single-site dynamics and $c=R^{-2}/8$ for block dynamics. For the Ising weight, if $B_J=\sum_{i<j}|J_{ij}|$, then $R\leq e^{2B_J}$; the respective rates are at least $e^{-4B_J}/(4n)$ and $e^{-4B_J}/8$. These are explicit asymptotic exponents, not explicit mixing-time bounds: the prefactor $C(w)$ remains nonquantitative.

\section{Extension to finite alphabets and positive interaction weights}
\begin{p1theorem}\label{p1:thm:alphabet}
Let $\Omega=\prod_{i=1}^n X_i$ with each $X_i$ finite and nonempty, and let $w:\Omega\to(0,\infty)$ be arbitrary. Define the same two heat-bath exchange kernels using $w$ instead of the Ising weight. For each array $\boldsymbol\pi=(\pi_i)_i$ of one-site marginals, let $\mu_{\boldsymbol\pi}$ uniquely maximize $H(q)+q[\log w]$ subject to those marginals. There are $C(w)<\infty$ and $c(w)>0$ such that
\[
 \|T^t(p)-\mu_{(p_i)_i}\|_{\pOneTV}\leq C(w)e^{-c(w)t}
\]
uniformly over every initial law and every integer $t\geq0$.
\end{p1theorem}
\begin{proof}
We detail the changes needed to include faces on which several alleles are absent. The fixed tangent space is now
\[
 E=\{v:\sum_xv(x)=0,\ \sum_{x:x_i=\alpha}v(x)=0\text{ for all }i,\alpha\in X_i\}.
\]
The entropy maximizer is positive on the product $S=\prod_i S_i$ of the marginal supports and has density proportional to $w(x)\prod_i\lambda_i(x_i)$ there, with all factors positive. The Gibbs section is continuous: to change marginal $\pi_i$ to $\pi_i'$, use a Markov kernel obtained from a maximal coupling of those two laws. It changes the joint law by at most $\|\pi_i-\pi_i'\|_{\pOneTV}$ and preserves the other marginals. The preceding compactness argument then applies. The comparison with $Q^0$, the linear map $L_\pi$, and the positive-box entropy proof are unchanged.

On $S\times S$, the block kernel is conditional expectation on the classes determined by the unordered pair at each coordinate. All subsets realizing a given output pair have the same multiplicity, namely $2^r$, where $r$ is the number of agreeing input coordinates. Thus this is indeed the Gibbs heat bath on each orbit. The one-site kernels are again conditional expectations on exchange orbits. The compression identity and the spectral interval $[0,1]$ remain valid. The equality condition says, for every $\alpha,\beta\in S_i$, that $h(\alpha,u)-h(\beta,u)$ is independent of $u$. It follows that $h$ is a sum of one-site functions; the full marginal constraints annihilate all such neutral modes.

At a boundary equilibrium, classify every configuration by its mutant coordinates $B=\{i:x_i\notin S_i\}$ together with the corresponding absent-allele labels $(x_i)_{i\in B}$. A mutant-resident collision partitions this labeled set. Ordering by its cardinality makes the derivative block triangular. Every block with at least two mutant coordinates is strictly substochastic, since a one-site exchange splits its labeled set. A block with just one absent allele is strictly positive and stochastic: swapping the mutant coordinate transfers that allele to a resident with any prescribed allowed configuration on the remaining coordinates. Its unique eigenvalue-$1$ direction has nonzero mass in that absent allele. In an eigenvector belonging to $E$, the separate marginal constraint for this absent allele kills that direction after the larger mutant blocks have vanished. The resident block has only the already described additive neutral modes, also killed by $E$. Therefore $\rho(DT(\mu)|_E)<1$ on every face. The compact common-iterate argument proves the result.
\end{proof}

\section{A nonlinear entropy reweighting bound in continuous time}
This section concerns the different evolution $\dot p=T(p)-p$. We keep its distinction from discrete iteration explicit. Let $\mu$ be the Gibbs law with the same one-site marginals as $p$, and define its entropy production by
\[
 \mathcal I_J(p)=-\sum_x(T(p)(x)-p(x))\log\frac{p(x)}{\mu(x)}.
\]
For positive $p$ and $\mu$, put $f=p/\mu$. Reversibility gives the standard symmetrized formula
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I_J(p)=\frac14\sum_{x,y,u,v}&\mu(x)\mu(y)Q_J(x,y;u,v)\\
 &\quad\cdot[f(x)f(y)-f(u)f(v)]\log\frac{f(x)f(y)}{f(u)f(v)}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Let $\kappa_0$ be any constant for which the zero-interaction kernel satisfies
\[
 \mathcal I_0(q)\geq\kappa_0 D(q\Vert\textstyle\bigotimes_iq_i).
\]
For $n\geq2$, the actual single-site heat bath used here admits $\kappa_0=1/[2(n-1)]$; the factor $1/2$ is its holding normalization. For the uniform block kernel, one may take $\kappa_0=(1-2^{1-n})/(n-1)$. These established recombination bounds hold for arbitrary finite coordinate alphabets \cite{p1:CS18}. The conservative alternatives $1/(2n)$ and $1/n$, respectively, remain valid when some coordinates are pinned; when at most one coordinate remains nontrivial, the desired entropy is identically zero.

\begin{p1theorem}\label{p1:thm:entropy}
For $R=\max w/\min w$ and positive laws on the allowed product support,
\[
 \mathcal I_J(p)\geq
 \begin{cases}
 \kappa_0 R^{-2}D(p\Vert\mu),&\text{single-site heat bath},\\
 \kappa_0 R^{-4}D(p\Vert\mu),&\text{uniform block heat bath}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Thus the continuous-time evolution has the corresponding global exponential relative-entropy decay, with prefactor one, uniformly over the equilibrium fields. Boundary initial laws follow by approximation on their allowed product support.
\end{p1theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $m=\min w$, $M=\max w$, and define the probability $q$ by
\[
 q(x)=\frac{p(x)/w(x)}{\sum_z p(z)/w(z)},\qquad
 Z=\sum_xq(x)w(x),\qquad p=wq/Z.
\]
Let $\pi=\bigotimes_iq_i$ and $\mu'=w\pi/\pi[w]$. Both $\mu$ and $\mu'$ belong to the same exponential family, so $\log(\mu/\mu')$ is additive. Since $p$ and $\mu$ have the same one-site marginals,
\[
 D(p\Vert\mu')=D(p\Vert\mu)+D(\mu\Vert\mu')\geq D(p\Vert\mu).
\]
Apply to $q$ and $\pi$ the same accept/reject channel, accepting an input $x$ with probability $w(x)/M$ and recording the accepted value. The accepted laws are $p$ and $\mu'$, and the acceptance probability under $q$ is $Z/M$. Data processing and the chain rule for relative entropy give
\begin{equation}\label{p1:eq:tilt-entropy}
 D(p\Vert\mu)\leq D(p\Vert\mu')\leq(M/Z)D(q\Vert\pi).
\end{equation}
All discarded terms in the chain rule are nonnegative.

Write $\mu=w\gamma/\gamma[w]$ with product $\gamma$. On every admissible swap, $\gamma(x)\gamma(y)=\gamma(u)\gamma(v)$. Substitution in the production formula therefore gives
\[
 \mathcal I_J(p)=\frac1{4Z^2}\sum_{x,y,u,v}w(x)w(y)Q_J(x,y;u,v)
 [q(x)q(y)-q(u)q(v)]\log\frac{q(x)q(y)}{q(u)q(v)}.
\]
Every bracket-log product is nonnegative.

For a single-site exchange set $a=w(x)w(y)$ and $b=w(u)w(v)$. Its heat-bath conductance is $ab/(a+b)\geq m^2/2$, while the zero-interaction exchange probability is $1/2$. The site-selection factors agree. Thus
\[
 \mathcal I_J(p)\geq(m^2/Z^2)\mathcal I_0(q)
 \geq\kappa_0\frac{m^2}{MZ}D(p\Vert\mu)
 \geq\kappa_0R^{-2}D(p\Vert\mu).
\]
For the full block heat bath, write $\mathcal O$ for the pair orbit and $a_z=w(z_1)w(z_2)$. The ratio of the weighted edge coefficient to its zero-interaction counterpart is
\[
 \frac{a_{(x,y)}a_{(u,v)}}{|\mathcal O|^{-1}\sum_{z\in\mathcal O}a_z}
 \geq\frac{m^4}{M^2}.
\]
Consequently
\[
 \mathcal I_J(p)\geq\frac{m^4}{M^2Z^2}\mathcal I_0(q)
 \geq\kappa_0\frac{m^4}{M^3Z}D(p\Vert\mu)
 \geq\kappa_0R^{-4}D(p\Vert\mu).
\]
The entropy differential identity and Gronwall's inequality prove continuous-time decay. For initial laws with zeros, restrict to the product of their marginal supports and approximate the law by its mixture with the Gibbs equilibrium. The finite-dimensional flow is continuous in the initial condition, and relative entropy to the fixed positive equilibrium is continuous, so the result passes to the limit.
\end{proof}

For completeness, uniformly over initial laws,
\[
 D(p\Vert\mu)=\mathcal F(\mu)-\mathcal F(p)\leq n\log2+\log R
\]
in the binary case (replace $n\log2$ by $\sum_i\log|X_i|$ for finite alphabets). Pinsker therefore supplies an explicit continuous-time total-variation prefactor as well. None of this identifies the sharp nonlinear constants, and the discrete-time entropy loss is not identified with its continuous-time derivative.

\section{Limitations}
\begin{itemize}
\item The discrete-time rate has the explicit lower bounds proved above, while its prefactor remains nonquantitative because of the uniform entrance-time argument. The continuous-time entropy estimate has prefactor one and a fully explicit rate.
\item The total-variation estimate in discrete time and the entropy estimate in continuous time are distinct. Neither computes a sharp fixed-reference recombination constant.
\item The qualitative convergence mechanism is established prior art and is rederived here only for completeness. The 2026 covariance manuscript is discussed for context and is not an input to this proof.
\end{itemize}
\clearpage
\endgroup
% END RENDERED SOURCE 01


% BEGIN RENDERED SOURCE 02
\begingroup
\chapter{Uniform Exponential Convergence for Nonlinear Independent Set Exchange}\label{chapter:2}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\thechapter.\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\theHchapter.\arabic{section}}
\lstset{style=collectedbase}

\begin{chapterabstract}
For a fixed finite graph of maximum degree $\Delta$ and every $\beta<1/(\Delta+1)$, we prove uniform exponential convergence of the discrete-time nonlinear single-site and mean-field independent-set exchange maps. The initial vertex marginals, respectively mean density, may range throughout $[0,\beta]$, including zero values. The asymptotic exponents are explicit; the common prefactors are not. An empty-set deletion estimate controls the linearization uniformly as fugacities vanish. Spectral continuity and fixed-coordinate adapted norms then give a uniform nonlinear contraction neighborhood and entrance time. The result concerns exponential ergodicity, not the stronger nearly-linear-rate conjecture.
\end{chapterabstract}

\section{Background, definitions, and statement}
The source \cite{p2:HC}, revised 20 October 2026, proves qualitative convergence in Proposition 1.1 and asks in Section 6(1) whether it is exponential throughout the same region, allowing a very small dimension-dependent rate. We consider precisely its discrete-time single-site and mean-field collision rules, not heat-bath variants. The stronger rate and algorithmic questions in Section 6(2) are not addressed here.

Classical mass-action theory already gives local exponential stability at positive equilibria \cite[Theorems 5.4--5.6]{p2:SJ}. Here $T(p)-p$ is a reversible quadratic mass-action field on the probability simplex. Together with conserved-mode removal, aperiodicity, and qualitative convergence, this supplies a fixed-positive-profile route. The estimates below instead make the rate explicit and uniform as conserved quantities vanish; local stability itself is not new.

Let $G=(V,\mathcal E)$ be a finite simple graph, $n=|V|\geq1$, and let $\Omega$ be its independent sets. Write $N[v]$ for the closed neighborhood of $v$ and $\Delta$ for the maximum degree. For an ordered pair $(I,J)\in\Omega^2$, select vertices $v,u$ and exchange the occupancy bit at $v$ in $I$ with that at $u$ in $J$, accepting the exchange exactly when both resulting sets are independent. Otherwise keep $(I,J)$ unchanged. The single-site rule selects $v=u$ uniformly in $V$; the mean-field rule selects $v,u$ independently uniformly in $V$. Denote the pair transition matrix by $Q$, its first-output marginal by $Q_1$, and define
\[
 T(p)(K)=\sum_{I,J\in\Omega}p(I)p(J)Q_1(K\mid I,J).
\]
Under the input law $p\otimes p$, both output marginals agree. The single-site map conserves all $m_v(p)=p(v\in I)$; the mean-field map conserves $\alpha(p)=\pTwoE_p|I|/n$.

For a vector $\lambda=(\lambda_v)_{v\in V}\geq0$, put
\[
 \mu_\lambda(I)=\frac{\prod_{v\in I}\lambda_v}{Z(\lambda)},\qquad
 Z(\lambda)=\sum_{I\in\Omega}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda_v.
\]
A zero fugacity excludes that vertex. The single-site Gibbs equilibrium $\mu_{m(p)}$ is the unique entropy maximizer with marginals $m(p)$. The mean-field equilibrium $\mu_{\alpha(p)}$ has a common fugacity and uniquely maximizes entropy at that density.

\begin{p2theorem}\label{p2:thm:main}
Fix $0<\beta<1/(\Delta+1)$ and write
\[
 d=1-(\Delta+1)\beta,\qquad L=\beta/d,\qquad Z_*=(1+L)^n.
\]
There exist finite constants $C_{\rm ss}(G,\beta),C_{\rm mf}(G,\beta)$ such that, for every integer $t\geq0$,
\begin{align*}
 \|T_{\rm ss}^t(p)-\mu_{m(p)}\|_{\pTwoTV}
 &\leq C_{\rm ss}(G,\beta)\exp\{-t/(16n^2Z_*^2)\},
 && \max_v m_v(p)\leq\beta,\\
 \|T_{\rm mf}^t(p)-\mu_{\alpha(p)}\|_{\pTwoTV}
 &\leq C_{\rm mf}(G,\beta)\exp\{-t/(48n^3Z_*^2)\},
 && \alpha(p)\leq\beta.
\end{align*}
The constants are uniform as positive conserved quantities approach zero, and include exactly zero values. The prefactors are not asserted to be explicit.
\end{p2theorem}
Every initial law in the strict region of \cite[Proposition 1.1]{p2:HC} belongs to one of these closed subregions by choosing a larger $\beta$ still below the threshold. No uniformity as $\beta\uparrow1/(\Delta+1)$ is asserted.

\section{Stationarity, entropy, and compactness}
Each specified vertex exchange, with rejection, is an involution on ordered pair states. Thus $Q$ is symmetric and stochastic. It is reversible for $\mu_\lambda\otimes\mu_\lambda$: for the single-site kernel all vertex counts are preserved, while for the mean-field kernel the common-fugacity weight is preserved.

\begin{p2lemma}\label{p2:lem:diag}
If $G$ has an edge, every diagonal entry of either pair kernel is at least $\theta=1/n^2$. Hence $Q\succeq(-1+2\theta)I$ in every reversible weighted inner product.
\end{p2lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $I$ and $J$ agree at some vertex $v$, selecting $v=u$ leaves the pair unchanged. If they disagree at every vertex, then they partition $V$. For any edge, its endpoints must lie in different sets. Transferring either endpoint to the other set is rejected. In either case a choice of probability at least $1/n^2$ holds the pair. Write $Q=\theta I+(1-\theta)\widetilde Q$, where $\widetilde Q$ is a reversible Markov matrix with spectrum in $[-1,1]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{p2lemma}\label{p2:lem:qual}
On each parameter region in Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:main}, the Gibbs equilibrium exists, is unique, and $T^t(p)$ converges to it. The equilibrium section is continuous on the entire closed region, including zero marginals.
\end{p2lemma}
\begin{proof}
First assume $G$ has an edge. Shannon entropy satisfies
\[
 2H(Tp)\geq H((p\otimes p)Q)\geq H(p\otimes p)=2H(p).
\]
The first inequality is subadditivity; the second follows because $Q$ is doubly stochastic. Equality $H(Tp)=H(p)$ forces equality in the second inequality. Strict concavity of entropy, together with the positive diagonal from Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:diag}, implies that the density $p(I)p(J)$ is constant across every positive transition of $Q$. This assertion includes zero entries. In particular $(p\otimes p)Q=p\otimes p$, so $Tp=p$, and the support of $p$ is closed under admissible exchanges.

We classify such equality laws in the specified region. Choose a supported set $I$ of minimum cardinality. For the single-site rule, if $v\in I$, then
\[
 p(J\cap N[v]=\varnothing)\geq1-\sum_{u\in N[v]}m_u(p)\geq d>0.
\]
A supported $J$ avoiding $N[v]$ permits the exchange $(I,J)\mapsto(I\setminus\{v\},J\cup\{v\})$. Support closure contradicts minimality. Hence $p(\varnothing)>0$. In the mean-field case,
\[
 \frac1n\sum_u p(J\cap N[u]=\varnothing)
 \geq1-\frac1n\sum_u\sum_{z\in N[u]}m_z(p)
 \geq1-(\Delta+1)\alpha(p)\geq d.
\]
Thus some $u$ and supported $J$ have $J\cap N[u]=\varnothing$; transferring any $v\in I$ to this $u$ gives the same contradiction. Again $p(\varnothing)>0$.

The admissible same-site exchange with an empty set yields
\[
 p(I)p(\varnothing)=p(I\setminus\{v\})p(\{v\})\qquad(v\in I).
\]
Induction implies $p(I)=p(\varnothing)\prod_{v\in I}\lambda_v$, where $\lambda_v=p(\{v\})/p(\varnothing)\geq0$. For the mean-field kernel, exchanging a singleton with the empty set at any target vertex gives $p(\{v\})=p(\{u\})$; its fugacity is common. Such a Gibbs law uniquely maximizes entropy on its conserved fiber: on its allowed support, $\log\mu$ is an affine combination of the conserved observables, so for any feasible $q$,
\[
 D(q\Vert\mu)=H(\mu)-H(q).
\]
For the single-site law, vertices with $\lambda_v=0$ have zero conserved marginal and are excluded from every law on the same fiber. The case of zero mean density is simply the point mass at the empty set.

Entropy is continuous and nondecreasing along every trajectory in the compact simplex. Any subsequential limit $q$ satisfies $H(Tq)=H(q)$, because successive entropies have the same limit. The preceding classification and entropy uniqueness show that every subsequential limit is the same Gibbs law. This proves existence and convergence without needing a separate feasible-profile construction.

For a Gibbs equilibrium in the single-site region,
\[
 m_v=\lambda_v\mu(I\cap N[v]=\varnothing),\qquad0\leq\lambda_v\leq\beta/d=L.
\]
For a common fugacity, summing this identity gives
\[
 \alpha=\frac\lambda n\sum_v\mu(I\cap N[v]=\varnothing)\geq\lambda[1-(\Delta+1)\alpha],
\]
so again $\lambda\leq L$. All equilibria therefore have fugacities in a compact cube $[0,L]^n$. If conserved profiles converge, take a convergent subsequence of their fugacities. The Gibbs formula is continuous on that cube because $Z\geq1$. Its limit has the desired conserved profile and hence is its unique entropy maximizer. This proves continuity of the equilibrium section.

If $G$ is edgeless, the maps on each conserved fiber are the linear refresh maps
\[
 T_{\rm ss}p=\frac1n\sum_v p_v\otimes p_{-v},\qquad
 T_{\rm mf}p=\frac1n\sum_v\operatorname{Bern}(\alpha)\otimes p_{-v}.
\]
Their convergence to the corresponding product equilibrium follows, for example, by conditioning on the event that all coordinates have been refreshed. The probability of its complement is at most $n(1-1/n)^t$ (with the one-coordinate case interpreted directly). The equilibrium sections are then explicitly continuous.
\end{proof}

\section{The derivative and an empty-set comparison}
Let $E_{\rm ss}$ be the fixed vector space of signed measures annihilating mass and every vertex occupancy, and $E_{\rm mf}$ the space annihilating mass and $|I|$. These spaces are independent of the conserved profile and invariant under the corresponding derivative $A_\mu=DT(\mu)$. For a full-support equilibrium write $v=\mu h$ and put $Mh(I,J)=h(I)+h(J)$. Reversibility and replica symmetry give
\[
 \langle h,A_\mu(\mu k)/\mu\rangle_\mu
 =\tfrac12\langle Mh,Q Mk\rangle_{\mu\otimes\mu}.
\]
If $\mu[h]=0$, then $\|Mh\|_{\mu^2}^2=2\|h\|_\mu^2$. Thus the derivative is self-adjoint on centered densities, and its deficit form is
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal D_\mu(h)
 &=\|h\|_\mu^2-\langle h,A_\mu(\mu h)/\mu\rangle_\mu\\
 &=\frac14\sum_{I,J,U,W}\mu(I)\mu(J)Q(I,J;U,W)
 [h(I)+h(J)-h(U)-h(W)]^2.
\end{align*}
The general compression viewpoint is classical in reversible quadratic dynamics; see \cite[Section 2.3]{p2:CS18}.

Suppose now $0<\lambda_v\leq L$. Two elementary estimates are
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:mass}
 \mu(\varnothing)\geq Z_*^{-1},\qquad
 \sum_{I\supseteq J}\mu(I)\leq Z_*\mu(J).
\end{equation}
Indeed $Z(\lambda)\leq\prod_v(1+\lambda_v)\leq Z_*$, and after factoring the weight of $J$, the weights of its admissible extensions are at most the sum over all subsets of $V\setminus J$.

Fix an ordering of vertices. For $v\in J$, set
\[
 e(J,v)=h(J)+h(\varnothing)-h(J\setminus\{v\})-h(\{v\}).
\]
Remove the vertices of $I$ in the fixed order. With $a_v=h(\{v\})-h(\varnothing)$, the residual
\[
 R(I)=h(I)-h(\varnothing)-\sum_{v\in I}a_v
\]
is the sum of the corresponding removal defects. Cauchy--Schwarz and \eqref{p2:eq:mass} show
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:removal}
 \sum_I\mu(I)R(I)^2
 \leq nZ_*\sum_{J}\sum_{v\in J}\mu(J)e(J,v)^2.
\end{equation}
To justify the summation, a fixed stage $(J,v)$ can arise only from initial sets $I\supseteq J$; its total initial weight is therefore at most $Z_*\mu(J)$. Overcounting all stages only enlarges the bound.

\begin{p2proposition}\label{p2:prop:gap}
On the fixed conserved tangent spaces, every equilibrium with fugacities bounded by $L$, including zero fugacities, satisfies
\[
 \rho(A_\mu|_{E_{\rm ss}})\leq1-\varepsilon_{\rm ss},\quad
 \varepsilon_{\rm ss}=\frac1{4n^2Z_*^2},\qquad
 \rho(A_\mu|_{E_{\rm mf}})\leq1-\varepsilon_{\rm mf},\quad
 \varepsilon_{\rm mf}=\frac1{12n^3Z_*^2}.
\]
In the mean-field statement the fugacity is common. A zero-dimensional tangent space is understood to have spectral radius zero.
\end{p2proposition}
\begin{proof}
First take positive fugacities. For the single-site rule, $h$ corresponding to a vector in $E_{\rm ss}$ is orthogonal to all additive occupancy functions. Consequently $\|h\|_\mu^2\leq\|R\|_\mu^2$. The transition
\[
 (J,\varnothing)\longmapsto(J\setminus\{v\},\{v\})
\]
has probability at least $1/n$. Keeping just these distinct directed edges in the nonnegative deficit sum gives
\[
 \mathcal D_\mu(h)\geq\frac{\mu(\varnothing)}{4n}
 \sum_J\sum_{v\in J}\mu(J)e(J,v)^2.
\]
Using \eqref{p2:eq:removal} and \eqref{p2:eq:mass} yields
$\|h\|_\mu^2\leq4n^2Z_*^2\mathcal D_\mu(h)$.

For the mean-field rule, $h$ is orthogonal to $1$ and $|I|$. Set $b=n^{-1}\sum_v a_v$ and $d_v=a_v-b$. Then
\[
 h(I)-h(\varnothing)-b|I|=R(I)+B(I),\qquad B(I)=\sum_{v\in I}d_v.
\]
The same deletion transitions now have probability at least $1/n^2$, so
\[
 \|R\|_\mu^2\leq\frac{4n^3Z_*}{\mu(\varnothing)}\mathcal D_\mu(h).
\]
Since $m_v\leq\lambda$ for common fugacity $\lambda$,
\[
 \|B\|_\mu^2\leq n\sum_v m_vd_v^2\leq n\lambda\sum_vd_v^2.
\]
The singleton-transfer edges $(\{v\},\varnothing)\mapsto(\varnothing,\{u\})$, each with probability at least $1/n^2$, give
\[
 \mathcal D_\mu(h)\geq\frac{\lambda\mu(\varnothing)^2}{4n^2}
 \sum_{v,u}(d_v-d_u)^2
 =\frac{\lambda\mu(\varnothing)^2}{2n}\sum_vd_v^2.
\]
Hence $\|B\|_\mu^2\leq2n^2\mu(\varnothing)^{-2}\mathcal D_\mu(h)$. Orthogonality and $(R+B)^2\leq2R^2+2B^2$ imply
\[
 \|h\|_\mu^2\leq(8n^3+4n^2)Z_*^2\mathcal D_\mu(h)
 \leq12n^3Z_*^2\mathcal D_\mu(h).
\]
The deletion and singleton lower bounds are used separately; no disjointness between these two collections of edges is required.

These bounds put the largest restricted derivative eigenvalue below $1-\varepsilon$. If $G$ has an edge, Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:diag} and compression put its smallest centered eigenvalue above $-1+2/n^2$. Both stated $\varepsilon$ are at most $2/n^2$, so the same estimate controls the absolute spectral radius. If $G$ is edgeless, the explicit refresh operators have nonnegative restricted spectrum with gap at least $1/n$, which is more than sufficient.

Finally, $A_\mu$ depends continuously on $\mu$ in the fixed coordinates of signed measures, and the tangent spaces $E$ do not change with $\mu$. Replace any zero fugacity by a positive number tending to zero, still bounded by $L$. The positive-fugacity estimates proved above require only that upper bound, not any marginal restriction. Continuity of the Gibbs formula and of finite-dimensional spectral radius proves the same estimate at every boundary equilibrium.
\end{proof}

\section{A uniform nonlinear neighborhood and global entrance}
We give the upgrade explicitly to avoid relying on density norms that degenerate at zero fugacity. Let $K$ be either of the compact convex sets of laws in Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:main}; it is invariant under the map. Write $r(p)$ for its continuous equilibrium section and $E$ for the appropriate fixed tangent space. Set $\varepsilon$ to the corresponding constant of Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:gap} and $a=1-\varepsilon/2$.

The matrices $A_\mu|_E$, $\mu\in r(K)$, form a compact family with spectral radii at most $1-\varepsilon$. A circle of radius strictly between $1-\varepsilon$ and $a$ has uniformly bounded resolvents over this family. The matrix Cauchy integral formula therefore bounds their powers uniformly at that radius. Consequently
\[
 N_\mu(v)=\sum_{k=0}^\infty a^{-k}\|A_\mu^kv\|_1
\]
is a norm on $E$ and there is a finite $B$, independent of $\mu$, such that
\[
 \|v\|_1\leq N_\mu(v)\leq B\|v\|_1,\qquad
 N_\mu(A_\mu v)\leq aN_\mu(v).
\]
No positive lower bound on the cells of $\mu$ is used.

Quadraticity and stochasticity give
\[
 T(\mu+v)-\mu=A_\mu v+T(v),\qquad \|T(v)\|_1\leq\|v\|_1^2.
\]
For a same-fiber displacement $v$, the remainder lies in $E$. Therefore, whenever $N_\mu(v)\leq\eta=\varepsilon/(4B)$,
\[
 N_\mu(T(\mu+v)-\mu)
 \leq(1-\varepsilon/4)N_\mu(v).
\]
By Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:qual}, every trajectory eventually has ordinary distance to its equilibrium less than $\eta/(2B)$. At its chosen entrance time, continuity of $T^t$ and $r$ gives an open neighborhood of initial laws with distance less than $\eta/B$. Every law in that neighborhood thus enters the invariant adapted ball at that same time. A finite subcover of the compact $K$ gives a common finite upper bound on these entrance times. Subsequent adapted contractions and $\|v\|_1\leq N_\mu(v)$ imply
\[
 \|T^t(p)-r(p)\|_{\pTwoTV}\leq C\exp(-\varepsilon t/4)
\]
with finite $C$ independent of $p\in K$. This proves Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:main}.

\section{Why the strict threshold matters}
Uniformity up to the endpoint cannot in general be added to the single-site result. Let $G$ be two disjoint edges $\{1,2\}$ and $\{3,4\}$, so $\Delta=1$. Every law supported on the four sets choosing exactly one vertex from each edge is frozen: transferring a vertex absent from the other replica would conflict with the occupied opposite endpoint. The law
\[
 p_0=\tfrac12\delta_{\{1,3\}}+\tfrac12\delta_{\{2,4\}}
\]
has all marginals $1/2$, but differs from the maximum-entropy law, which is uniform on the four sets. Set $p_\eta=(1-\eta)p_0+\eta\delta_\varnothing$. Its marginals are strictly below $1/2$. Its entropy-maximizing equilibrium is the product over the two edges of a law assigning probabilities $\eta,(1-\eta)/2,(1-\eta)/2$ to the empty and two singleton states. As $\eta\downarrow0$, these equilibria converge to the uniform law on the four maximal sets, whereas $T^t(p_\eta)\to p_0$ for every fixed $t$. Therefore no single finite prefactor and positive exponent can hold uniformly over the entire open region with no distance from its threshold.

\section{Scope and qualifications}
The argument proves an explicit asymptotic exponent with a nonexplicit common prefactor, not an explicit mixing-time estimate and not a rate of order $1/n$ near the full threshold. Its entropy monotonicity and support-closure mechanisms are prior ingredients of the qualitative theory. Its compression and local spectral-stability framework also have classical precedents. The proof's quantitative step is the empty-set deletion comparison uniform as fugacities vanish; no historical novelty claim for that step is made here.
\clearpage
\endgroup
% END RENDERED SOURCE 02


% BEGIN RENDERED SOURCE 03
\begingroup
\chapter{Fast Entropy Contraction for Nonlinear Independent Set Exchange}\label{chapter:3}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\thechapter.\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\theHchapter.\arabic{section}}
\lstset{style=collectedbase}

\begin{chapterabstract}
We prove global relative entropy contraction for nonlinear independent-set exchange. For single-site exchange, maximum marginal $\beta\leq1/(2\Delta+1)$ gives entropy decay at rate $(1-2\Delta\beta)/n$ and an explicit total-variation bound. A heterogeneous local condition is also sufficient. The mean-field map admits the same discrete-time bound with its conserved density replacing $\beta$. Each nonlinear update is represented by a reversible linear kernel with a varying Gibbs reference. A contractive coupling and the curvature theorem of Caputo--Salez give a discrete entropy inequality, which transfers to the fixed equilibrium because the relevant moments are conserved. The result enlarges known fast-convergence regions but does not resolve the full single-site conjecture.
\end{chapterabstract}

\section{Statement and relation to the original question}
Let $G=(V,\mathcal E)$ be a finite simple graph, $n=|V|\geq1$, and $\Omega_G$ its independent sets. The nonlinear single-site map $T$ samples $I,J$ independently from $p$, chooses $v\in V$ uniformly, and exchanges the two occupancy bits at $v$ if both resulting sets are independent; otherwise the pair is unchanged. The law of its first output is $T(p)$. This is exactly the single-site rule of \cite{p3:HC}, not a heat-bath modification. It preserves every marginal $m_v=p(v\in I)$.

Coordinates with marginal zero are absent, and coordinates with marginal one are deterministically occupied. Remove these deterministic coordinates and restrict to the resulting allowed independent-set support. Write $F=\{v:0<m_v<1\}$ for the remaining free coordinates; no vertex in $F$ neighbors a deterministically occupied vertex. The original normalization $1/n$ is retained, so updates at removed coordinates are holding steps. Put
\[
 s_v=\sum_{u\in N(v)}m_u\qquad(v\in F).
\]
On the fixed-marginal fiber, let $\mu_m$ be the maximum-entropy law. Under the assumptions below it has full support on the allowed support and the hard-core Gibbs form there.

\begin{p3theorem}\label{p3:thm:local}
Suppose $F\neq\varnothing$ and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:local}
 m_v+2s_v\leq1\qquad(v\in F).
\end{equation}
Set $\kappa=n^{-1}\min_{v\in F}(1-2s_v)>0$. Let $h(r)=-r\log r-(1-r)\log(1-r)$ denote binary entropy, with $0\log0=0$. For every law $p$ with these marginals,
\[
 D(Tp\Vert\mu_m)\leq(1-\kappa)D(p\Vert\mu_m).
\]
Consequently, for every integer $t\geq0$,
\[
 D(T^tp\Vert\mu_m)\leq(1-\kappa)^tD(p\Vert\mu_m),\qquad
 \|T^tp-\mu_m\|_{\pThreeTV}\leq\sqrt{\frac12\sum_{v\in V}h(m_v)}\,e^{-\kappa t/2}.
\]
If $F=\varnothing$, the fiber consists of one law and all errors are zero.
\end{p3theorem}

\begin{p3theorem}\label{p3:thm:uniform}
Let $\Delta\geq1$ be the maximum degree and $0\leq\beta\leq1/(2\Delta+1)$. Uniformly over all initial laws with $\max_vm_v\leq\beta$, Theorem~\ref{p3:thm:local} holds with
\[
 \kappa=\frac{1-2\Delta\beta}{n}.
\]
The endpoint $\beta=1/(2\Delta+1)$ is included, as are zero marginals. The total-variation prefactor is at most $\sqrt{nh(\beta)/2}$. For edgeless graphs, the same conclusion holds with $\kappa=1/n$ after removing deterministic coordinates.
\end{p3theorem}
Theorem~\ref{p3:thm:uniform} extends the allowed marginal range in \cite[Theorem 1.2]{p3:HC}, which gives an explicit total-variation bound when $m_v\leq(1-\delta)/[3(\Delta+1)]$. We do not claim our total-variation exponent is larger at every common parameter value. The conjecture in \cite[Section 6(2)]{p3:HC} asks for a nearly-linear rate throughout the larger region $m_v<1/(\Delta+1)$; that remaining interval is not settled here. The curvature theorem used below is an established general result, not a new principle claimed in this note.

\section{The frozen reversible kernel}
For a law $p$ on the allowed support, define
\[
 a_v(p)=p(I\cap N[v]=\varnothing),\qquad b_v(p)=m_v+a_v(p)
 =p(I\cap N(v)=\varnothing),\quad v\in F.
\]
Thus $b_v\leq1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:vacancy}
 a_v(p)\geq1-m_v-s_v\geq s_v.
\end{equation}
Moreover $a_v(p)>0$: if $s_v>0$ this follows from \eqref{p3:eq:vacancy}; if $s_v=0$, then $a_v(p)=1-m_v>0$.

\begin{p3lemma}\label{p3:lem:frozen}
There is a Markov kernel $K_p$ such that $Tp=pK_p$. It selects a coordinate with probability $1/n$, inserts a vacant vertex $v$ with probability $m_v$ if all its neighbors are vacant, and removes an occupied vertex $v$ with probability $a_v(p)$. Its invariant Gibbs law $\nu_p$ has fugacities
\[
 \tau_v=\frac{m_v}{a_v(p)}\quad(v\in F).
\]
On the allowed support $K_p$ is reversible and irreducible.
\end{p3lemma}
\begin{proof}
For an insertion in the first parent, the donor must be occupied at $v$, an event of probability $m_v$; removal from the donor is always admissible. For a deletion from the first parent, the donor must have its entire closed neighborhood of $v$ vacant, an event of probability $a_v(p)$. These are precisely the stated transition probabilities. Detailed balance on each insertion/deletion edge holds because the Gibbs weight ratio is $\tau_v=m_v/a_v(p)$. All free-site insertion and deletion rates are positive, and the empty free configuration connects the state space.
\end{proof}

For completeness, the equilibrium $\mu_m$ exists and is positive on the allowed support under \eqref{p3:eq:local}. Entropy has a unique maximizer on the nonempty compact marginal fiber. Its support contains the support of every other feasible law: otherwise mixing with that law would give an infinite positive entropy derivative at a zero cell. Since applying $T$ preserves the fiber, this support is closed under admissible pair exchanges. A supported independent set of minimum size must be empty: if it contains $v$, a supported donor avoiding $N[v]$ exists by $1-m_v-s_v>0$, and the exchange deletes $v$. Positive free marginals then supply every singleton, and support closure builds every independent set. Lagrange multipliers now give the positive Gibbs form. This argument is on the free induced graph after removing deterministic coordinates.

\section{A nonexpanding contractive coupling}
Use Hamming distance $d(I,J)=|I\mathbin\triangle J|$ on independent sets of the free induced graph. This is a geodesic metric: delete $I\setminus J$ and then insert $J\setminus I$ to obtain a path of length $d(I,J)$.

\begin{p3lemma}\label{p3:lem:coupling}
For each fixed $p$ satisfying \eqref{p3:eq:local} and all allowed $I,J$, the rows $K_p(I,\cdot)$ and $K_p(J,\cdot)$ admit a coupling $(X,Y)$ with
\[
 d(X,Y)\leq d(I,J)\quad\text{almost surely},\qquad
 \mathbb E d(X,Y)\leq(1-\kappa)d(I,J).
\]
\end{p3lemma}
\begin{proof}
First let $J=I\cup\{v\}$ be adjacent. Couple the chosen update coordinates synchronously. At $v$, use the coupling with masses $a_v/n$ on $(I,I)$, $m_v/n$ on $(J,J)$, and $(1-a_v-m_v)/n$ on $(I,J)$. This is valid because $a_v+m_v\leq1$. Away from $v$, couple identical births or deaths whenever possible. The only pairs at distance two are
\[
 (I\cup\{u\},J),\qquad
 u\in N(v),\quad I\cap N[u]=\varnothing,
\]
each of mass $m_u/n$. Write $B$ for their total mass, so $B\leq s_v/n\leq a_v/n$.

For each such $u$, remove mass $m_u/n$ both from its distance-two pair and from the good pair $(I,I)$, replacing it with the same mass on each of
\[
 (I\cup\{u\},I),\qquad (I,J).
\]
Both coupling marginals are unchanged, sufficient good mass is available, and all resulting distances are at most one. The expected distance is unchanged: the replaced distances were two and zero, while the new distances are one and one. Hence it is
\[
 1-\frac{a_v+m_v}{n}+B
 \leq1-\frac{a_v+m_v-s_v}{n}
 \leq1-\frac{1-2s_v}{n}
 \leq1-\kappa.
\]
The penultimate step uses the union bound $a_v+m_v\geq1-s_v$. Glue these couplings along a Hamming geodesic between arbitrary $I,J$. The triangle inequality gives both the almost-sure and expected bounds.
\end{proof}

We invoke the following precise finite-state case of \cite[Theorem 2, $M=1$ and $\ell=1$]{p3:CS}. If $K$ is reversible for a positive probability $\nu$, is nonexpanding in $W_\infty$, and contracts $W_1$ by a factor $1-\kappa$, then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:cs}
 D(qK\Vert\nu)\leq(1-\kappa)D(q\Vert\nu)
 \qquad\text{for every probability }q.
\end{equation}
Its regularity hypothesis is automatic on the finite metric space. This is a full discrete-step entropy inequality; no conversion from a continuous-time modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality is used. Lemma~\ref{p3:lem:coupling} verifies its hypotheses for each frozen $K_p$.

\section{Transfer through the conserved exponential family}
Both $\mu_m$ and $\nu_p$ are Gibbs measures on the same allowed support, so $\log(\mu_m/\nu_p)$ is an affine combination of free-site occupancies. If $q$ has the same marginals as $p$, then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pyth}
 D(q\Vert\nu_p)=D(q\Vert\mu_m)+D(\mu_m\Vert\nu_p).
\end{equation}
Applying \eqref{p3:eq:cs} to $q=p$, and then \eqref{p3:eq:pyth} to $p$ and $Tp$, gives the slightly stronger inequality
\[
 D(Tp\Vert\mu_m)
 \leq(1-\kappa)D(p\Vert\mu_m)-\kappa D(\mu_m\Vert\nu_p).
\]
Discard its last nonpositive term. The marginal profile and $\kappa$ are conserved, so iteration proves the entropy assertion. Finally,
\[
 D(p\Vert\mu_m)=H(\mu_m)-H(p)\leq\sum_{v\in V}h(m_v)\leq n\log2.
\]
Pinsker's inequality and $(1-\kappa)^t\leq e^{-\kappa t}$ prove the stated total-variation bound. In the uniform regime $\beta\leq1/(2\Delta+1)\leq1/3$, binary entropy is increasing on $[0,\beta]$, giving $\sum_vh(m_v)\leq nh(\beta)$. The uniform condition in Theorem~\ref{p3:thm:uniform} implies \eqref{p3:eq:local}, because $m_v+2s_v\leq(2\Delta+1)\beta\leq1$, and gives $1-2s_v\geq1-2\Delta\beta$. On an edgeless graph all $s_v=0$ and the kernel is ordinary coordinate refresh at rate $1/n$. This completes both proofs.

\section{The discrete-time mean-field extension}
The mean-field map $T_{\rm mf}$ chooses the updated vertex $v$ and donor vertex $u$ independently uniformly from $V$, rather than setting $u=v$. It conserves the mean density $\alpha=\mathbb E_p|I|/n$. Denote its maximum-entropy Gibbs equilibrium by $\mu_\alpha$.

\begin{p3theorem}\label{p3:thm:mf}
Suppose $\Delta\geq1$ and $0\leq\alpha\leq1/(2\Delta+1)$. Put $\kappa_\alpha=(1-2\Delta\alpha)/n$. Then
\[
 D(T_{\rm mf}^tp\Vert\mu_\alpha)\leq(1-\kappa_\alpha)^tD(p\Vert\mu_\alpha),\qquad
 \|T_{\rm mf}^tp-\mu_\alpha\|_{\pThreeTV}\leq\sqrt{\frac{nh(\alpha)}2}\,e^{-\kappa_\alpha t/2}.
\]
The zero-density case is trivial. On edgeless graphs the same statements hold with $\kappa_\alpha=1/n$, including the deterministic endpoint densities.
\end{p3theorem}
\begin{proof}
Assume $\alpha>0$, and set $A(p)=n^{-1}\sum_u p(I\cap N[u]=\varnothing)$. The frozen kernel inserts at every available vertex with probability $\alpha/n$ and removes each occupied vertex with probability $A(p)/n$. Indeed the donor's randomly chosen bit is occupied with probability $\alpha$, while a deletion requires its randomly chosen closed neighborhood to be vacant. Thus its reversible Gibbs reference $\nu_p$ has common fugacity $\alpha/A(p)$. The identities and union bounds give
\[
 \alpha+A(p)\leq1,\qquad
 A(p)\geq1-(\Delta+1)\alpha\geq\Delta\alpha>0.
\]
For adjacent configurations the coupling proof above applies with $m_v$ replaced by $\alpha$ and $a_v$ by $A(p)$. Bad mass is at most $\Delta\alpha/n$, and the diagonal mass $A(p)/n$ is sufficient for the same repair. The expected distance is at most
\[
 1-\frac{A(p)+\alpha-\Delta\alpha}{n}
 \leq1-\frac{1-2\Delta\alpha}{n}.
\]
Caputo--Salez therefore gives full-step entropy contraction for this frozen kernel.

The equilibrium exists and has full support: the preceding maximum-entropy support argument applies, since the positive average vacancy $A(p)$ allows deletion of any occupied vertex by transfer to some vacant closed neighborhood. Once the empty set belongs to the support, cross-site transfers supply every singleton and then every independent set. At fixed density, entropy uniqueness gives a Gibbs law with common fugacity. Now $\log(\mu_\alpha/\nu_p)$ is affine in $|I|$, whose expectation is conserved. The same Pythagorean calculation proves the one-step inequality and its iteration. Finally,
\[
 D(p\Vert\mu_\alpha)=H(\mu_\alpha)-H(p)
 \leq\sum_v h(\mu_\alpha(v\in I))\leq nh(\alpha)
\]
by concavity of binary entropy. Pinsker completes the proof. In the edgeless case the frozen kernel is ordinary Bernoulli-$\alpha$ coordinate refresh, and the deterministic endpoints have zero error.
\end{proof}

This is a discrete-time statement. The broader critical-density entropy estimate in \cite[Theorem 1.3]{p3:HC} concerns the continuous-time equation $\dot p=T_{\rm mf}(p)-p$; it is not being transferred to discrete time here. Theorem~\ref{p3:thm:mf} instead extends the discrete-time low-density regime of \cite[Theorem 1.2]{p3:HC} by the direct coupling just proved.

\section{Remaining limitation}
The threshold $1/(2\Delta+1)$ arises from the sufficient supply of diagonal coupling mass $a_v\geq\sum_{u\in N(v)}m_u$ (or $A\geq\Delta\alpha$ in the mean-field case). This need not hold throughout $m_v<1/(\Delta+1)$. No extension beyond this coupling condition, and no claim to have resolved the full near-linear-rate conjecture, is made. The result and its proof are also separate from the earlier qualitative-to-exponential compactness argument: the bound here is global, has an explicit prefactor, and is obtained in one discrete step.
\clearpage
\endgroup
% END RENDERED SOURCE 03


% BEGIN RENDERED SOURCE 04
\begingroup
\chapter{A Tail Obstruction to an Exponential Convergence Conjecture}\label{chapter:4}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\thechapter.\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\theHchapter.\arabic{section}}
\lstset{style=collectedbase}

\begin{chapterabstract}
The constant exchange kernel satisfies the monotonicity and growth conditions in the concluding conjecture of Esenturk and Velazquez, but permits initial laws for which convergence to equilibrium cannot be exponential, even in total variation. A Poisson-clock coupling gives a necessary exponential-tail condition and an explicit counterexample with finite second moment, as well as a variant with every finite polynomial moment. This is a direct application of a known slow-tail mechanism, discussed by Graham for the same mean-field zero-range dynamics. It does not contradict the stronger hypotheses of the proved exponential-convergence theorem.
\end{chapterabstract}

\section{Scope and the constant-kernel dynamics}

The concluding conjecture in \cite{p4:EV} asks for exponential convergence under the hypotheses of its Theorem~1 and monotonicity of the exchange kernel: nondecreasing in the donor coordinate and nonincreasing in the receiver coordinate. The relevant growth and data assumptions are $K(j,k)\leq Cjk$ for large $j,k$ and $M_p(0)<\infty$ for some $p>1$, where $M_p(t):=\sum_{k\geq1}k^p c_k(t)$. Consider
\begin{equation}\label{p4:kernel}
 K(j,k)=1\quad(j\geq1,\ k\geq0),\qquad K(0,k)=0.
\end{equation}
This kernel has all these properties. Normalize the total cluster concentration to one and write $c_k(t)$ for the concentration at size $k\in\pFourN$. The exchange equations reduce to
\begin{align}
 \dot c_0&=c_1-b(t)c_0,\label{p4:eq0}\\
 \dot c_k&=c_{k+1}+b(t)c_{k-1}-(1+b(t))c_k,\qquad k\geq1,\label{p4:eqk}\\
 b(t)&=\sum_{k\geq1}c_k(t)=1-c_0(t)\in[0,1].\nonumber
\end{align}
Thus $c(t)$ is the law of a reflected birth--death process with unit death rate away from zero and birth rate $b(t)$.

Here and below, for probability laws $u,v$ on $\pFourN$,
\begin{equation}\label{p4:norms}
 \pFourTV(u,v)=\sup_{A\subseteq\pFourN}|u(A)-v(A)|
 =\frac12\sum_{k\geq0}|u_k-v_k|,
 \qquad \pFournormplus{u-v}=\sum_{k\geq1}|u_k-v_k|.
\end{equation}
The second expression is the positive-coordinate norm in the exponential estimate under discussion. In particular, for $A\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots\}$, both quantities in \eqref{p4:norms} are at least $u(A)-v(A)$. More generally $\pFourTV(u,v)\leq\pFournormplus{u-v}$, since $|u_0-v_0|\leq\pFournormplus{u-v}$.

\newpage
\section{A probability representation and a necessary tail condition}

For completeness, \eqref{p4:eq0}--\eqref{p4:eqk} define a unique global probability-valued solution for every initial probability law. Indeed, the death and birth operators are bounded on $\ell^1$, and $c\mapsto1-c_0$ is Lipschitz. The resulting vector field is locally Lipschitz, so the Banach-space ordinary differential equation has a unique local solution. Nonnegativity and total mass one are preserved (equivalently, use the positive, conservative propagator for its bounded time-dependent generator). The $\ell^1$ norm therefore stays one, which gives global existence.

Once this deterministic function $b(t)$ is fixed, construct the process from independent rate-one Poisson clocks: a death-attempt clock $D_t$, whose rings remove a particle only when the state is positive, and a birth-attempt clock $B_t$, thinned with acceptance probability $b(t)$. Take both clocks, and the thinning marks, independent of $X_0$. Uniqueness for the bounded linear forward equation identifies its law with $c(t)$. Pathwise,
\begin{equation}\label{p4:coupling}
 X_0-D_t\leq X_t\leq X_0+B_t,\qquad D_t,B_t\sim\operatorname{Pois}(t).
\end{equation}
If $\pFourE X_0^q<\infty$ for some $q>0$, the upper bound and the finite moments of $B_t$ propagate that moment on every finite time interval. In particular, when $\pFourE X_0<\infty$, compensation of the two jump counts gives
\[
 \pFourE X_t-\pFourE X_0=\int_0^t\bigl[b(s)-\pFourPp(X_s>0)\bigr]\,ds=0.
\]
Thus the mean $\rho$ is conserved, without any formal interchange of an infinite weighted series.

The stationary probability laws are precisely
\begin{equation}\label{p4:equilibria}
 q_k=(1-r)r^k,\qquad 0\leq r<1.
\end{equation}
To see this, the stationary flux $J_k=bq_k-q_{k+1}$ satisfies $J_0=0$ by \eqref{p4:eq0} and $J_k=J_{k-1}$ by \eqref{p4:eqk}. Hence $q_{k+1}=bq_k$. Normalization gives \eqref{p4:equilibria} with $r=b$; $b=1$ cannot give a probability law. The mean is $r/(1-r)$, so the equilibrium of mean $\rho$ has $r=\rho/(1+\rho)$.

\begin{p4lemma}[An exponential initial tail is necessary]\label{p4:lemma}
Suppose a probability curve $\mu_t=\mathcal L(X_t)$ admits the lower coupling in \eqref{p4:coupling}, with $D_t$ independent of $X_0$. Let $q$ satisfy
\[
 Q(m):=q(\{m,m+1,\ldots\})\leq A e^{-am}\quad(m\geq1)
\]
for some $A,a>0$. If $\pFourTV(\mu_t,q)\leq C e^{-ct}$ for all $t\geq0$, with $C,c>0$, then, for every integer $N\geq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p4:necessary}
 \pFourPp(X_0\geq N)\leq 2C e^{-cN/4}+2A e^{-aN/2}.
\end{equation}
\end{p4lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set $t=N/4$ and $L=\lceil N/2\rceil$. Markov's inequality gives
$\pFourPp(D_t>N/2)\leq\pFourE D_t/(N/2)=1/2$.
On $\{X_0\geq N,\ D_t\leq N/2\}$, the integer-valued lower bound in \eqref{p4:coupling} implies $X_t\geq L$. Independence therefore yields
\[
 \tfrac12\pFourPp(X_0\geq N)\leq\pFourPp(X_t\geq L)
 \leq\pFourTV(\mu_t,q)+Q(L).
\]
Substitute the assumed bounds and use $L\geq N/2$.
\end{proof}

The same conclusion, with adjusted constants, holds if the assumed exponential convergence starts only at a finite time. This is a necessary condition, not a sufficiency statement. In particular, finitely many polynomial moment assumptions cannot replace it for these dynamics.

\newpage
\section{Explicit initial laws}

\begin{p4proposition}[A finite-second-moment counterexample]\label{p4:example}
For \eqref{p4:eq0}--\eqref{p4:eqk}, take
\begin{equation}\label{p4:initial}
 c_0(0)=\frac47,\qquad c_{2^j}(0)=\frac3{8^j}\quad(j\geq1),
 \qquad c_k(0)=0\quad\text{otherwise}.
\end{equation}
This is a probability law of mean one and second moment three. With
$\pi_k=2^{-k-1}$ and $t_j=2^{j-2}$, for every $j\geq6$,
\begin{equation}\label{p4:lowerbound}
 \pFourTV(c(t_j),\pi)\geq\frac3{256\,t_j^3},
 \qquad \pFournormplus{c(t_j)-\pi}\geq\frac3{256\,t_j^3}.
\end{equation}
Consequently neither distance admits a bound $Ce^{-ct}$ with $C,c>0$, even when the constants may depend on this initial law.
\end{p4proposition}
\begin{proof}
The geometric-series identities are
\[
 \frac47+3\sum_{j\geq1}8^{-j}=1,\qquad
 3\sum_{j\geq1}4^{-j}=1,\qquad
 3\sum_{j\geq1}2^{-j}=3.
\]
More generally, $M_p(0)=3\sum_{j\geq1}2^{(p-3)j}<\infty$ for every $0<p<3$. By the preceding construction, the solution is global and retains mean one, whose stationary law is $\pi$.

Put $L_j=2^{j-1}=2t_j$. By Markov's inequality,
$\pFourPp(D_{t_j}\leq2t_j)\geq1/2$.
A particle starting at $2^j$ is then still at or above $L_j$. Independence and \eqref{p4:coupling} give
\begin{equation}\label{p4:taildifference}
 c(t_j)(\{L_j,L_j+1,\ldots\})\geq\frac3{2\cdot8^j},
 \qquad
 \pi(\{L_j,L_j+1,\ldots\})=2^{-2^{j-1}}.
\end{equation}
For $j\geq6$, $2^{-2^{j-1}}\leq3/(4\cdot8^j)$. For example, the inequality holds at $j=6$, and $2^{j-1}-3j$ is increasing thereafter. Applying \eqref{p4:norms} to this positive tail set proves
\[
 \pFourTV(c(t_j),\pi),\ \pFournormplus{c(t_j)-\pi}
 \ \geq\frac3{4\cdot8^j}=\frac3{256\,t_j^3}.
\]
Since $t_j\to\infty$, this lower bound is incompatible with any $Ce^{-ct}$.
\end{proof}

The obstruction persists even if every polynomial moment is finite. For a second example, set
\begin{equation}\label{p4:allmoments}
 c_{2^j}(0)=2^{-j^2-j}\quad(j\geq1),\qquad
 c_0(0)=1-\sum_{j\geq1}2^{-j^2-j},
\end{equation}
with all other coordinates zero. The sum is less than one, and
\[
 \rho=\sum_{j\geq1}2^{-j^2},\qquad
 M_q(0)=\sum_{j\geq1}2^{-j^2+(q-1)j}<\infty\quad\text{for every }q>0.
\]
For its mean-matched equilibrium $q_k=(1-r)r^k$, $r=\rho/(1+\rho)\in(0,1)$, the same tail event at $t_j=2^{j-2}$ gives
\[
 \pFourTV(c(t_j),q),\ \pFournormplus{c(t_j)-q}
 \ \geq 2^{-j^2-j-1}-r^{2^{j-1}}
 \ \geq 2^{-j^2-j-2}
\]
for all sufficiently large $j$. The last inequality follows because $2^j$ grows faster than $j^2$. The resulting lower bound again dominates every $Ce^{-ct_j}$ eventually. Neither example has an exponentially bounded initial tail. The argument also excludes exponential approximation to any other fixed stationary law, since every such law has a geometric tail.

\newpage
\section{What the counterexample does and does not establish}

Theorem~10 of \cite{p4:EV} imposes more than the concluding conjecture. In particular, its structural hypothesis H3 includes
\[
 K(j,k)=j a_k+b_j+\varepsilon\beta_j\alpha_k,\qquad a_k\geq\widetilde a>0,
\]
