with $b_j$ nondecreasing and a bounded perturbation. For fixed $k$, the right side is at least $j\widetilde a+b_1-O(1)$ and diverges as $j\to\infty$, unlike \eqref{p4:kernel}. The accepted Theorem~10 also requires $p>2$, which \eqref{p4:initial} can meet. The failure identified here concerns the broader conjecture, not the proved theorem. No convergence theorem, or optimal decay-rate assertion, is needed for the counterexample.

The mechanism is not new. Graham \cite{p4:Graham}, Section~1 (journal p.~500; arXiv PDF p.~4), explicitly explains that the initial tail affects relaxation and obstructs exponential relative-entropy decay when it is not geometrically bounded. Section~5 describes the same time-inhomogeneous birth--death equation used above. His relative-entropy observation alone does not imply a lower bound in total variation: the usual entropy-to-total-variation inequality has the opposite logical direction. The coupling proof here supplies that direct lower bound and makes the obstruction explicit for the later conjecture. This note makes no claim of priority for the mechanism or for identifying its relevance to that conjecture.
\clearpage
\endgroup
% END RENDERED SOURCE 04

\CollectedPart{Partition Products and Coefficient Inequalities}{These three chapters concern coefficient inequalities for partition-related generating functions. Each retains its complete analytic argument, finite coverage analysis and executable certificates. Their claims are separate: denominator log concavity, shifted-polynomial unimodality and regular-partition log concavity. Unimodality in Chapter 6 must not be read as global coefficient log concavity.}

% BEGIN RENDERED SOURCE 05
\begingroup
\chapter{Log Concavity of Reciprocal Subsum Denominators}\label{chapter:5}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\thechapter.\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\theHchapter.\arabic{section}}
\lstset{style=collectedbase}
\lstset{basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize,columns=fullflexible,keepspaces=true,
 breaklines=true,breakatwhitespace=false,showstringspaces=false,upquote=true,
 frame=single,rulecolor=\color{black!18},framesep=5pt,
 numbers=left,numberstyle=\tiny\color{black!45},numbersep=8pt,
 xleftmargin=12pt,xrightmargin=3pt,aboveskip=10pt,belowskip=10pt,
 literate={³}{{$^3$}}1 {²}{{$^2$}}1 {σ}{{$\sigma$}}1 {≤}{{$\le$}}1 {≥}{{$\ge$}}1,
 postbreak=\mbox{$\hookrightarrow$\space}}
\begin{chapterabstract}
We prove that the coefficients of the reciprocal-subsum denominator
$D_n(x)$ of Ballantine--Beck--Feigon--Maurischat are log-concave exactly
when $n\notin\{3,5,6,7\}$. The proof groups its known dyadic factorization
into balanced four-row blocks. A saddle-point determinant identity, with
explicit phase and entire-frequency tail control, proves strict
log-concavity away from a bounded edge. An effective eta--Bessel estimate
for four-colored distinct partitions and exact truncation bounds settle
all block parameters at least $28000$. Integer-only directed dyadic
certificates establish the remaining $1\,119\,898\,742$ edge inequalities
for parameters $257$ through $27999$; smaller bases are checked exactly.
All rational analytic checkers and the finite certificate engine are
embedded as reproducible source listings. The finite and analytic regions
are explicitly matched, including floors, midpoints and boundary indices.
\end{chapterabstract}


\clearpage
\section{The denominator and the theorem}

For a finite sequence of nonnegative numbers, log-concavity means
\(a_k^2\ge a_{k-1}a_{k+1}\) at every internal index. Strict
log-concavity means that all these inequalities are strict. A polynomial
is called log-concave when its coefficient sequence is log-concave. All
products considered below have positive coefficients throughout their
degree intervals.

For a partition \(\lambda\) put
\(\operatorname{sp}(\lambda,x)=\prod_i(1+x^{\lambda_i})\). Ballantine,
Beck, Feigon and Maurischat define the denominator
\(D_n(x)=\operatorname{den}(n,x)\) associated to the sum of the
reciprocals of these subsum polynomials. More explicitly, put
\(D_n^*(x)=\prod_{i=1}^n(1+x^i)^{\lfloor n/i\rfloor}\),
\(h_\lambda=D_n^*/\operatorname{sp}(\lambda,x)\) and
\(G_n=\gcd_{\lambda\vdash n}h_\lambda\), with monic normalization; then
\(D_n=D_n^*/G_n\). Their Proposition 3.8(a) gives the identity
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:known}
D_n(x)=\prod_{i=1}^n(1+x^i)^{1+\lfloor\log_2(n/i)\rfloor}
      =\prod_{r\ge0}Q_{\lfloor n/2^r\rfloor}(x),
\qquad Q_m(x)=\prod_{j=1}^m(1+x^j),\quad Q_0=1.
\end{equation} We use this known factorization, and do not claim it as a
result of this paper. In particular the object here is exactly the
ordinary denominator in BBFM Conjecture 4, not a binary numerator or a
modified conjecture \cite{p5:BBFM}. The corresponding entry of Table 1 of
Chen--Ono--Zhang is marked open \cite{p5:COZ}.

\begin{p5theorem}[Denominator log-concavity]\label{p5:thm:main}
For every positive integer $n$, the coefficients of $D_n$ are log-concave
if and only if $n\notin\{3,5,6,7\}$.
\end{p5theorem}

The proof is computer-assisted in a precise, finite sense. Its analytic
part treats continuously varying saddle parameters using rational
interval envelopes, and treats all sufficiently large block sizes. Its
remaining finite coefficient inequalities are certified by integer-only
directed dyadic arithmetic. The complete source listings needed to
reproduce the calculations are included in this standalone manuscript.
Ordinary machine integer arithmetic, a C++17 compiler supporting 128-bit
integers, and Python arbitrary-precision integers and rational fractions
are the computational trust base; this is not a proof-assistant
formalization.

Define the balanced four-row block \begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:block}
B_n(x)=Q_n(x)Q_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(x)
             Q_{\lfloor n/4\rfloor}(x)Q_{\lfloor n/8\rfloor}(x)
       =\sum_{k=0}^{M_n}b_{n,k}x^k,
\quad
M_n=\sum_{a=0}^3\frac{\lfloor n/2^a\rfloor(\lfloor n/2^a\rfloor+1)}2.
\end{equation}

\begin{p5theorem}[Balanced blocks]\label{p5:thm:block}
For every integer $n\ge8$, $B_n$ is strictly log-concave at every internal
coefficient index.
\end{p5theorem}

Here is the coverage map for its proof. Palindromicity always permits
restriction to \(1\le k\le\lfloor M_n/2\rfloor\).

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Block parameter & Index region & Proof component\\
\midrule
$8\le n\le256$ & Entire half-range & Exact finite base\\
$257\le n\le27999$ & $k\le40399$ & Directed dyadic certificate\\
$n\ge100$ & Saddle $0\le t\le1/100$ & Fourier determinant estimate\\
$n\ge28000$ & $1\le k\le40400$ & Four-color truncation transfer\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The Fourier estimate implies that any uncovered lower-half index for
\(n\ge100\) is strictly smaller than \(40400\). Thus these regions
overlap and exhaust the domain. No numerical sampling of an unbounded
parameter is used to infer a universal inequality.

\subsection{Relation to prior work}

The factorization \eqref{p5:eq:known} and its increment recurrence are from
BBFM. Convolution preservation, eta transformation, Bessel identities,
Fourier inversion and elementary interval arithmetic are classical
tools. Agarwal--Garg--Maji, Theorem 2.6, already imply eventual
log-concavity of the four-colored distinct-part sequence by specializing
their colored regular partition theorem to \((r,\ell,d)=(4,2,2)\)
\cite{p5:AGM}. We need an explicit error bound and a verified all-index
version, which we supply below. Chern's work treats Fourier coefficients
of eta quotients including weight zero \cite{p5:Chern}; a direct contour
argument here avoids any reliance on a negative-weight-only Rademacher
formula. The contribution asserted by this manuscript is the explicit
balanced-block proof and its assembled finite certificates, followed by
the denominator conclusion. No priority claim for the auxiliary infinite
sequence, or for a new general principle of log-concavity, is needed.

\section{Elementary reduction to balanced blocks}

\begin{p5lemma}[Convolution]\label{p5:lem:convolution}
The convolution of two nonnegative log-concave sequences with interval
support is log-concave and has interval support.
\end{p5lemma}
\begin{proof}
Extend each sequence by zero to the integers. For such a sequence $a$, its
Toeplitz matrix $T(a)_{ij}=a_{j-i}$ has nonnegative minors of size two.
Indeed, on positive entries this follows by multiplying inequalities of
the decreasing ratios $a_{r+1}/a_r$: among pairs with equal sum, the pair
with indices closer together has the larger product. If a diagonal entry
is zero while both off-diagonal entries are positive, interval support
rules this out; all other zero cases are immediate. Conversely, adjacent
minors give log-concavity. Since $T(a*b)=T(a)T(b)$, the size-two
Cauchy--Binet formula expresses every minor of the product as a sum of
products of nonnegative minors. Each relevant sum is finite for finite
supports. The convolution support is the sum of the two support intervals.
\end{proof}

The support of \(Q_m\) is its entire interval: by induction the subset
sums of \(1,\ldots,m\) fill \([0,m(m+1)/2]\). Products inherit this
property. For \(n\ge8\), choose the unique \(j\ge0\) with
\(8\cdot16^j\le n<128\cdot16^j\) and set \(m_s=\lfloor n/16^s\rfloor\).
Nested floors in \eqref{p5:eq:known} give
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:reduction}
D_n=B_{m_0}B_{m_1}\cdots B_{m_{j-1}}D_{m_j},
\qquad m_s\ge128\ (s<j),\quad 8\le m_j\le127.
\end{equation} Consequently Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:block} and
the finite base \(D_8,\ldots,D_{127}\) imply
Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:main} for \(n\ge8\) by
Lemma~\ref{p5:lem:convolution}. The small cases and the
finite bases are treated explicitly in the final verification section.

\section{Fourier inversion and the saddle criterion}

Consider first any polynomial \(P(x)=\prod_j(1+x^j)^{m_j}\) of degree
\(M\), where the multiplicities are nonnegative integers and \(m_1>0\).
For an integer \(0<k\le M/2\), the strictly decreasing continuous
function \[
\mu(t)=\sum_j\frac{m_jj}{1+e^{tj}}
\] has a unique solution \(t\ge0\) to \(\mu(t)=k\); its range is
\((0,M/2]\). Write \(r=e^{-t}\), \(p_j=r^j/(1+r^j)\),
\(v_j=p_j(1-p_j)\), \(\sigma^2=\sum_jm_jj^2v_j\) and
\(M_q=\sum_jm_jj^qv_j\). The centered characteristic function is \[
\phi(\theta)=\frac{P(re^{i\theta})}{P(r)}e^{-ik\theta}.
\] Fourier inversion gives
\(r^{k+s}a_{k+s}/P(r)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\phi(\theta)e^{-is\theta}\,d\theta\) for integral \(s\). Multiplication
for \(s=0\) and for \(s=-1,1\), followed by symmetrization, therefore
gives the exact determinant identity
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:determinant}
\frac{r^{2k}(a_k^2-a_{k-1}a_{k+1})}{P(r)^2}
=\frac1{(2\pi)^2}\operatorname{Re}\iint_{[-\pi,\pi]^2}
\phi(\theta)\phi(\psi)[1-\cos(\theta-\psi)]\,d\theta\,d\psi.
\end{equation} The use of \(\phi(\theta)\phi(\psi)\), not a complex
conjugate, is important. The imaginary part vanishes by simultaneous
reflection of both variables.

On a symmetric core \(C=[-\delta,\delta]\) choose the continuous phase
\(\phi=Ae^{i\alpha}\), with \(A\) even and \(\alpha\) odd. Put \[
J_q=\int_C\theta^qA(\theta)\,d\theta,\quad
H_q=\int_C\theta^q\alpha(\theta)^2A(\theta)\,d\theta,\quad
\Lambda=\int_C\theta\alpha(\theta)A(\theta)\,d\theta.
\] The inequalities \(1-\cos u\le u^2/2\) and \(\cos v\ge1-v^2/2\) bound
the phase loss on the core by one quarter of
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:parity}
\iint_{C^2}(\theta-\psi)^2(\alpha(\theta)+\alpha(\psi))^2
 A(\theta)A(\psi)\,d\theta\,d\psi
=2(H_2J_0+H_0J_2)-4\Lambda^2.
\end{equation} This identity follows simply by expansion and parity.
Discarding the negative square is safe; replacing absolute cubic phase
bounds by a signed pointwise cubic expression would not be safe. If
\(|\alpha(\theta)|\le c|\theta|^3\) and
\(A(\theta)\le e^{-a\sigma^2\theta^2/2}\) on \(C\), full Gaussian
moments bound the loss by \begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:phasegeneral}
\frac{c^2}2(J_8J_0+J_6J_2)
\le\frac{120\pi c^2}{a^5\sigma^{10}}.
\end{equation} Here the Gaussian double factorials are \(105\) and
\(15\).

For \(T_q=\int_{[-\pi,\pi]\setminus C}\theta^qA(\theta)\,d\theta\),
parity and the quadratic kernel bound show that the absolute
contribution outside \(C^2\) is at most
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:tailgeneral}
T_2J_0+T_0J_2+T_0T_2.
\end{equation} All subsequent bounds on the double integral omit the
common positive factor \((2\pi)^{-2}\) and are normalized by
multiplication by \(\sigma^4\).

\section{Balanced moment and tail certificates}

\subsection{Moment bounds with real scale and floored row lengths}

\begin{p5lemma}\label{p5:lem:moments}
Let $n\ge h\ge100$, with $n$ integral, and set
$v(x)=e^x/(1+e^x)^2$,
$S_q=\sum_{a=0}^3\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/2^a\rfloor}j^qv(j/h)$,
$L=\min(n,5h)$. With $\sigma^2=S_2$, $M_3=S_3$, $M_4=S_4$,
\[
M_3^2/\sigma^6<15/(2h),\qquad
\sigma^2/L^2>79h/1000,\qquad
M_4/\sigma^2<14h^2,\qquad \sigma^2<7h^3.
\]
\end{p5lemma}
\begin{proof}\leavevmode\par
\subsubsection{A single error bound, including the floor}

For a nonnegative differentiable unimodal function \(f\) with \(f(0)=0\),
let \(M_x=\max_{0\le u\le x}f(u)\). For any real endpoint \(x\ge0\)
and mesh \(\Delta>0\),
\begin{equation}
\left|\Delta\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor x/\Delta\rfloor}f(j\Delta)
       -\int_0^x f(u)\,du\right|\le\Delta M_x. \label{p5:eq:mom1}
\end{equation}
In particular, a separate floor error need not be added to the usual
Riemann-sum error. To prove \eqref{p5:eq:mom1}, put
\(r(u)=u-\Delta\lfloor u/\Delta\rfloor\). Integration by parts against
the step function gives the exact error
\[
 E=\int_0^x r(u)f'(u)\,du-r(x)f(x).
\]
Its upper bound is at most \(\Delta\int_0^x(f')_+\le\Delta M_x\).
If \(f\) has not reached its maximum by \(x\), the lower bound is
\(-\Delta f(x)=-\Delta M_x\). Otherwise, the negative variation is
\(M_x-f(x)\), so the lower bound is again
\(-\Delta(M_x-f(x))-\Delta f(x)=-\Delta M_x\).
This also handles an endpoint on the mesh, where \(r(x)=0\).

Write \(f_q(x)=x^qv(x)\), \(I_q(z)=\int_0^z f_q(x)\,dx\).
The logarithmic derivative \(q/x-\tanh(x/2)\) decreases strictly, so
\(f_q\) is unimodal. Since \(v(x)\le1/4\) and \(v(x)\le e^{-x}\),
\[
 \max_{0\le u\le x}f_2(u)\le A_2(x):=\min(1,x^2/4),\qquad
 \max_{0\le u\le x}f_3(u)\le A_3(x):=\min(2,x^3/4).
\]
The global constants follow from \(4/e^2<1\) and \(27/e^3<2\).
With \(z=n/h\), \eqref{p5:eq:mom1} applied to each row gives
\begin{equation}
\left|\frac{S_q}{h^{q+1}}-F_q(z)\right|
 \le\frac{E_q(z)}h,
 \quad F_q(z):=\sum_{a=0}^3 I_q(z/2^a),
 \quad E_q(z):=\sum_{a=0}^3 A_q(z/2^a). \label{p5:eq:mom2}
\end{equation}
This is exactly where the actual balanced multiplicities are retained.

\subsubsection{Rational grid certificate}

The grid has denominator \(G=256\), with integral endpoints
\(i/G\) for \(0\le i\le16G\). The exponential is enclosed by its
positive Taylor sum through degree 150 and its geometric remainder
\[
 T_{150}(x)\le e^x\le
 T_{150}(x)+\frac{x^{151}/151!}{1-x/152},\qquad 0\le x\le16.
\]
Bounds are rounded outward to denominator \(10^{18}\). Because
\(u/(1+u)^2\) decreases for \(u\ge1\), substituting the exponential
bounds in reverse order encloses \(v(x)\). On a grid cell \([l,u]\),
monotonicity of \(v\) then gives rational lower and upper integrals by
multiplying the appropriate endpoint bound by
\((u^{q+1}-l^{q+1})/(q+1)\). Their cumulative sums are denoted by
\(L_q,U_q\).

For a parameter cell \(z\in[i/G,(i+1)/G]\), the exact checker in Appendix~\ref{p5:app:sources} forms
\[
 s_i=\sum_{a=0}^3
 L_2\!\left(\frac{\lfloor i/2^a\rfloor}{G}\right)
 -\frac{E_2((i+1)/G)}{100},
\]
\[
 t_i=\sum_{a=0}^3
 U_3\!\left(\frac{\lceil(i+1)/2^a\rceil}{G}\right)
 +\frac{E_3((i+1)/G)}{100}.
\]
By \eqref{p5:eq:mom2} and monotonicity, \(S_2/h^3\ge s_i\) and
\(S_3/h^4\le t_i\). The exact checks establish
\[
 s_i>0,\qquad t_i^2/s_i^3<15/2\quad(G\le i<16G),
\]
and
\[
 \frac{s_i}{((i+1)/G)^2}>79/1000\quad(G\le i<5G).
\]
For \(z\ge16\), use
\[
 S_2/h^3\ge F_2(16)-4/100,
 \qquad S_3/h^4\le4I_3(\infty)+8/100.
\]
Here \(I_3(\infty)=\tfrac92\zeta(3)\) and
\(\zeta(3)<1203/1000\), the latter following from the bound $\sum_{j=1}^{10}j^{-3}+1/200<1203/1000$.
These bounds prove the same ratio throughout the infinite remaining
parameter range.

For \(z\ge5\), monotonicity of the row sums in their endpoints gives
\[
 \frac{\sigma^2}{25h^3}
 \ge\frac{F_2(5)-E_2(5)/100}{25}>79/1000.
\]
Together with the finite grid this proves the core-variance inequality.

\subsubsection{Fourth moment and upper variance}

To justify the fourth-moment reduction explicitly, put $w_j=j^2v(j/h)$.
For any finite upper endpoint, the identity
\[
\Big(\sum w_j\Big)\Big(\sum w_jm_jj^2\Big)
-\Big(\sum w_jm_j\Big)\Big(\sum w_jj^2\Big)
=\frac12\sum_{i,j}w_iw_j(m_i-m_j)(i^2-j^2)\le0
\]
holds because the balanced multiplicities $m_j$ decrease. It bounds
\(M_4/\sigma^2\) by the single-row truncated ratio. Extending that row
only increases the weighted average of \(j^2\). Since
\(\max f_4<5\), the integral identities
\(I_4(\infty)=7\pi^4/30\) and \(I_2(\infty)=\pi^2/6\) give
\[
 \frac{M_4}{h^2\sigma^2}
 \le\frac{7(22/7)^4/30+5/100}{(3.14)^2/6-1/100}<14.
\]
Finally each of the four rows is bounded above by its infinite sum; \eqref{p5:eq:mom1}
and \(\max f_2<1\) imply
\[
 \frac{\sigma^2}{h^3}\le4\left(\frac{(22/7)^2}{6}+\frac1{100}\right)<7.
\]

For completeness, the integrals used here follow by integration by parts
from $v(x)=-\frac{d}{dx}(1+e^x)^{-1}$ and expansion into exponentials:
\[
I_q(\infty)=q!\,(1-2^{1-q})\zeta(q)\quad(q>1).
\]
All finite comparisons in this proof are reproduced by the moment checker
in Appendix~\ref{p5:app:sources} (\texttt{verify\_balanced\_moments.py}).
Its loops cover all $3840$ parameter cells, while the separate monotone
bounds cover $z\ge16$ and the variance range $z\ge5$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Absolute Fourier tails}

\begin{p5lemma}\label{p5:lem:tails}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{p5:lem:moments}, let
$A(\theta)=|B_n(e^{-1/h+i\theta})/B_n(e^{-1/h})|$ and $L=\min(n,5h)$.
Then
\[
\sigma\int_{2/L\le|\theta|\le\pi}A(\theta)\,d\theta<1/3000,
\qquad
\sigma^3\int_{2/L\le|\theta|\le\pi}\theta^2A(\theta)\,d\theta<1/50.
\]
The same bounds hold with $L=n$ and the actual tilted variance when
$n\ge100$ and $0\le t\le1/n$.
\end{p5lemma}
\begin{proof}
The following finite rational envelope construction proves the inequalities
uniformly on the indicated continuous domain. Its full implementation is
\texttt{verify\_balanced\_tail\_h100.py} in Appendix~\ref{p5:app:sources}.
\subsubsection{Global modulus and Abel summation}

Write \(m_j=\#\{a: j\le\lfloor n/2^a\rfloor\}\) and
\(w_j=m_jv(j/h)\). The sequence \(w_j\) decreases, and \(w_1\le1\).
The Bernoulli product gives
\[
 \log A(\theta)\le-2\sum_jw_j\sin^2(j\theta/2)
 =-W_0+\sum_jw_j\cos(j\theta),\quad W_0=\sum_jw_j.
\]
For \(0<|\theta|\le\pi\), every partial cosine sum is at most
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^N\cos(j\theta)
 =\frac{\sin((N+1/2)|\theta|)}{2\sin(|\theta|/2)}-\frac12
 \le C(\theta):=\frac1{2\sin(|\theta|/2)}-\frac12.
\]
Abel summation, and \(C\ge0\), therefore give
\[
 \sum_jw_j\cos(j\theta)\le w_1C(\theta)\le C(\theta).
\]
This is the global gain over a bound using only the first \(\lfloor h\rfloor\)
weights.

Put \(z=n/h\) and
\[
 J(z)=\sum_{a=0}^3\int_0^{z/2^a}v(x)\,dx
 =\sum_{a=0}^3\left(\frac12-\frac1{1+e^{z/2^a}}\right).
\]
Since \(v\) decreases, for \(N=\lfloor n/2^a\rfloor\),
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^Nv(j/h)
 \ge h\int_{1/h}^{(N+1)/h}v(x)\,dx
 \ge h\int_0^{z/2^a}v(x)\,dx-\frac14.
\]
Consequently \(W_0\ge hJ(z)-1\).

For \(s=h|\theta|\le8\), one has \(|\theta|\le0.08<1\), so
\(\sin(|\theta|/2)\ge23|\theta|/48\). Hence, writing \(H=100\),
\[
 A(s/h)\le\exp\{-hF_A(s,z)\},\quad
 F_A(s,z)=J(z)-\frac1{2H}-\frac{24}{23s}.
\]
For every \(8/h\le|\theta|\le\pi\), monotonicity of
\(\sin(|\theta|/2)\) instead gives
\[
 A(\theta)\le\exp\{-hF_\infty(z)\},\quad
 F_\infty(z)=J(z)-\frac1{2H}-\frac3{23}.
\]

\subsubsection{A complementary prefix bound}

Let a parameter cell have \(z\ge z_0\), and a frequency cell be
\(s\in[\ell,u]\). For each of the four rows choose only the weights
\[
 1\le j\le\lfloor hc_a\rfloor,
 \qquad c_a=\min(z_0/2^a,2/u).
\]
They exist in that row and satisfy \(|j\theta|\le2\). Therefore
\(\sin(j|\theta|/2)\ge5j|\theta|/12\).

Define \(I_2(x)=\int_0^x u^2v(u)\,du\). The function \(f_2(x)=x^2v(x)\) is
unimodal, has maximum less than one, and increases on \([0,2]\):
its logarithmic derivative is \(2/x-\tanh(x/2)>0\) there.
The standard unimodal right-Riemann error is at most \(1/h\).
On the increasing interval the right-Riemann sum is a lower bound for
the integral without an error subtraction. Thus the selected variance
in row \(a\), divided by \(h^3\), is at least
\[
 I_2(c_a-1/H)-\frac{\mathbf1_{\{c_a>2\}}}{H}.
\]
All arguments \(c_a-1/H\) in the certificate are positive.
The resulting modulus estimate on that frequency cell is
\[
 A(s/h)\le e^{-hF_P},\qquad
 F_P=\frac{25\ell^2}{72}
 \sum_{a=0}^3\left[I_2(c_a-1/H)
       -\frac{\mathbf1_{\{c_a>2\}}}{H}\right].
\]
Combining both independent upper bounds for the modulus uses the exponent
\(F=\max(F_P,F_A(\ell,z_0))\), not their sum.

\subsubsection{Variance upper bounds}

For a finite parameter cell \(z\in[z_0,z_1]\subset[1,5]\), let
\(x_a=z_1/2^a\). The same Riemann estimate gives
\[
 \frac{\sigma^2}{h^3}\le B(z_1):=
 \sum_{a=0}^3\left[I_2(x_a)+\frac{E(x_a)}H\right],
 \qquad
 E(x)=\begin{cases}x^2v(x),&x\le2,\\1,&x>2.\end{cases}
\]
For \(x\le2\), the endpoint value bounds the sum-to-integral error;
for \(x>2\), the global maximum bound suffices.
For \(z\ge5\), the cruder bound \(\sigma^2<9h^3\) suffices:
\(v(x)\le e^{-x}\), the integral of \(x^2e^{-x}\) is 2, and its
maximum is less than one, giving \(\sigma^2/h^3\le4(2+1/H)<9\).

\subsubsection{Exact finite certificate covering continuous parameters}

The tail checker uses \(z_0=i/32\), \(z_1=(i+1)/32\), for
\(32\le i<160\), and a final cell \(z\ge5\).
For a finite cell the true starting frequency is
\(s=2/\min(z,5)\ge2/\min(z_1,5)\).
For the final cell it is \(s=2/5\).
Round this lower endpoint downward to the grid of denominator 40,
and partition up to \(s=8\) into cells of width \(1/40\).
This only enlarges the integration region.

The integral enclosures for \(I_2\) use a grid of denominator 256 on
\([0,5]\). At every endpoint the exponential is enclosed by its degree-80
Taylor polynomial and a geometric upper bound for the positive remainder.
Bounds are rounded outward to denominator \(10^{24}\). Monotonicity of
\(v\) then bounds each integral cell by the appropriate endpoint value of
\(v\) times the exact integral of \(x^2\); a final partial cell is treated
the same way. The formula for \(J\) is enclosed directly from the
exponential lower bounds.

For each parameter cell choose a rational \(R>\sqrt B\). If \(E_F\) is
a rational upper bound for \(e^{-HF}\), the near-tail moment bounds are
\[
 x_{\rm near}\le2R\sqrt H\sum_{[\ell,u]}(u-\ell)E_F,
\]
\[
 y_{\rm near}\le2R^3H^{3/2}\sum_{[\ell,u]}
           \frac{u^3-\ell^3}{3}E_F.
\]
These also hold at every \(h\ge H\), because the certificate checks
\(F>3/(2H)\), making both relevant powers of \(h\) times \(e^{-hF}\)
decreasing. The far tail satisfies
\[
 x_{\rm far}\le2\pi RH^{3/2}e^{-HF_\infty},\qquad
 y_{\rm far}\le\frac{2\pi^3}3R^3H^{9/2}e^{-HF_\infty}.
\]
Here \(F_\infty>9/(2H)\) is also checked, and \(\pi<22/7\) is used.

All negative exponential bounds are rational: a Taylor lower bound for
\(e^{1/100}\), iterated with downward rounding, certifies
\(e^{k/100}\) from below. Rounding the desired positive exponent down to
that grid and taking the reciprocal, rounded upward, gives \(E_F\).
The tail checker proves \(x_{\rm near}+x_{\rm far}<1/3000\) and
\(y_{\rm near}+y_{\rm far}<1/50\) separately in every parameter cell.
Thus this is a finite certificate for a uniform analytic tail lemma,
not a finite coefficient scan.

\subsubsection{Central-tilt extension}

For \(0\le t\le1/n\), set \(h=n\). Every variance weight satisfies
\(v(tj)\ge v(j/n)\); thus all preceding damping estimates for \(z_0=1\)
remain valid. Abel summation still applies because \(m_jv(tj)\) decreases
and its first term is at most one.
The actual variance satisfies
\[
 \frac{\sigma^2}{n^3}\le
 \sum_{a=0}^3\left(\frac{2^{-3a}}{12}
       +\frac{2^{-2a}}{8n}+\frac{2^{-a}}{24n^2}\right)<\frac1{10}
 \quad(n\ge100),
\]
by \(v\le1/4\) and the exact sum-of-squares formula. The tail checker repeats
the first parameter cell's tail bounds with \(R>\sqrt{1/10}\), which
still yields the two displayed bounds. Its slightly enlarged starting
region contains the required \(|\theta|\ge2/n\).

Every comparison asserted by that checker is between exact rational
numbers. In particular its final decimal reports are not inputs to any
proof comparison. This completes the proof of the tail lemma.
\end{proof}

\section{Positivity of the Fourier determinant at scale 100}

\begin{p5theorem}\label{p5:thm:fourier}
Let $n\ge100$. At every lower-half integer coefficient index of $B_n$
whose saddle satisfies $0\le t\le1/100$, the Tur\'an determinant is strictly
positive. The reflected index has the same property.
\end{p5theorem}
\begin{proof}
First suppose $n\ge h=1/t\ge100$. The case $0\le tn\le1$, which includes
all remaining saddles in the statement, is treated at the end.
\subsection{Normalization and moment inputs}

Use the centered characteristic function $\phi=Ae^{i\alpha}$, variance $\sigma^2$, and identity \eqref{p5:eq:determinant}. All contributions below omit its common
positive factor \((2\pi)^{-2}\), and are normalized by multiplying by
\(\sigma^4\). Put
\[
 L=\min(n,5h),\quad \delta=1/L,\quad C=[-\delta,\delta],
 \quad a=11/12,\quad b=64/49.
\]
Lemma~\ref{p5:lem:moments} gives
\[
 M_3^2/\sigma^6<15/(2h),\qquad
 \sigma^2/L^2>79h/1000,\qquad
 M_4/\sigma^2<14h^2.
\]
If \(n\le5h\), \(M_4\le n^2\sigma^2\); otherwise
\(M_4<14h^2\sigma^2<L^2\sigma^2\). Thus in either case
\(M_4\le L^2\sigma^2\).

\subsection{A sharper cubic phase estimate}

For a Bernoulli factor write
\[
 f_p(u)=\log(1-p+pe^{iu})-ipu,\qquad v=p(1-p).
\]
For \(|u|\le1\), put \(s=\sin^2(u/2)\le1/4\). The exact derivative is
\[
 |f_p'''(u)|^2
 =v^2\frac{1-4v+4vs}{(1-4vs)^3}\le v^2.
\]
Indeed, the denominator minus the numerator is
\[
 4v(1-4s+12vs^2-16v^2s^3)\ge0,
\]
since \(1-4s\ge0\) and \(3-4vs>0\). Taylor's integral remainder therefore
proves
\begin{equation}
|\operatorname{Im}f_p(u)|\le v|u|^3/6. \label{p5:eq:four1}
\end{equation}
This estimate retains every phase term.


For clarity, the rare-factor series used below is
\[
\operatorname{Im} f_p(u)=\sum_{\ell\ge1}
 \frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}q^\ell}{\ell}(\sin(\ell u)-\ell u),\qquad
\sum_{\ell\ge1}\ell^2q^\ell=\frac{q(1+q)}{(1-q)^3},
\]
where $p=q/(1+q)$ and $0<q<1$. The absolute cubic bound follows directly
by summing $|\sin(\ell u)-\ell u|\le|\ell u|^3/6$.
All weights $j\le L$ are active on $C$.
 If there are weights \(j>L\),
then \(L=5h\) and their \(q=e^{-j/h}<e^{-5}<1/144\). The absolutely
convergent logarithm series, using $|\sin u-u|\le |u|^3/6$, gives
\[
 |\operatorname{Im} f_p(j\theta)|
 \le\frac{vj^3|\theta|^3}{6}
       \left(\frac{1+q}{1-q}\right)^3
 <\frac7{40}vj^3|\theta|^3,
\]
because \((145/143)^3/6<7/40\). Summing this and \eqref{p5:eq:four1} yields
\begin{equation}
|\alpha(\theta)|\le(7/40)M_3|\theta|^3\qquad(\theta\in C). \label{p5:eq:four2}
\end{equation}
The phase is continuous and odd. The active denominator bound and the
rare-factor bound \(((1-q)/(1+q))^2>49/64\) give
\(A(\theta)\ge e^{-b\sigma^2\theta^2/2}\), by $\log(1-y)\ge-y/(1-y)$ and $\sin^2 u\le u^2$.

The modulus identity is
$A(\theta)^2=\prod_j(1-4v_j\sin^2(j\theta/2))^{m_j}$.
For active factors, $1-4v_j\sin^2(j\theta/2)\ge\cos^2(1/2)\ge49/64$.
Together with the rare-factor denominator just bounded, this and
$\log(1-y)\ge-y/(1-y)$ give the stated lower Gaussian.
For the upper bound use the global inequality
$\sin^2(u/2)\ge u^2/4-u^4/48$ and $\log(1-y)\le-y$.
It gives

\begin{equation}
A(\theta)\le
 \exp\{-\sigma^2\theta^2/2+M_4\theta^4/24\}
 \le e^{-a\sigma^2\theta^2/2}\qquad(\theta\in C). \label{p5:eq:four3}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Positive core and phase loss}

The lower-Gaussian endpoint satisfies
\[
 b\sigma^2\delta^2>b(79/10)>(16/5)^2.
\]
The standard Gaussian tail estimates, with
\(e^{128/25}>160\) and \(\sqrt{2\pi}>5/2\), show that both retained
moments of orders 0 and 2 exceed \(49/50\) of their full Gaussian moments.
Since \(L\ge100\), the kernel correction
\(1-1/(3L^2)>999/1000\). The normalized positive-modulus contribution is
therefore greater than
\begin{equation}
\frac{999}{1000}\left(\frac{49}{50}\right)^2
 \frac{2\pi}{b^2}>\frac72. \label{p5:eq:four4}
\end{equation}

Use the exact phase parity identity \eqref{p5:eq:parity}, including its negative square term before safely discarding this
nonpositive contribution to the loss. With \eqref{p5:eq:four2}, the loss is at most
\begin{equation}
\frac{120\pi}{a^5}\left(\frac7{40}\right)^2
       \frac{M_3^2}{\sigma^6}
 <\frac{120\pi}{a^5}\left(\frac7{40}\right)^2
       \frac{15}{2h}
 <\frac{27}{20}\qquad(h\ge100). \label{p5:eq:four5}
\end{equation}
Also, \eqref{p5:eq:four3} gives the absolute core moments
\(J_0<3/\sigma\) and \(J_2<3/\sigma^3\).

\subsection{First tail: between one and two core widths}

Set \(U=\sigma^2/L^2>79h/1000\) and \(r=L|\theta|\). The global quartic
bound and \(M_4\le L^2\sigma^2\) give
\[
 A(\theta)\le e^{-Uf(r)/2},\qquad f(r)=r^2-r^4/12.
\]
The function \(f\) is increasing on \([1,2]\). For \(p=1/2,3/2\),
\(U^pe^{-Uf(r)/2}\) decreases in \(U\ge79/10\), since
\((79/10)f(1)>3\). Therefore the normalized moments satisfy
\[
 x_1:=\sigma T_{0,1}
 \le2\sqrt{79/10}\int_1^2e^{-(79/20)f(r)}dr<1/40,
\]
\begin{equation}
y_1:=\sigma^3T_{2,1}
 \le2(79/10)^{3/2}\int_1^2r^2e^{-(79/20)f(r)}dr<1/4. \label{p5:eq:four6}
\end{equation}
These two integral inequalities are certified by 256 rational rectangles:
use the left endpoint in the increasing \(f\), the right endpoint in
\(r^2\), \(\sqrt{79/10}<3\), and a positive rational Taylor sum to bound
the exponential reciprocal above. The checker is \texttt{verify\_balanced\_h100\_constants.py} in Appendix~\ref{p5:app:sources}.

\subsection{Remaining tail and positivity}

Lemma~\ref{p5:lem:tails} proves
\[
 x_2:=\sigma\int_{2/L\le|\theta|\le\pi}A(\theta)d\theta<1/3000,
\]
\begin{equation}
y_2:=\sigma^3\int_{2/L\le|\theta|\le\pi}\theta^2A(\theta)d\theta<1/50.
\label{p5:eq:four7}
\end{equation}
It retains all four balanced row lengths in low-frequency prefix bounds
and uses Abel summation for the full far-frequency interval; no frequency
interval is omitted. Its finite grid verifies analytic envelopes for
all real \(h\ge100\) and all integer \(n\ge h\), not just sampled saddles.

For total tail moments set \(x=x_1+x_2\), \(y=y_1+y_2\). Keeping the
quadratic kernel, the normalized absolute contribution outside \(C^2\)
is at most \(3x+3y+xy\). By \eqref{p5:eq:four6}--\eqref{p5:eq:four7},
\[
 3x+3y+xy<9/10.
\]
Combining \eqref{p5:eq:four4}, \eqref{p5:eq:four5}, and this bound gives a normalized real double integral
strictly greater than
\[
 7/2-27/20-9/10=5/4>0.
\]
This proves the tilted theorem. All remaining scalar comparisons are reproduced by the same core-constants checker; the moment and remaining-tail checks were proved separately.

\subsection{Central extension down to block size 100}

Suppose \(n\ge100\) and \(0\le tn\le1\). Set \(L=n\), regardless of
whether \(t=0\). Since \(v(tj)\ge v(j/n)\), the balanced moment lower
bound applied at scale \(h=n\) implies
\[
 \sigma^2/n^2>(79/1000)n.
\]
Every weight is active on \(C=[-1/n,1/n]\). Thus \eqref{p5:eq:four1} gives the stronger
phase bound \(|\alpha(\theta)|\le M_3|\theta|^3/6\), and
\(M_3\le n\sigma^2\). The modulus bounds \eqref{p5:eq:four3} and the positive core
bound \eqref{p5:eq:four4} are unchanged, since \(M_4\le n^2\sigma^2\). The normalized
phase loss is now less than
\[
 \frac{120\pi}{36a^5}\frac{n^2}{\sigma^2}
 <\frac{120\pi}{36a^5}\frac{1000}{79n}
 <\frac{41}{20}\qquad(n\ge100).
\]
The first-tail argument still uses only
\(U=\sigma^2/n^2>79n/1000\) and \(M_4\le n^2\sigma^2\), so \eqref{p5:eq:four6} holds.
The remaining-tail certificate includes this central case: the modulus
only decreases as \(t\) decreases from \(1/n\), while the normalization
is bounded separately by \(\sigma^2<n^3/10\). Thus \eqref{p5:eq:four7} also holds.
The normalized determinant integral is therefore greater than
\[
 7/2-41/20-9/10=11/20>0.
\]
This proves the central extension including $t=0$.


\end{proof}

\begin{p5corollary}\label{p5:cor:edge}
For $n\ge100$, any lower-half index not covered by
Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:fourier} satisfies $1\le k<40400$.
\end{p5corollary}
\begin{proof}
An uncovered saddle has $h=1/t<100$. Since all multiplicities are at most
four,
\[
k\le4\sum_{j\ge1}je^{-j/h}\le4(h^2+h)<40400.
\]
For the middle inequality, for $j-1\le x\le j$ one has
$je^{-j/h}\le(x+1)e^{-x/h}$; integrate and sum to obtain
$\sum_{j\ge1}je^{-j/h}\le\int_0^\infty(x+1)e^{-x/h}dx=h^2+h$.
\end{proof}

\section{An effective all-index four-color theorem}

Put \[
F(q)=\prod_{j\ge1}(1+q^j)^4
    =\prod_{j\ge1,\ j\ \mathrm{odd}}(1-q^j)^{-4}
    =\sum_{n\ge0}q_4(n)q^n.
\] The identity follows by cancelling the even factors in Euler's
product; it is also a bijection between colored distinct-part and
colored odd-part partitions.

\begin{p5theorem}\label{p5:thm:q4}
The sequence $q_4(n)$ is strictly log-concave at every integer $n\ge1$.
For $n\ge10000$, with $s=n+1/6$, it moreover satisfies
\[
2\log q_4(n)-\log q_4(n-1)-\log q_4(n+1)
>\frac9{10(s+3)^2}.
\]
\end{p5theorem}
\begin{proof}
The analytic proof treats every $n\ge10000$; the finite base is specified
at the end of this section.
\subsection{Standard identities and the main term}

Put \(A=\pi^2/3\), so \(3<A<4\). Dedekind eta inversion gives, for
\(\Re z>0\),

\begin{equation}
F(e^{-z})=\frac14\exp\left({A\over z}+{z\over6}\right)
 G(z),\qquad G(z)=\frac1{F(e^{-2\pi^2/z})}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour1}
\end{equation}

The sign is \textbf{plus} \(z/6\). For example, \eqref{p5:eq:qfour1} follows by taking the ratio
of the eta inversion identities for
\(E(z)=\prod_{j\ge1}(1-e^{-jz})\):

\[
 E(z)=\sqrt{2\pi/z}\,e^{-\pi^2/(6z)+z/24}E(4\pi^2/z),
 \qquad F(e^{-z})=(E(2z)/E(z))^4.
\]

For real \(s>0\), define

\begin{equation}
M(s)=\frac14\sqrt{A/s}\ I_1(2\sqrt{As})
 ={A\over4}\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(As)^j}{j!(j+1)!}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour2}
\end{equation}

In particular, \(4M(s)\) is the residue at zero of
\(\exp(sz+A/z)\).

The eta transformation and the Bessel integral and canonical product
are the classical identities recorded in \cite{p5:DLMF}. Their use here does
not involve an asymptotic expansion with an uncontrolled remainder.

\subsection{A deliberately weak lower bound on the main term}

For \(x\ge1\), the standard integral is

\[
 I_1(x)={x\over\pi}\int_{-1}^1e^{xu}\sqrt{1-u^2}\,du.
\]

Restrict to \(1-1/x\le u\le1-1/(2x)\). Then \(u\ge0\),
\(e^{xu}\ge e^{x-1}\), and
\(\sqrt{1-u^2}\ge1/\sqrt{2x}\). Consequently

\begin{equation}
I_1(x)\ge{e^{x-1}\over2\pi\sqrt{2x}}
 >{e^x\over25\sqrt x}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour3}
\end{equation}

The numerical inequality is elementary: \(2\pi\sqrt2e<25\), using
\(\pi<22/7\), \(\sqrt2<10/7\), and \(e<11/4\).
Put \(h=\sqrt{s/A}\), \(t=1/h\). From \eqref{p5:eq:qfour2}--\eqref{p5:eq:qfour3}, for \(h\ge50\),

\begin{equation}
M(s)>{e^{2Ah}\over300h^{3/2}},
 \label{p5:eq:qfour4}
\end{equation}

since \(x=2Ah\ge1\) and \(100\sqrt{2A}<300\).

\subsection{An elementary global minor-arc bound}

For \(r=e^{-t}\) and real \(\theta\), the odd-part product gives the
absolutely convergent expansion

\[
 \log F(r)-\log|F(re^{i\theta})|
 =4\sum_{j\text{ odd}}\sum_{m\ge1}
 {r^{jm}\over m}(1-\cos(jm\theta))
 \ge4S(t,\theta),
\]

where summing the first harmonic yields exactly

\begin{equation}
S(t,\theta)=\sum_{j\text{ odd}}e^{-jt}(1-\cos(j\theta))
 ={1\over2\sinh t}
 -{\sinh t\cos\theta\over2(\sinh^2t+\sin^2\theta)}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour5}
\end{equation}

Suppose \(0<t\le1/50\) and \(t\le|\theta|\le\pi\). By evenness assume
\(\theta\ge t\). On \([t,\pi/2]\), use
\(\sin\theta\ge\sin t\) and \(\cos\theta\le1\); on \([\pi/2,\pi]\),
the second term in \eqref{p5:eq:qfour5} is nonpositive. In both cases,

\[
 S(t,\theta)\ge
 {\sin^2t\over2\sinh t(\sinh^2t+\sin^2t)}.
\]

The elementary bounds \(\sin t\ge(99/100)t\), \(\sinh t\le(101/100)t\),
and \(\sin t\le t\), valid on the stated interval, imply

\begin{equation}
4S(t,\theta)\ge {1960200\over2040301}\,{1\over t}
 >{9\over10t}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour6}
\end{equation}

For clarity, \(\sin t\ge t-t^3/6\) proves the sine lower bound. For sinh,
its power series divided by \(t\) is at most
\(\sum_{m\ge0}t^{2m}=1/(1-t^2)<101/100\).

As \(G(t)<1\) on the positive real line, \eqref{p5:eq:qfour1} yields
\(F(e^{-t})<(1/4)e^{A/t+t/6}\). For an integer \(n\) let
\(s=n+1/6\) and choose \(t=\sqrt{A/s}\). The coefficient integral outside
\(|\theta|\le t\) is therefore bounded in absolute value by

\begin{equation}
E_{\rm minor}\le\frac14e^{2Ah-(9/10)h}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour7}
\end{equation}

The factor \(1/(2\pi)\) in coefficient extraction cancels the crude
upper bound \(2\pi\) for total minor-arc length.

\subsection{Major-arc correction and explicit closure to the residue}

On \(z=t+i\theta\), \(|\theta|\le t\), we have
\(\Re(1/z)\ge1/(2t)\). Write
\(w=e^{-2\pi^2/z}\), \(u=|w|\le e^{-\pi^2h}\). From the product in \eqref{p5:eq:qfour1},

\[
 |\log G(z)|\le4\sum_{j\ge1}{u^j\over1-u^j}
 \le{4u\over(1-u)^2}.
\]

For \(u\le1/100\), the last quantity is at most \(5u\), and
\(e^{5u}-1\le10u\). Thus
\(|G(z)-1|\le10e^{-\pi^2h}\).
Also \(\Re(sz+A/z)\le2Ah\) on the vertical major segment. Integrating
its length \(2t\) with factor \(1/(8\pi)\), we get

\begin{equation}
E_{\rm transform}\le {5t\over2\pi}e^{2Ah-\pi^2h}
 <t e^{2Ah-\pi^2h}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour8}
\end{equation}

Orient the vertical segment from \(t-it\) to \(t+it\). Close it by the
\textbf{left} arc of \(|z|=\sqrt2t\), oriented counterclockwise from angle
\(\pi/4\) to \(7\pi/4\). This is a positively oriented contour around
zero. On the arc,

\[
 \Re(sz+A/z)
 =\left(s+{A\over2t^2}\right)\Re z
 ={3s\over2}\Re z\le{3\over2}Ah,
\]

because \(\Re z\le t\). Its length is
\((3\pi/2)\sqrt2t\). By the residue theorem and \eqref{p5:eq:qfour2}, the difference
between the major integral without \(G\) and \(M(s)\) has absolute value
at most

\begin{equation}
E_{\rm arc}\le{3\sqrt2t\over16}e^{(3/2)Ah}
 <{t\over2}e^{(3/2)Ah}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour9}
\end{equation}

Only the explicit exponential \(e^{sz+A/z}\), not \(F\), is continued to
the left half-plane. No invalid use of an infinite product there is involved.

\subsection{Uniform relative error}

Combining \eqref{p5:eq:qfour4}, \eqref{p5:eq:qfour7}--\eqref{p5:eq:qfour9}, using \(A>3\) and \(\pi^2>9\), gives

\begin{equation}
\left|\frac{q_4(n)}{M(n+1/6)}-1\right|
 \le75h^{3/2}e^{-9h/10}
 +150h^{1/2}e^{-3h/2}
 +300h^{1/2}e^{-9h}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour10}
\end{equation}

For \(h\ge50\), division by \(h^{3/2}e^{-9h/10}\) bounds the right side
by
\(75+(150/h)e^{-3h/5}+(300/h)e^{-81h/10}<84<300\).
Therefore the relative error is at most

\begin{equation}
\epsilon(h):=300h^{3/2}e^{-9h/10}
 <\frac1{100(s+3)^2}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour11}
\end{equation}

Here are elementary checks of the last strict inequality, to avoid relying
on floating-point evaluation. Since \(s=Ah^2<4h^2\) and \(h\ge50\),
\(s+3<5h^2\). It suffices to prove
\(750000h^{11/2}e^{-9h/10}<1\). Replace \(h^{11/2}\) by \(h^6\).
The function \(h^6e^{-9h/10}\) decreases on \([50,\infty)\), and

\[
 750000\cdot50^6=11718750000000000<20^{15}<e^{45}.
\]

The final inequality follows from \(e^3>20\); the Taylor sum through
degree 8 already exceeds 20.

\subsection{Main-term curvature from the canonical product}

The classical canonical product for \(J_1\), equivalently for \(I_1\),
gives

\[
 M(s)={A\over4}\prod_{m\ge1}
 \left(1+{4As\over j_{1,m}^2}\right),
\]

where \(j_{1,m}\) are the positive real zeros of \(J_1\). Local uniform
convergence permits twice differentiating the logarithm for \(s>0\):

\begin{equation}
-\frac{d^2}{ds^2}\log M(s)
 =\sum_{m\ge1}\frac1{(s+j_{1,m}^2/(4A))^2}
 >\frac1{(s+2)^2}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour12}
\end{equation}

For the last inequality, \(j_{1,1}<4\) and \(A>3\) suffice. The zero
bound does not require a numerical zero table: the alternating series

\[
 J_1(4)=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m2^{2m+1}}{m!(m+1)!}
\]

has, after its partial sum through summation index 6, a nonpositive alternating remainder;
the partial sum is exactly \(-934/14175<0\). Since \(J_1(x)>0\) for small
positive \(x\), it has a positive zero below 4. All its nonzero zeros are
real, as required by the standard product for order 1.

Integrating \eqref{p5:eq:qfour12} against the triangular kernel on \([-1,1]\), for \(s>1\),

\begin{equation}
2\log M(s)-\log M(s-1)-\log M(s+1)
 >\frac1{(s+3)^2}.
 \label{p5:eq:qfour13}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Transfer to the exact coefficients}

Let \(n\ge10000\), \(s=n+1/6\). The parameters for all three indices
\(n-1,n,n+1\) satisfy \(h\ge50\), since \(A<10/3\) and
\((9999+1/6)/(10/3)>2500\). Thus \eqref{p5:eq:qfour11} applies to all three.
Write \(q_4(m)=M(m+1/6)(1+\delta_m)\). On these three indices,

\[
 |\delta_m|<\frac1{100(s+2)^2}<\frac12,
 \qquad |\log(1+\delta_m)|\le2|\delta_m|.
\]

The possible loss in the log-concavity expression \eqref{p5:eq:qfour13} is therefore less
than \(8/[100(s+2)^2]\), and

\[
 \frac{8}{100(s+2)^2}<\frac1{(s+3)^2}
\]

for \(s>1\) (indeed for all \(s\ge0\)). Hence
\(2\log q_4(n)-\log q_4(n-1)-\log q_4(n+1)>0\)
for every \(n\ge10000\).


For the stronger displayed bound, use
$(s+3)/(s+2)\le10/9$ for $s\ge7$ and
$(8/100)(10/9)^2=8/81<1/10$ in the preceding curvature comparison.

\subsection{The finite base}

The script \texttt{verify\_four\_color\_prefix.py} in
Appendix~\ref{p5:app:sources} multiplies the four-colored odd-part factors
through degree $10000$ using arbitrary-precision integers. Each color of
an odd part $j$ is inserted by the ascending recurrence
$a[k]\leftarrow a[k]+a[k-j]$, for $j\le k\le10000$; ascending order
allows arbitrary repetitions. It checks strict determinants for every
$1\le k\le9999$. Independently it verifies all coefficients by the
formal logarithmic-derivative identity
\[
nq_4(n)=4\sum_{j=1}^n\sigma_{\rm odd}(j)q_4(n-j),
\qquad \sigma_{\rm odd}(j)=\sum_{d\mid j,\ d\ {
m odd}}d.
\]
The minimum integer determinant in this finite range is $4$.
The SHA-256 of the newline-separated decimal coefficient list, including
the final newline, is
\begin{center}\ttfamily\small
b682e4acb8c8ed380987918a0512ce917e75a05c0635d009334ce44bc52da9ee.
\end{center}
Thus the exact finite base and the proved analytic tail cover every index.
\end{proof}

\section{Quantitative transfer to finite four-row products}

We next compare any four-row product \(P=\prod_{i=1}^4Q_{L_i}\) with
\(F\). The result is used only for the balanced blocks, whose shortest
row is \(\lfloor n/8\rfloor\).

\subsection{The coefficient deficit: a genuine union bound}

Let

\[
 F(x)=\prod_{j\ge1}(1+x^j)^4=\sum_{k\ge0}q_kx^k,
 \qquad P(x)=\prod_{i=1}^4Q_{L_i}(x)=\sum_k b_kx^k,
 \qquad d=\min_iL_i.
\]

All parts carry one of four colors, and each colored size is used at
most once. A partition counted by \(q_k-b_k\) contains a colored part
\((i,j)\) with \(j>L_i\). Removing that specified part injects the set
of such partitions into the unrestricted colored partitions of \(k-j\).
Thus, with \(q_t=0\) for \(t<0\),

\begin{equation}
0\le q_k-b_k\le\sum_{i=1}^4\sum_{j>L_i}q_{k-j}
 \le4\sum_{j>d}q_{k-j}.                                      \label{p5:eq:transfer1}
\end{equation}

Multiple offending parts merely produce overcounting, in the permissible
direction. This argument does not divide by a polynomial and assume
positivity of an alternating inverse.

The odd-part representation of \(F\) injects partitions of \(k-1\) into
those of \(k\) by adding a part of size 1 in a fixed color; partitions
having no size-1 part of that color give a nonempty complement. Hence
\(q_k>q_{k-1}>0\). Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:q4} implies

\[
 R_k:=q_{k-1}/q_k<1\ (k\ge1),\qquad R_{k-1}<R_k\ (k\ge2).
\]

Consequently, for \(1\le j\le k\),

\[
 \frac{q_{k-j}}{q_k}=\prod_{u=k-j+1}^kR_u\le R_k^j.
\]

Define \(\delta_k=1-b_k/q_k\). Summing the geometric series in
\eqref{p5:eq:transfer1} gives

\begin{equation}
0\le\delta_k\le\frac{4R_k^{d+1}}{1-R_k}.                     \label{p5:eq:transfer2}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Explicit ratio bound}

The preceding proof supplies, for all the indices used below,

\[
 q_k=M(k+1/6)(1+\eta_k),\qquad
 M(s)=\tfrac14\sqrt{A/s}\,I_1(2\sqrt{As}),\qquad A=\pi^2/3>3,
\]

and \(|\eta_k|<1/[100(k+19/6)^2]\). For \(k\ge10000\), both \(k\) and
\(k-1\) satisfy the auxiliary proof's \(h\ge50\) hypothesis. Writing
\(s=k+1/6\), their errors are each less than \(1/[100(s+2)^2]\).

For \(f=\log M\), direct differentiation gives

\begin{equation}
f'(s)=\sqrt{A/s}\,\frac{I_0(2\sqrt{As})}{I_1(2\sqrt{As})}-\frac1s
 >\sqrt{A/s}-\frac1s.                                      \label{p5:eq:transfer3}
\end{equation}

Here \(I_1'(x)=I_0(x)-I_1(x)/x\) follows from the power series; the
standard angular integral gives, for \(x>0\),

\[
 I_0(x)-I_1(x)=\frac1\pi\int_0^\pi
 e^{x\cos\theta}(1-\cos\theta)\,d\theta>0.
\]

Integrating \eqref{p5:eq:transfer3} from \(s-1\) to \(s\), and using
\(|\log(1+\eta)|\le2|\eta|\) for \(|\eta|<1/2\), yields

\begin{equation}
\log(q_k/q_{k-1})>
 \sqrt{3/s}-\frac1{s-1}-\frac1{25(s+2)^2}.                  \label{p5:eq:transfer4}
\end{equation}

For every \(k\ge10000\),

\[
 \sqrt{3k/s}>\frac{173}{100},\qquad
 \frac{\sqrt k}{s-1}<\frac1{50},\qquad
 \frac{\sqrt k}{25(s+2)^2}<\frac1{100}.
\]

For the first, \(3k/s\ge180000/60001>(173/100)^2\). For the second,
\(s-1>k/2\); for the third, \(s+2>k\). Therefore \eqref{p5:eq:transfer4}
proves

\begin{equation}
\boxed{R_k<e^{-17/(10\sqrt k)}}\qquad(k\ge10000).           \label{p5:eq:transfer5}
\end{equation}

This constant is deliberately nonoptimal.

\subsection{How much curvature must be retained?}

Let \(\kappa_k=2\log q_k-\log q_{k-1}-\log q_{k+1}\). The preceding
proof, with \(s=k+1/6\), gives

\begin{equation}
\kappa_k>\frac1{(s+3)^2}-\frac{8}{100(s+2)^2}
 >\frac{9}{10(s+3)^2}=:\kappa_k^0 \qquad(k\ge10000).        \label{p5:eq:transfer6}
\end{equation}

Indeed \((s+3)/(s+2)\le10/9\) for \(s\ge7\), and
\((8/100)(10/9)^2=8/81<1/10\).

Only the deficit at the center index \(k\) is needed. Since
\(b_{k\pm1}\le q_{k\pm1}\),

\begin{equation}
\frac{b_k^2}{b_{k-1}b_{k+1}}
 \ge (1-\delta_k)^2e^{\kappa_k}.                            \label{p5:eq:transfer7}
\end{equation}

This is stated when neighboring coefficients are positive; if their
product is zero and \(b_k>0\), strict log-concavity is immediate. The
exact sufficient condition is \(\delta_k<1-e^{-\kappa_k/2}\). A
convenient certified sufficient condition is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{\delta_k\le\kappa_k^0/4
       =\frac9{40(k+19/6)^2}.}                            \label{p5:eq:transfer8}
\end{equation}

In fact \(\kappa_k^0<1\), so \eqref{p5:eq:transfer8} implies
\(\delta_k<1/2\), and \(2\log(1-\delta_k)\ge-4\delta_k\ge-\kappa_k^0\).
The strict inequality \(\kappa_k>\kappa_k^0\) then makes
\eqref{p5:eq:transfer7} exceed 1. No error bounds at the neighboring
truncated coefficients are required.

Combining the deficit bound and the ratio estimate gives
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:deficitlarge}
\delta_k<4\left(1+\frac{10\sqrt{k}}{17}\right)
\exp\left(-\frac{17(d+1)}{10\sqrt{k}}\right)
\qquad(k\ge10000).
\end{equation} Indeed \(e^a\ge1+a\) implies \((1-e^{-a})^{-1}\le1+1/a\).

\subsection{A uniform cutoff at block size 28000}

\begin{p5proposition}\label{p5:prop:large}
If $d=\min_i L_i\ge3500$, then $P$ is strictly log-concave at every
index $1\le k\le40400$ in its support. In particular, every $B_n$ with
$n\ge28000$ is strictly log-concave at every internal index.
\end{p5proposition}
\begin{proof}
There are two index ranges; both are needed.
For $1\le k\le9999$, the exact small-transfer checker and its included independent-prefix
dependency in Appendix~\ref{p5:app:sources} prove
\[
R_{9999}<99/100,\qquad
(q_k^2-q_{k-1}q_{k+1})/q_k^2>10^{-9}.
\]
The dependency generates coefficients through $10001$ by the displayed
logarithmic-derivative recurrence; every division is checked exact.
Thus it is independent of the colored-factor multiplication above.
The checker also verifies the single integer inequality
\[
400\cdot10^{12}\cdot99^{3501}<100^{3501}.
\]
Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:q4} makes $R_k$ increase, so
$4R_k^{d+1}/(1-R_k)<10^{-12}$ throughout this range. Consequently
\[
b_k^2=(1-\delta_k)^2q_k^2
 >(1-2\cdot10^{-12})q_k^2
 >(1-10^{-9})q_k^2
 >q_{k-1}q_{k+1}\ge b_{k-1}b_{k+1}.
\]
This treats the entire interval, in particular the transition indices
$3501,\ldots,9999$, not merely the indices where the finite and infinite
products agree exactly.

For $10000\le k\le40400$, we have $\sqrt{k}<201$,
$1+10\sqrt{k}/17<120$, and
$17(d+1)/(10\sqrt{k})>17\cdot3501/(10\cdot201)>59/2$.
Positive Taylor sums through degrees $8$ and $10$ give $e^3>20$ and
$e^{5/2}>12$. Hence \eqref{p5:eq:deficitlarge} yields
\[
\delta_k<\frac{480}{12\cdot20^9}
 <\frac9{40\cdot40404^2}
 <\frac9{40(k+19/6)^2}.
\]
These comparisons too are exact rational assertions in the small-transfer
checker. The sufficient curvature condition already proved therefore
applies. Finally for $n\ge28000$ the shortest row of $B_n$ is at least
$3500$. Corollary~\ref{p5:cor:edge} and this edge argument cover the whole
lower half, and palindromicity covers the other half.
\end{proof}

\section{Finite certificates and completion of the proof}

\subsection{Exact finite bases and the exceptions}

For the denominator family the recurrence \[
\frac{D_n}{D_{n-1}}=\prod_{a=0}^{v_2(n)}(1+x^{n/2^a})
\] is the increment identity of BBFM, Proposition 3.10. Starting with
\(D_0=1\), the included \texttt{check.py} multiplies these factors by
descending integer-array updates, checks palindromicity, and tests every
lower-half Tur\textquotesingle an determinant for \(1\le n\le256\). The
only failing parameters are \(3,5,6,7\), and in particular
\(D_8,\ldots,D_{127}\) pass. The following explicit witnesses also
verify all four exceptions:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$n$ & $k$ & $(a_{k-1},a_k,a_{k+1})$ & $a_k^2-a_{k-1}a_{k+1}$\\
\midrule
3 & 2 & $(2,2,3)$ & $-2$\\
5 & 5 & $(11,14,18)$ & $-2$\\
6 & 2 & $(3,5,9)$ & $-2$\\
7 & 2 & $(3,5,9)$ & $-2$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The same computation verifies \(D_1,D_2,D_4\). If \(n=0\) is allowed,
\(D_0=1\) is vacuously log-concave.

For balanced blocks the quotient is instead
\begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:increment}
\frac{B_n}{B_{n-1}}
=\prod_{\substack{0\le a\le3\\2^a\mid n}}(1+x^{n/2^a}).
\end{equation} The fourth-row stopping condition is essential. The
included \texttt{check\_balanced\_blocks.py} verifies ordinary
log-concavity for \(8\le n\le256\) with arbitrary-precision integers.
The directed certificate described next certifies strict inequalities
throughout those complete half-ranges as well.

\subsection{Directed dyadic arithmetic: why the finite predicate is a proof}

A nonzero dyadic endpoint is represented as \(m2^e\), with
\(2^{63}\le m\le2^{64}-1\) and \(e\) a signed 64-bit integer; zero is
\((0,0)\). Every coefficient is stored between two such nonnegative
endpoints. All operations in \texttt{dyadic\_certificate.cpp} are
integer-only. Here are the invariants sufficient to verify its
soundness.

For an exact unsigned 128-bit numerator, normalization discards low bits
for downward rounding and increments the retained mantissa exactly when
the discarded remainder is nonzero for upward rounding. A carry to
\(2^{64}\) is normalized exactly to \(2^{63}\) at the next exponent. For
addition order the exponents \(e_a\ge e_b\) and put \(d=e_a-e_b\). If
\(d\le64\), the exact numerator \(m_a2^d+m_b\) is at most
\((2^{64}-1)2^{64}+(2^{64}-1)=2^{128}-1\) and so is formed without
overflow. If \(d>64\), the smaller operand is strictly less than one
unit at exponent \(e_a\); the larger operand and its next representable
successor enclose the sum. Zero operands are handled exactly. Exponent
calculations use signed 128-bit intermediates and reject any
out-of-range conversion. Therefore downward addition of lower endpoints
and upward addition of upper endpoints preserve the interval invariant.

To compare products, each pair of 64-bit mantissas is multiplied exactly
into 128 bits. A product with mantissa bit-length \(b\) and exponent
\(e\) has top-bit position \(e+b\). Unequal positions decide the
comparison. If positions agree, the shorter mantissa is shifted to the
larger bit-length, at most \(128\), which aligns the exponents exactly
without discarding a bit. Zero products are treated separately. Thus the
certificate predicate \begin{equation}\label{p5:eq:predicate}
\operatorname{lo}(b_{n,k})^2>
\operatorname{hi}(b_{n,k-1})\operatorname{hi}(b_{n,k+1})
\end{equation} is an exact sufficient condition for the true strict
inequality. A failed comparison is reported as inconclusive, never as a
counterexample.

Starting at \(B_0=1\), the descending update
\(p[j]\leftarrow p[j]+p[j-w]\) for a new weight \(w\) implements
multiplication by \(1+x^w\). Both right-hand entries are old values. The
loop in \eqref{p5:eq:increment} stops at the first failed divisibility
test, since failure for \(2^a\) implies failure for higher powers.
Entries above a chosen cap may be discarded: no positive weight can feed
a discarded high index back into a smaller one. The stored cap is
\(K+1\), and the checked inclusive endpoint is
\(\min(K,\lfloor M_n/2\rfloor)\); both neighbors are available. Choosing
a first reported row does not skip its earlier construction. Each
polynomial is palindromic, so the checked prefix also certifies its
reflected prefix. The even-degree midpoint is included.

The requested construction has at most \(52495\) factors; both exact
coefficients and upper endpoints are bounded inductively by the exactly
representable \(2^{52495}\). All exponent and degree storage bounds are
therefore amply within range. In particular \(M_{27999}=520598750\).
These facts verify the arithmetic, recurrence, truncation, and index
invariants independently of performance tests of the implementation.

\subsection{Completed finite ranges and integrity checks}

The exact source SHA-256 is

\begin{center}\ttfamily\small
b3154e0381004bb878a33af90f513d5e20b3ab157db6080bf1fc34c2ccb9c7fe.
\end{center}

The completed scans have the following scope. The parameter \(K\) is
inclusive; ``strict'\,' counts only the proved predicates
\eqref{p5:eq:predicate}, not estimated determinant signs.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
Parameter range & $K$ & Rows & Strict inequalities & Inconclusive\\
\midrule
$8$--$256$ & $40400$ & $249$ & $1\,878\,532$ & $0$\\
$257$--$27999$ & $40399$ & $27\,743$ & $1\,119\,898\,742$ & $0$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The first scan covers complete half-ranges. The second covers complete
half-ranges only through \(n=348\), and otherwise the lower-half prefix
through \(40399\) and its reflection. It is not represented as a
complete coefficient scan for every polynomial. The missing middle
indices are covered analytically by
Corollary~\ref{p5:cor:edge}.

For the two newline-delimited JSON outputs, respectively, the SHA-256
identifiers are

\begin{center}\ttfamily\small
fc6f65c4a2650f67b7cc97656460da2412ba4887d4cb99dfb13c2ede565be57a\\[3pt]
93e20573a804fc4e213286519b3ff26b725fa584709253d95de7766c72d4e14f.
\end{center}

Each output was checked for its metadata and source identity,
consecutive complete parameter rows, degree formula, retained cap,
checked and reflected index endpoints, empty inconclusive lists, strict
counts and terminal summary. The exact expected total is independently
computed as \(\sum_n\min(K,\lfloor M_n/2\rfloor)\). Process exit was
successful; completeness checks are additionally necessary because a
stream-write failure alone need not produce a C++ exception. The
supplemental validator in the appendix specifies and reproduces these
checks. Hashes identify artifacts; neither a hash nor a self-reported
source macro substitutes for the mathematical arithmetic invariants or
for actually executing the scan.

\subsection{Completion}

For \(8\le n\le256\), the complete finite certificate proves
Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:block}. For \(257\le n\le27999\), every
index that is not covered by the Fourier theorem has distance at most
\(40399\) from an edge, and is covered by the second certificate. For
\(n\ge28000\), Proposition~\ref{p5:prop:large} proves the
whole claim. This establishes Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:block}
for every integer in its stated range. Finally \eqref{p5:eq:reduction},
Lemma~\ref{p5:lem:convolution}, and the finite \(D_n\) base
prove Theorem~\ref{p5:thm:main}, with the four displayed
witnesses showing that the exceptions are genuine. \hfill\(\square\)

\section{Reproduction protocol}

The document contains all executable source text; no repository notes,
external data files, external bibliography, or TeX input files are
required. The historical explanatory comments inside the verbatim
listings describe the local scope of each checker; the full assembled
proof is the preceding text. Source names in those comments are not
runtime dependencies unless an import is present. The only sibling-file
dependency among the analytic checkers is the small-transfer script's
use of the included independent \(q_4\) prefix script.

Save the appendix listings under their indicated names in
\texttt{research/bbfm\_logconcavity/}, starting from an otherwise empty
working directory. The extraction script in the appendix automates this
from this single TeX file. Use Python without the \texttt{-O} flag,
since assertions are part of the certificate. Run the moment, tail and
core scripts, the four-color prefix and small-transfer scripts, and both
finite base scripts. Compile the C++ source with its actual SHA-256
macro, then run the two finite ranges. The commands and validator are
included below. The C++ runs require neither floating-point arithmetic
nor mathematical libraries. The Python programs use only the standard
library.

A successful rerun must reproduce the exact strict counts and zero
inconclusives, not merely return quickly or print a partial prefix. A
nonzero exit, assertion failure, incomplete output, or different source
hash is not a completed reproduction. No computation in this proof
relies on TeX shell escape.


\clearpage
\CollectedAppendix
\section{Complete executable sources}\label{p5:app:sources}
The following listings are the full source files, not excerpts. Their
numeric comparisons use integers or rational fractions; floating-point
conversions in reporting fields do not affect certification. In the TeX
source, each listing has a machine-readable source marker. The extraction
utility after the listings preserves the source bytes, including final
newlines. Python requires version 3.8 or later; C++ requires a compiler
with the GNU-compatible \texttt{\_\_int128} integer extension.

\subsection{verify\_balanced\_moments.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small ee5953f5b7037b6f84fdc48593dd01ee17777cbabb0f129421497d1b7e930bf5}.
%<source:verify_balanced_moments.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
"""Exact all-parameter moment certificate for four balanced rows, h >= 100.

No coefficient scan. Every inequality is rational; displayed decimals are reports.
See balanced_moments.md for the sum/integral and floor arguments.
"""
from fractions import Fraction as F
from pathlib import Path
import json

H = 100
GRID = 256
LAST = 16 * GRID
DEN = 10**18

def down(x):
    return F(x.numerator * DEN // x.denominator, DEN)

def up(x):
    return F(-((-x.numerator * DEN) // x.denominator), DEN)

def exp_interval(x):
    term = total = F(1)
    for j in range(1, 151):
        term *= x / j
        total += term
    nxt = term * x / 151
    return down(total), up(total + nxt / (1 - x / 152))

vl, vu = [], []
for i in range(LAST + 1):
    el, eu = exp_interval(F(i, GRID))
    vl.append(down(eu / (1 + eu)**2))
    vu.append(up(el / (1 + el)**2))
lo, hi = {2: [F(0)], 3: [F(0)]}, {2: [F(0)], 3: [F(0)]}
for i in range(LAST):
    l, u = F(i, GRID), F(i + 1, GRID)
    for q in (2, 3):
        area = (u**(q + 1) - l**(q + 1)) / (q + 1)
        lo[q].append(lo[q][-1] + vl[i + 1] * area)
        hi[q].append(hi[q][-1] + vu[i] * area)

def max_bound(q, x):
    return min(F(1 if q == 2 else 2), x**q / 4)

def balanced_interval(q, i, upper=False):
    # z >= i/GRID for lower, z <= i/GRID for upper.
    result = F(0)
    for a in range(4):
        d = 2**a
        j = (i + d - 1)//d if upper else i//d
        result += (hi if upper else lo)[q][j]
    return result

def error(q, z):
    return sum((max_bound(q, z / 2**a) for a in range(4)), F(0)) / H

worst = (F(0), None)
variance_worst = (F(100), None)
for i in range(GRID, LAST):
    z_upper = F(i + 1, GRID)
    s_lower = balanced_interval(2, i) - error(2, z_upper)
    t_upper = balanced_interval(3, i + 1, upper=True) + error(3, z_upper)
    assert s_lower > 0
    ratio = t_upper**2 / s_lower**3
    if ratio > worst[0]:
        worst = ratio, i
    assert ratio < F(15,2), (i, float(ratio))
    if i < 5 * GRID:
        vratio = s_lower / z_upper**2
        if vratio < variance_worst[0]:
            variance_worst = vratio, i
        assert vratio > F(79,1000), (i, float(vratio))

zeta_upper = sum((F(1,j**3) for j in range(1,11)), F(0)) + F(1,200)
assert zeta_upper < F(1203,1000)
s_tail = balanced_interval(2, LAST) - F(4,H)
t_tail = 4 * F(9,2) * F(1203,1000) + F(8,H)
tail_ratio = t_tail**2 / s_tail**3
assert tail_ratio < F(15,2)
v_tail = (balanced_interval(2, 5*GRID) - error(2,F(5))) / 25
assert v_tail > F(79,1000)

# Fourth moment covariance reduces to the single infinite row.
fourth_ratio = (F(7,30)*F(22,7)**4 + F(5,H))/(F(314,100)**2/6 - F(1,H))
assert fourth_ratio < 14
# Four rows; each right sum differs by at most max(f_2)/h.
variance_upper = 4 * (F(22,7)**2/6 + F(1,H))
assert variance_upper < 7
# Constants used to bound max f_q, from e > 27/10.
assert F(4)/F(27,10)**2 < 1
assert F(27)/F(27,10)**3 < 2
assert F(256)/F(27,10)**4 < 5

result = {
    'status': 'PASS', 'h_min': H,
    'third_moment_ratio_constant': '15/2',
    'core_variance_constant': '79/1000',
    'fourth_moment_constant': 14,
    'variance_upper_constant': 7,
    'grid_denominator': GRID, 'z_interval': [1,16],
    'exp_taylor_degree': 150, 'rounding_denominator': DEN,
    'third_moment_worst_cell': [f'{worst[1]}/{GRID}',f'{worst[1]+1}/{GRID}'],
    'third_moment_upper_decimal': float(worst[0]),
    'third_moment_upper_exact': [str(worst[0].numerator),str(worst[0].denominator)],
    'third_moment_tail_upper_decimal': float(tail_ratio),
    'core_variance_worst_cell': [f'{variance_worst[1]}/{GRID}',f'{variance_worst[1]+1}/{GRID}'],
    'core_variance_lower_decimal': float(variance_worst[0]),
    'core_variance_lower_exact': [str(variance_worst[0].numerator),str(variance_worst[0].denominator)],
    'core_variance_tail_lower_decimal': float(v_tail),
    'fourth_moment_upper_decimal': float(fourth_ratio),
    'variance_upper_decimal': float(variance_upper),
}
Path(__file__).with_suffix('.json').write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2)+'\n')
print(json.dumps(result,indent=2))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{verify\_balanced\_tail\_h100.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small 16448f7811ca7c2ee926cf9ad46979572a44fe570ce9d1ea52c6f765356de95a}.
%<source:verify_balanced_tail_h100.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
"""Exact rational Fourier absolute-moment tail certificate for balanced blocks.

For h>=100,n>=h,B_n=Q_n Q_floor(n/2) Q_floor(n/4) Q_floor(n/8),
x=sigma*integral_{2/min(n,5h)<=|theta|<=pi}|phi(theta)|dtheta < 1/3000,
y=sigma^3*integral_{2/min(n,5h)<=|theta|<=pi}theta^2|phi(theta)|dtheta < 1/50.
See balanced_tail_h100.md for the proof and definitions.
All certificate comparisons use fractions; decimal output is descriptive only.
"""
from fractions import Fraction as F
from math import isqrt
from pathlib import Path
import json

H=100
GRID=256
DEN=10**24
TAIL_DEN=10**32

def down(x,den=DEN):
    return F(x.numerator*den//x.denominator,den)
def up(x,den=DEN):
    return F(-((-x.numerator*den)//x.denominator),den)
def exp_interval(x):
    term=total=F(1)
    for j in range(1,81):
        term*=x/j
        total+=term
    nxt=term*x/81
    return down(total),up(total+nxt/(1-x/82))

elo=[]; vlow=[]; vhigh=[]
for i in range(5*GRID+1):
    lo,hi=exp_interval(F(i,GRID))
    elo.append(lo)
    vlow.append(down(hi/(1+hi)**2))
    vhigh.append(up(lo/(1+lo)**2))
Ilo=[F(0)]; Ihi=[F(0)]
for i in range(5*GRID):
    l,u=F(i,GRID),F(i+1,GRID)
    area=(u**3-l**3)/3
    Ilo.append(Ilo[-1]+vlow[i+1]*area)
    Ihi.append(Ihi[-1]+vhigh[i]*area)

def I2lo(x):
    assert 0<=x<=5
    i=x.numerator*GRID//x.denominator
    if i==5*GRID:return Ilo[i]
    return down(Ilo[i]+vlow[i+1]*(x**3-F(i,GRID)**3)/3)

def I2hi(x):
    assert 0<=x<=5
    i=x.numerator*GRID//x.denominator
    if i==5*GRID:return Ihi[i]
    return up(Ihi[i]+vhigh[i]*(x**3-F(i,GRID)**3)/3)

def Jlo(z):
    ans=F(0)
    for a in range(4):
        x=z/2**a
        assert (x*GRID).denominator==1
        el=elo[int(x*GRID)]
        ans+=F(1,2)-1/(1+el)
    return down(ans)

# e^(k/100) lower bounds by one exact Taylor lower bound and rounded
# multiplication. Induction certifies every table entry independently
# of floating point. Tail reciprocals are rounded upward.
r=down(sum((F(1,100)**j / __import__('math').factorial(j) for j in range(21)),F(0)),TAIL_DEN)
exp_table=[F(1)]
for k in range(1,20001):
    exp_table.append(down(exp_table[-1]*r,TAIL_DEN))
def neg_exp_up(x):
    assert 0<=x<=200
    k=x.numerator*100//x.denominator
    return up(1/exp_table[k],TAIL_DEN)

def sqrt_up(x):
    den=10**6
    t=isqrt(x.numerator*den*den//x.denominator)+1
    ans=F(t,den)
    assert ans*ans>x
    return ans

results=[]
first_tail=None
for zi in range(32,161):
    zlo=F(zi,32)
    zhi=F(zi+1,32) if zi<160 else None
    mass=Jlo(zlo)
    if zhi is None:
        # f2(x)<=x^2 e^-x is unimodal with maximum<1; its integral is2.
        # Four rows give sigma²/h³ <=4(2+1/H)<9.
        assert 4*(2+F(1,H))<9
        B=F(9)
    else:
        # f2 increases on[0,2]: 2/x-tanh(x/2)>0.
        B=sum((I2hi(zhi/2**a)+((zhi/2**a)**2*vhigh[int(zhi/2**a*GRID)] if zhi/2**a<=2 else F(1))/H for a in range(4)),F(0))
    R=sqrt_up(B)
    s0=s2=F(0)
    minimum=F(100)
    start=2/min(zhi or F(5),F(5))
    first=start.numerator*40//start.denominator
    for si in range(first,320):
        l,u=F(si,40),F(si+1,40)
        V=F(0)
        for a in range(4):
            c=min(zlo/2**a,2/u)
            V+=I2lo(c-F(1,H))-(F(1,H) if c>2 else F(0))
        prefix=F(25,72)*l*l*V
        abel=mass-F(1,2*H)-F(24,23)/l
        rate=max(prefix,abel)
        # h^(1/2)e^-rate*h and h^(3/2)e^-rate*h decrease for h>=H.
        assert rate>F(3,2*H)
        minimum=min(minimum,rate)
        E=neg_exp_up(H*rate)
        s0+=(u-l)*E
        s2+=(u**3-l**3)/3*E
    xn=2*R*10*s0
    yn=2*R**3*1000*s2
    ratefar=mass-F(1,2*H)-F(3,23)
    assert ratefar>F(9,2*H)
    E=neg_exp_up(H*ratefar)
    xf=2*F(22,7)*R*1000*E
    yf=F(2,3)*F(22,7)**3*R**3*10**9*E
    x,y=xn+xf,yn+yf
    if zi==32:first_tail=(x/R,y/R**3)
    assert x<F(1,3000),(zi,float(x))
    assert y<F(1,50),(zi,float(y))
    results.append({"z_lower":str(zlo),"z_upper":str(zhi) if zhi is not None else "infinity","sigma_squared_upper":float(B),"minimum_near_rate":float(minimum),"far_rate":float(ratefar),"x_upper":float(x),"y_upper":float(y)})
central_variance=sum((F(1,12*2**(3*a))+F(1,8*H*2**(2*a))+F(1,24*H**2*2**a) for a in range(4)),F(0))
assert central_variance<F(1,10)
Rc=sqrt_up(F(1,10))
xc,yc=first_tail[0]*Rc,first_tail[1]*Rc**3
assert xc<F(1,3000) and yc<F(1,50)
report={"central_tn_le_1":{"variance_bound":"1/10","variance_certificate_upper":float(central_variance),"x_upper":float(xc),"y_upper":float(yc)},"status":"PASS","h_min":H,"frequency_start":"2/min(n,5h)","x_bound":"1/3000","y_bound":"1/50","integral_grid_denominator":GRID,"z_grid_denominator":32,"s_grid_denominator":40,"exp_grid_denominator":100,"rounding_denominator":DEN,"tail_rounding_denominator":TAIL_DEN,"max_x_upper":max(r['x_upper']for r in results),"max_y_upper":max(r['y_upper']for r in results),"parameter_cells":results}
Path(__file__).with_suffix('.json').write_text(json.dumps(report,indent=2)+'\n')
print(json.dumps({k:v for k,v in report.items()if k!='parameter_cells'},indent=2))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{verify\_balanced\_h100\_constants.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small 3f06b305f1da42a90c036b2f6ce5603ec031a5f3ac70fb8e9c427cc9247e7d35}.
%<source:verify_balanced_h100_constants.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
"""Exact scalar/core/first-tail checks for balanced_fourier_h100.md.

Moment and remaining-tail rational certificates are separate. No coefficient scan.
"""
from fractions import Fraction as F
from pathlib import Path
import json


def exp_lower(x, terms=100):
    term=total=F(1)
    for j in range(1,terms+1):
        term *= x/j
        total += term
    return total

H=100
a,b=F(11,12),F(64,49)
C=F(15,2)
phase_coefficient=F(7,40)
assert exp_lower(F(5))>144
assert F(145,143)**3/6 < phase_coefficient
assert F(143,145)**2>F(49,64)
assert F(14,25)<1
assert 2*F(22,7)/a**3<9

# Lower Gaussian endpoint is > 16/5. Each retained moment >49/50.
U=F(79,10)
assert b*U>F(16,5)**2
assert exp_lower(F(128,25))>160
assert F(5,2)**2 < 2*F(314,100)
tail_fraction_0=F(4,5)*F(5,16)/160
tail_fraction_2=F(4,5)*(F(16,5)+F(5,16))/160
assert tail_fraction_0<F(1,50) and tail_fraction_2<F(1,50)
assert 1-F(1,3*H**2)>F(999,1000)
positive=F(999,1000)*F(49,50)**2*2*F(314,100)/b**2
assert positive>F(7,2)
phase=120*F(22,7)*phase_coefficient**2*C/(a**5*H)
assert phase<F(27,20)

# delta < |theta| <= 2 delta. Put r=L |theta| and U=(sigma/L)^2.
# f(r)=r^2-r^4/12 increases on [1,2]. For p=1/2,3/2,
# U^p exp[-Uf(r)/2] decreases when U>=79/10.
# Integrate via exact positive rectangles, with sqrt(79/10)<3.
N=256
assert U<9
assert U*F(11,12)>3
x=y=F(0)
for i in range(N):
    l=1+F(i,N)
    u=1+F(i+1,N)
    f=l*l-l**4/12
    eminus=1/exp_lower(U*f/2)
    x+=F(6,N)*eminus
    y+=F(6,N)*U*u*u*eminus
assert x<F(1,40),float(x)
assert y<F(1,4),float(y)
central_phase=120*F(22,7)/(36*a**5*F(79,1000)*H)
assert central_phase<F(41,20)
xt=F(1,40)+F(1,3000)
yt=F(1,4)+F(1,50)
assert 3*xt+3*yt+xt*yt<F(9,10)
assert F(7,2)-F(27,20)-F(9,10)==F(5,4)
assert F(7,2)-F(41,20)-F(9,10)==F(11,20)
result={
  'status':'PASS', 'h_min':H,
  'positive_core_lower_decimal':float(positive),
  'phase_loss_upper_decimal':float(phase),
  'central_phase_loss_upper_decimal':float(central_phase),
  'total_tail_bound':'9/10',
  'first_tail_x_upper_decimal':float(x),
  'first_tail_y_upper_decimal':float(y),
  'first_tail_x_bound':'1/40', 'first_tail_y_bound':'1/4',
  'first_tail_grid':N,
}
Path(__file__).with_suffix('.json').write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2)+'\n')
print(json.dumps(result,indent=2))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{verify\_four\_color\_prefix.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small fa5dffc5c3eb42abf182560c7f1e173bef4431ae9c725398cf70d5d985b34556}.
%<source:verify_four_color_prefix.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
"""Exact finite part of the all-index q4 proof; see four_color_partition_tail.md.

No finite list alone is claimed to prove an infinite assertion.
The analytic argument covers indices n >= 10000.
"""
import hashlib, json, time
from pathlib import Path

N = 10000
start = time.time()
# F(x) = product_{j odd}(1-x^j)^(-4).  Four independent colors.
a = [1] + [0] * N
for j in range(1, N + 1, 2):
    for color in range(4):
        for k in range(j, N + 1):
            a[k] += a[k-j]
assert a[:10] == [1, 4, 10, 24, 51, 100, 190, 344, 601, 1024]
T = [a[k]*a[k] - a[k-1]*a[k+1] for k in range(1, N)]
assert all(v > 0 for v in T)
# Independently recover each coefficient using n a_n = 4 sum sigma_odd(j) a_(n-j).
sigma_odd = [0] * (N + 1)
for d in range(1, N + 1, 2):
    for k in range(d, N + 1, d):
        sigma_odd[k] += d
for n in range(1, N + 1):
    assert n*a[n] == 4*sum(sigma_odd[j]*a[n-j] for j in range(1,n+1))
coeff_bytes = ("\n".join(map(str,a)) + "\n").encode()
result = {
    "coefficient_range": [0,N],
    "strict_log_concavity_range": [1,N-1],
    "failed_indices": [],
    "minimum_integer_turan": min(T),
    "coefficient_sha256": hashlib.sha256(coeff_bytes).hexdigest(),
    "independent_recurrence_verified": True,
    "elapsed_seconds": time.time()-start,
}
path = Path(__file__).with_name('four_color_prefix.json')
path.write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2)+"\n")
print(json.dumps(result,indent=2))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{check\_q4\_prefix\_independent.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small 2e2326abcd2fab7cc863bd70a13f7e9b1aec7c8baab0f84760376ab95aa8e375}.
%<source:check_q4_prefix_independent.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
"""Exact q4 prefix via log derivative; not an infinite-tail proof."""
import json,hashlib
from pathlib import Path
N=10001
b=[0]*(N+1)
for d in range(1,N+1,2):
 for j in range(d,N+1,d):b[j]+=4*d
a=[0]*(N+1);a[0]=1
for n in range(1,N+1):
 s=sum(b[j]*a[n-j] for j in range(1,n+1))
 assert s%n==0
 a[n]=s//n
bad=[n for n in range(1,N) if a[n]*a[n]<a[n-1]*a[n+1]]
result={'checked_internal_indices':[1,N-1],'failures':bad,'coefficient_sha256':hashlib.sha256(('\n'.join(map(str,a))+'\n').encode()).hexdigest(),'method':'n*a_n=4 sum_(j=1)^n sigma_odd(j)*a_(n-j), exact integer arithmetic','scope':'finite verification only'}
Path(__file__).with_suffix('.json').write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2)+'\n')
print(json.dumps(result))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{verify\_small\_q4\_transfer.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small 1c2d8594fb17d8e4f88c7ccb58d787845814e28d618126d40201c059d89c96c2}.
%<source:verify_small_q4_transfer.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
"""Exact finite bridge for q4 indices below 10000, not a BBFM proof alone."""
from fractions import Fraction as F
from pathlib import Path
import runpy,contextlib,io,json
with contextlib.redirect_stdout(io.StringIO()):d=runpy.run_path(str(Path(__file__).with_name('check_q4_prefix_independent.py')))
a=d['a']
assert 100*a[9998]<99*a[9999]
worst=F(1)
for k in range(1,10000):
 T=a[k]*a[k]-a[k-1]*a[k+1]
 assert 10**9*T>a[k]*a[k],k
 worst=min(worst,F(T,a[k]*a[k]))
assert 400*10**12*99**3501<100**3501
# Large-index bridge with the already-proved R_k<exp(-1.7/sqrt(k)).
assert 40400<201**2
assert F(17*3501,10*201)>F(59,2)
def lower_exp(x,N):
 t=s=F(1)
 for j in range(1,N+1):
  t*=x/j;s+=t
 return s
assert lower_exp(F(3),8)>20
assert lower_exp(F(5,2),10)>12
assert F(4*120,12*20**9)<F(9,40*40404**2)
result={'status':'PASS','small_indices':[1,9999],'R_9999_upper':'99/100','relative_Turan_lower':'1/1000000000','minimum_relative_Turan_exact':[str(worst.numerator),str(worst.denominator)],'minimum_relative_Turan_decimal':float(worst),'d_min':3500,'deficit_upper':'1/1000000000000','large_indices':[10000,40400],'balanced_parameter_cutoff':28000,'scope':'conditional on the independently audited H=100 Fourier theorem; not a finite residual scan'}
Path(__file__).with_suffix('.json').write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2)+'\n')
print(json.dumps(result))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{check.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small 0a657b45ba725cabb4ebb7e42af74804927ac15d12cf2714720b00342b37564c}.
%<source:check.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
import json, time
from fractions import Fraction
from pathlib import Path
N=256
p=[1]
rows=[]
t0=time.time()
for n in range(1,N+1):
    m=n
    while True:
        p += [0]*m
        for k in range(len(p)-1,m-1,-1): p[k]+=p[k-m]
        if m%2:break
        m//=2
    bad=[]
    # palindromicity halves work, exact integer certificate.
    assert p==p[::-1]
    for k in range(1,(len(p)+1)//2):
        d=p[k]*p[k]-p[k-1]*p[k+1]
        if d<0:bad.append((k,p[k-1],p[k],p[k+1],d))
    rows.append({'n':n,'degree':len(p)-1,'bad_half_indices':bad})
    if bad:print('failure',n,bad[:8],flush=True)
    if n%32==0:print('checked',n,'degree',len(p)-1,'sec',time.time()-t0,flush=True)
Path('research/bbfm_logconcavity/exact_check.json').write_text(json.dumps(rows))
print('DONE',time.time()-t0)
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{check\_balanced\_blocks.py}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small 8f0fbd1cd3a12ad569e6d84cdbc2dc9524af65d87b0fdccd2febb10b02de2c2f}.
%<source:check_balanced_blocks.py>
\begin{lstlisting}
import json
p=[1];bad=[]
for n in range(1,257):
 for s in range(4):
  if n%(1<<s):break
  k=n//(1<<s);q=p+[0]*k
  for j,a in enumerate(p):q[j+k]+=a
  p=q
 if n>=8:
  hits=[j for j in range(1,(len(p)+1)//2) if p[j]*p[j]<p[j-1]*p[j+1]]
  if hits:bad.append([n,hits])
print(json.dumps({'range':[8,256],'failures':bad,'last_degree':len(p)-1,'status':'finite exact verification only'}))
\end{lstlisting}
%</source>

\subsection{dyadic\_certificate.cpp}
\noindent SHA-256: \texttt{\small b3154e0381004bb878a33af90f513d5e20b3ab157db6080bf1fc34c2ccb9c7fe}.
%<source:dyadic_certificate.cpp>
\begin{lstlisting}
// Finite certificate engine for B_n = Q_n Q_floor(n/2) Q_floor(n/4) Q_floor(n/8).
// All arithmetic is integer-only. Build via test_dyadic_certificate.py so that
// SOURCE_SHA256 records this exact source. No assertion is made beyond the
// explicitly reported finite (n,k) ranges. See dyadic_certificate.md.
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstdint>
#include <iostream>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <vector>
#ifndef SOURCE_SHA256
#error "Define SOURCE_SHA256 as the SHA256 of this source file."
#endif
using U128 = unsigned __int128;
using I128 = __int128;
constexpr uint64_t HIGH = uint64_t(1) << 63;
struct Dyadic { uint64_t m = 0; int64_t e = 0; };
struct Interval { Dyadic lo, hi; };

static int bits(U128 x) {
    uint64_t hi = uint64_t(x >> 64);
    return hi ? 128 - __builtin_clzll(hi) :
        (uint64_t(x) ? 64 - __builtin_clzll(uint64_t(x)) : 0);
}
static int64_t exponent(I128 e) {
    if (e < std::numeric_limits<int64_t>::min() ||
        e > std::numeric_limits<int64_t>::max())
        throw std::overflow_error("dyadic exponent overflow");
    return int64_t(e);
}
static bool valid(Dyadic a) { return a.m ? a.m >= HIGH : a.e == 0; }
// Exact numerator n, then directed normalization to a 64-bit mantissa.
static Dyadic normalize(U128 n, int64_t e, bool upward) {
    if (!n) return {};
    int shift = bits(n) - 64;
    if (shift <= 0)
        return {uint64_t(n << -shift), exponent(I128(e) + shift)};
    U128 q = n >> shift;
    if (upward && (n - (q << shift))) ++q;
    I128 out_e = I128(e) + shift;
    if (q == (U128(1) << 64)) { q >>= 1; ++out_e; }
    return {uint64_t(q), exponent(out_e)};
}
static Dyadic add(Dyadic a, Dyadic b, bool upward) {
    if (!a.m) return b;
    if (!b.m) return a;
    if (a.e < b.e) std::swap(a,b);
    I128 d = I128(a.e) - b.e;
    if (d <= 64) {
        // Maximum is (2^64-1)*2^64 + (2^64-1) = 2^128-1.
        U128 numerator = (U128(a.m) << unsigned(d)) + b.m;
        return normalize(numerator, b.e, upward);
    }
    // 0 < b < 2^a.e: lower rounds to a, upper adds exactly one ulp.
    if (!upward) return a;
    if (a.m == std::numeric_limits<uint64_t>::max())
        return {HIGH, exponent(I128(a.e) + 1)};
    return {a.m + 1, a.e};
}
static Interval add(Interval a, Interval b) {
    return {add(a.lo,b.lo,false), add(a.hi,b.hi,true)};
}
// Compare a*b with c*d exactly; exponent sums use signed 128-bit integers.
static int compare_products(Dyadic a, Dyadic b, Dyadic c, Dyadic d) {
    U128 p = U128(a.m)*b.m, q = U128(c.m)*d.m;
    if (!p || !q) return (p>q)-(p<q);
    int bp=bits(p), bq=bits(q);
    I128 ep=I128(a.e)+b.e, eq=I128(c.e)+d.e;
    I128 tp=ep+bp, tq=eq+bq;
    if (tp != tq) return tp > tq ? 1 : -1;
    // Equal top-bit positions: align to the larger mantissa bit length.
    // The shifted operand has at most max(bp,bq)<=128 significant bits.
    if (bp < bq) p <<= bq-bp;
