\section{Exact constants for the all modulus cutoff}\label{p7:app:allkconstants}
This is the complete 24-check rational certificate for Section~\ref{p7:sec:allk}.
\begin{lstlisting}[label=p7:src:allkconstants,language=Python]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Exact rational numeric checks for the all-modulus theorem in this manuscript."""
from fractions import Fraction as Q
from math import factorial
from pathlib import Path
import json

def atan_enclosure(x, terms=40):
    s=sum(((-1)**j*x**(2*j+1)/(2*j+1) for j in range(terms)),Q(0))
    z=s+(-1)**terms*x**(2*terms+1)/(2*terms+1)
    return min(s,z),max(s,z)
def exp_lower(x,terms=250):
    return sum((x**j/factorial(j) for j in range(terms)),Q(0))
a,b=atan_enclosure(Q(1,5));c,d=atan_enclosure(Q(1,239))
plo,phi=16*a-4*d,16*b-4*c
Alo,Ahi=plo**2/6,phi**2/6
t=Q(1,200);alpha=Q(747,1000);r=Q(28,1000)
checks={}
def check(name,statement):
    checks[name]=bool(statement)
    assert statement,name
check('saddle threshold <65798',Ahi/t**2<65798)
check('variance upper <3.3/t^3',2*Ahi+t<Q(33,10))
check('quartic loss <.253',(12*Ahi+24*t)/(48*(Alo-t))<Q(253,1000))
check('skew ratio <.028',(3*Ahi+2*t)**2/(Alo-t)**3*t<r)
check('1/(4V)<1e-6',t**3/(4*(Alo-t))<Q(1,10**6))
check('standardized core endpoint >9',(Alo-t)/(4*t)>81)
# Normal second moment bound sqrt(2/pi)*(9+1/9)*exp(-81/2)<1e-15.
check('both Gaussian tail losses <1e-15',Q(82,9)/exp_lower(Q(81,2))<Q(1,10**15))
check('sin x >=.999x for x<=.04',1-Q(4,100)**2/6>Q(999,1000))
# sinh(x)/x <=1+(x^2/6)/(1-x^2/20), by ratios in the tail series.
check('sinh x <=1.001x for x<=.04',1+(Q(4,100)**2/6)/(1-Q(4,100)**2/20)<Q(1001,1000))
check('near tail S ratio >.199',Q(999,1000)**2/(4*Q(1001,1000)**2+Q(999,1000)**2)>Q(199,1000))
check('far tail S ratio >.499',Q(999,1000)**2/(Q(1001,1000)**2+Q(999,1000)**2)>Q(499,1000))
H2=sum((Q(1,j*j) for j in range(1,11)),Q(0));H1=sum((Q(1,j) for j in range(1,11)),Q(0))
check('H10 second >1.549',H2>Q(1549,1000))
check('H10 first <2.93',H1<Q(293,100))
check('near tail exponent >.15',Q(199,1000)*(H2/2-t*H1/2)>Q(15,100))
check('far tail exponent >.15',(Q(1,2)-t/2)*Q(499,1000)>Q(15,100))
check('A phase loss <.152',(r*Q(105,72))**2*alpha**(-9)<Q(152,1000)**2)
check('B phase loss <.017',(r*Q(15,72))**2*alpha**(-7)<Q(17,1000)**2)
check('A core >.847',1-Q(1,10**6)-Q(152,1000)-Q(1,10**15)>Q(847,1000))
check('B core >.982',1-Q(1,10**6)-Q(17,1000)-Q(1,10**15)>Q(982,1000))
expsq=1/exp_lower(2*Q(15,100)/t)
check('A tail loss <.128',32*phi*Q(33,10)**3*t**(-9)*expsq<Q(128,1000)**2)
check('B tail loss <2e-9',2*phi*Q(33,10)*t**(-3)*expsq<Q(2,10**9)**2)
check('both tail bounds increase with t',Q(15,100)>Q(9,2)*t)
check('A final >.719',Q(847,1000)-Q(128,1000)>=Q(719,1000))
check('B final >.981',Q(982,1000)-Q(2,10**9)>Q(981,1000))
result={'method':'exact Fraction arithmetic, Machin pi enclosure, positive exponential Taylor partial sums','passed':sum(checks.values()),'total':len(checks),'checks':checks,'analytic_cutoff':65798,'moduli':'all integers k>=2'}
p=Path(__file__).with_name('constants_certificate.json');p.write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2)+'\n');print(json.dumps(result,indent=2))
\end{lstlisting}

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}
\section{Independent small modulus audit}\label{p7:app:smallaudit}
The expected JSONL record is generated and checked against Table~\ref{p7:tab:classification}
by the replay driver before this audit runs. This script then compares
coin-change coefficients and performs a fresh complete C++ replay.
\begin{lstlisting}[label=p7:src:smallaudit,language=Python]
"""Independent coin-change oracle and fresh full-source replay for k=2,...,29."""
import hashlib,json,pathlib,subprocess,time
D=pathlib.Path(__file__).parent
src=D/'check_small_moduli.cpp';data=D/'small_moduli_65798.jsonl'
start=time.time();N=500
records=[json.loads(line) for line in data.read_text().splitlines() if line.strip()]
assert [r['k'] for r in records]==list(range(2,30))
assert all(r['N']==65798 for r in records)
for r in records:
 for field in ['negative','zero']:
  assert r[field]==sorted(set(r[field]))
  assert all(1<=n<=65798 for n in r[field])
 assert not set(r['negative'])&set(r['zero'])

def coin(k=None):
 b=[1]+[0]*(N+1)
 for part in range(1,N+2):
  if k is not None and part%k==0:continue
  for n in range(part,N+2):b[n]+=b[n-part]
 return b
p=coin()
# Independent signed-index pentagonal generation.
gpent=sorted((j*(3*j-1)//2,(-1)**abs(j)) for j in range(-N,N+1) if j and j*(3*j-1)//2<=N+1)
table=[];values=0;neg=0;zero=0
for r in records:
 k=r['k'];b=coin(k)
 e=[p[n]+sum(s*p[n-k*g] for g,s in gpent if k*g<=n) for n in range(N+2)]
 assert b==e,(k,'b coefficient mismatch')
 negative=[n for n in range(1,N+1) if b[n]**2<b[n-1]*b[n+1]]
 zeros=[n for n in range(1,N+1) if b[n]**2==b[n-1]*b[n+1]]
 assert negative==[n for n in r['negative'] if n<=N],(k,'negative list mismatch')
 assert zeros==[n for n in r['zero'] if n<=N],(k,'zero list mismatch')
 assert not [n for n in r['negative']+r['zero'] if n>N],(k,'unexpected late exception')
 cutoff=max(negative+zeros)+1
 assert b[cutoff-1]**2<=b[cutoff-2]*b[cutoff]
 assert b[cutoff]**2>b[cutoff-1]*b[cutoff+1]
 table.append({'k':k,'finite_last_nonpositive_plus_one':cutoff,'negative':negative,'zero':zeros})
 values+=N+2;neg+=len(negative);zero+=len(zeros)
# Recompile the untouched source; its hard-coded N remains 65798.
binary=D/'audit_small_moduli'
subprocess.run(['g++','-O2','-std=c++17','-Wall','-Wextra',str(src),'-lgmpxx','-lgmp','-o',str(binary)],check=True)
r=subprocess.run([str(binary)],capture_output=True,text=True,check=True)
replay=[json.loads(line) for line in r.stdout.splitlines() if line.strip()]
assert replay==records,'fresh full-source output differs from recorded artifact'
(D/'audit_small_moduli_replay_65798.jsonl').write_text(r.stdout)
report={'status':'PASS','oracle_N':N,'full_replay_N':65798,'moduli':28,
'coefficient_values_compared':values,'oracle_determinants_compared':28*N,
'full_replay_determinants':28*65798,'negative_count':neg,'zero_count':zero,
'source_sha256':hashlib.sha256(src.read_bytes()).hexdigest(),
'data_sha256':hashlib.sha256(data.read_bytes()).hexdigest(),
'full_replay_exit_code':r.returncode,'seconds':time.time()-start,'finite_threshold_table':table,
'all_n_status':'NOT ESTABLISHED BY THIS FINITE AUDIT; requires separately verified analytic tail'}
(D/'audit_small_moduli_results.json').write_text(json.dumps(report,indent=2)+'\n')
print(json.dumps({k:v for k,v in report.items() if k!='finite_threshold_table'},indent=2))
print('threshold table:',[(x['k'],x['finite_last_nonpositive_plus_one']) for x in table])
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Single source extraction and replay}\label{p7:app:replay}
Save this short driver as \texttt{replay.py}. It extracts the six tagged
source listings from the single manuscript, checks that none is missing, compiles
the exact verifier, and runs all certificates in a new directory.
For example,
\begin{lstlisting}[numbers=none]
python3 replay.py KEIO_Regular_Partition_Log_Concavity_2026.tex replay
\end{lstlisting}
The directory \texttt{replay} must not already exist. No files from the
research workspace are used. The finite $N=25703$ run took about
10.4 seconds in the recorded environment; compilation, the independent
audit, and machine differences add to the total elapsed time. The
small-modulus scan and its full replay add further computation, typically
tens of seconds. The driver verifies the complete negative and zero lists
against the table rather than interpreting exit code zero as a sign result.
\begin{lstlisting}[label=p7:src:replay,language=Python]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import pathlib, re, subprocess, sys

if len(sys.argv) != 3:
    raise SystemExit("usage: replay.py MANUSCRIPT.tex NEW_DIRECTORY")
source = pathlib.Path(sys.argv[1]).read_text()
dest = pathlib.Path(sys.argv[2]).resolve()
dest.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=False)
pattern = (r"\\begin\{lstlisting\}\[label=src:(\w+),[^\n]*\]\n"
           r"(.*?)\n\\end\{lstlisting\}")
blocks = dict(re.findall(pattern, source, flags=re.S))
files = {"cpp": "certify.cpp", "oracle": "audit_independent.py",
         "constants": "certify_fourier_constants.py",
         "smallcpp": "check_small_moduli.cpp",
         "allkconstants": "certify_all_k_constants.py",
         "smallaudit": "audit_small_moduli.py"}
for tag, name in files.items():
    if tag not in blocks:
        raise SystemExit("missing source listing: " + tag)
    (dest / name).write_text(blocks[tag] + "\n")

def run(args):
    subprocess.run(args, cwd=dest, check=True)

run(["g++", "-O3", "-std=c++17", "certify.cpp",
     "-lgmpxx", "-lgmp", "-o", "certify"])
with (dest / "cert25703.json").open("w") as out:
    subprocess.run([str(dest / "certify"), "25703"],
                   cwd=dest, stdout=out, check=True)
import json
record = json.loads((dest / "cert25703.json").read_text())
assert record["N"] == 25703 and record["status"] == "PASS"
assert record["exact_checks"] == 43931660
assert record["max_k_exclusive"] == 2737
run([sys.executable, "certify_fourier_constants.py"])
run([sys.executable, "audit_independent.py"])
run(["g++", "-O3", "-std=c++17", "check_small_moduli.cpp",
     "-lgmpxx", "-lgmp", "-o", "check_small_moduli"])
with (dest / "small_moduli_65798.jsonl").open("w") as out:
    subprocess.run([str(dest / "check_small_moduli")],
                   cwd=dest, stdout=out, check=True)
records = [json.loads(line) for line in
           (dest / "small_moduli_65798.jsonl").read_text().splitlines()]
O = lambda m: list(range(1, m+1, 2))
expected = {2: [2,4,8,11,13,14,16,17,19,20,23,26,29,32],
            3: O(57), 4: [1,4,8,11,13,16], 5: O(41), 6: O(11),
            7: O(35), 8: [1,3,5,7,11,13,17,19], 9: O(33),
            10: O(19), 11: O(31), 12: O(21), 13: O(31),
            14: O(19)+[23], 23: O(25)+[29], 27: O(27),
            29: O(25)+[29]}
for k in (15,17,19,21): expected[k] = O(29)
for k in (16,18,20,22,24,25,26,28): expected[k] = O(25)
assert [z["k"] for z in records] == list(range(2,30))
for z in records:
    k = z["k"]
    assert z["N"] == 65798 and z["negative"] == expected[k]
    assert z["zero"] == ({2: [1], 4: [5,7]}.get(k, []))
run([sys.executable, "certify_all_k_constants.py"])
run([sys.executable, "audit_small_moduli.py"])
print("PASS: both finite certificates, all constants, both audits")
print("Reproduction records:", dest)
\end{lstlisting}
\clearpage
\endgroup
% END RENDERED SOURCE 07

\CollectedPart{Involutions and Partial Log Concavity}{The final chapter retains partial results on inversion-parity log concavity for involutions, including infinite edge strips, an eventual linear-window theorem, formal identities and exact obstructions. It does not resolve the full Dukes conjecture. Unproved candidates remain explicitly distinguished from theorems.}

% BEGIN RENDERED SOURCE 08
\begingroup
\chapter{Partial Results on Dukes Inversion Parity Log Concavity}\label{chapter:8}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\thechapter.\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{\theHchapter.\arabic{section}}
\lstset{style=collectedbase}
\lstset{language=Python,basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize,
  columns=fullflexible,keepspaces=true,showstringspaces=false,
  breaklines=true,breakatwhitespace=false,frame=single,
  rulecolor=\color{black},framerule=0.2pt,aboveskip=8pt,belowskip=8pt,
  xleftmargin=3pt,xrightmargin=3pt,tabsize=4}
\begin{chapterabstract}
Let $a_{n,k}$ count involutions of $S_n$ with $k$ inversions. We establish
$a_{n,k}^2\ge a_{n,k-2}a_{n,k+2}$ for every $n\ge4$ in both edge strips
$2\le k\le100$ and $2\le\binom n2-k\le100$, with exactly four equality pairs within $2\le k\le\binom n2-2$.
The lower strip uses stable coefficient polynomials and an exact nonnegative
Newton-basis certificate; the upper strip uses a finite base and reversed
convolution propagation. For every fixed $0<a<b<\infty$ and positive integer
$h$, we also prove strict $h$-step log concavity uniformly for $an\le k\le bn$
when $n$ is sufficiently large. Its threshold is existential, not numerically
explicit. We give conditional likelihood-ratio preservation, exact formal
identities, and obstructions to several stronger induction claims. Bernstein
and four-step strong-minor assertions are recorded only as unproved candidates.
The full all-$n$, all-$k$ Dukes conjecture is not resolved. A self-contained
exact replay program for the two infinite edge strips is included in this
single source file.
\end{chapterabstract}

\section{The problem and the scope of the results}
For $n\ge0$, set
\[
 A_n(q)=\sum_{\pi\in S_n,\,\pi^2=1}q^{\pEightinv(\pi)}
       =\sum_{k\ge0}a_{n,k}q^k,
 \qquad N_n=\binom n2.
\]
All coefficient sequences are extended by zero outside their support.
Write $[m]_t=1+t+\cdots+t^{m-1}$ for $m\ge1$. The basic recurrence is
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:mainrec}
 A_0=A_1=1,\qquad
 A_n=A_{n-1}+q[n-1]_{q^2}A_{n-2}\quad(n\ge2).
\end{equation}
Indeed, an involution either fixes $n$, or pairs $n$ with some $i<n$.
Deleting the pair and standardizing leaves an involution on $n-2$ points;
the removed entries contribute $2(n-i)-1$ inversions. Summing over $i$
gives \eqref{p8:eq:mainrec}. This recurrence and the conjecture below appear
in Dukes \cite{p8:Dukes}.

Dukes' inversion-parity conjecture asks for
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:dukes}
 D_{n,k}:=a_{n,k}^2-a_{n,k-2}a_{n,k+2}\ge0
 \qquad(n\ge4,\ 2\le k\le N_n-2).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, both parity sections in
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:parity}
 A_n(q)=E_n(t)+qO_n(t),\qquad t=q^2,
\end{equation}
are log concave. Here a nonnegative sequence $v$ is log concave when
$v_j^2\ge v_{j-1}v_{j+1}$ for all integers $j$.
Hart and Rowley \cite{p8:HartRowley}, Conjecture 6.1(a), restate the
corresponding type-$A$ length-profile question. Their counterexample for
a type-$B$ unimodality question is not a counterexample to \eqref{p8:eq:dukes}.

The principal conclusions of this paper are the following.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For fixed $k$, $a_{n,k}$ is a polynomial in $n$ for every $n\ge k$,
with degree $k$ and leading coefficient $1/k!$.
\item For every $n\ge4$, \eqref{p8:eq:dukes} holds whenever
$2\le k\le100$ or $2\le N_n-k\le100$. In the original admissible domain,
equality in either strip occurs exactly at
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:equalities}
 (n,k)=(4,2),(4,4),(6,3),(6,5).
\end{equation}
\item For fixed $0<a<b<\infty$ and fixed $h\ge1$, there is an integer
$n_0(a,b,h)$ such that
\[
 a_{n,k}^2>a_{n,k-h}a_{n,k+h}
 \quad(n\ge n_0(a,b,h),\ an\le k\le bn).
\]
No numerical value of $n_0$ is claimed.
\end{enumerate}
The proofs of the infinite edge statements have finite exact computational
parts. Appendix~\ref{p8:sec:code} contains all code needed to regenerate them,
without auxiliary files or third-party Python packages. The other proved
statements are algebraic or analytic. No finite experiment in this paper
is substituted for an unproved infinite assertion.

The compact-saddle method has established precedents, notably De Angelis
\cite{p8:DeAngelis}, building on earlier work of Odlyzko and Richmond.
The finite $q$-Hermite expansion used below is classical
\cite{p8:DLMF}. Neither the general method nor publication priority for
this application is claimed here.

\section{Stable coefficient polynomials}
\begin{p8theorem}\label{p8:thm:poly}
For each integer $k\ge0$, there is a polynomial $P_k(X)$ of degree $k$
and leading coefficient $1/k!$ such that
\[
 a_{n,k}=P_k(n)\qquad(n\ge k).
\]
More precisely, for nonnegative integers $c_{k,d,m}$ independent of $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:binompoly}
 P_k(X)=\sum_{0\le d,m\le k}c_{k,d,m}\binom{X-d}{m}.
\end{equation}
For $k=0$, the only term is $c_{0,0,0}=1$.
\end{p8theorem}
\begin{proof}
The inversion graph of a permutation has one vertex for each position
and one edge for each inversion. If an inversion edge joins $i<j$,
every intermediate position $t$ is adjacent to at least one endpoint:
either $\pi(t)<\pi(i)$, or $\pi(t)>\pi(i)>\pi(j)$.
Consequently each connected component is a position interval. Distinct
components have no inversions between them, so their value intervals
occur in the same order. Thus the components are precisely the unique
direct-sum indecomposable blocks of the permutation.

For an involution each such position interval is also its value interval,
so each block is itself an involution. An indecomposable block of length
$\ell$ has a connected inversion graph and hence at least $\ell-1$
inversions.

Delete all singleton blocks, and fix the resulting ordered list of $m$
non-singleton blocks. Let its total length be $L$, its inversion count
be $k$, and put $d=L-m$. The preceding bound gives $d\le k$; since
each non-singleton block has at least one inversion, also $m\le k$.
There are only finitely many such lists, because $L\le k+m\le2k$.

For $n\ge L$, inserting the $n-L$ singleton blocks into the $m+1$ gaps
can be done in
\[
 \binom{n-L+m}{m}=\binom{n-d}{m}
\]
ways. For $k\le n<L$, the top entry satisfies $0\le n-d<m$, so the
same ordinary binomial coefficient is zero, as required. Counting the
ordered lists with given $(k,d,m)$ proves \eqref{p8:eq:binompoly} for every
$n\ge k$, including the boundary $n=k$.

The degree is at most $k$. A degree-$k$ contribution requires $m=k$;
then each block has exactly one inversion and must be $21$. There is
exactly one such ordered list, giving $\binom{X-k}{k}$. All other terms
have smaller degree. This proves the stated degree and leading coefficient.
\end{proof}

\begin{p8corollary}\label{p8:cor:fixedk}
For every fixed $k\ge2$, $D_{n,k}>0$ for all sufficiently large $n$.
\end{p8corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $n\ge k+2$, $D_{n,k}$ agrees with the degree-$2k$ polynomial
$P_k(n)^2-P_{k-2}(n)P_{k+2}(n)$. Its leading coefficient is
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:leading}
 \frac1{(k!)^2}-\frac1{(k-2)!(k+2)!}
 =\frac{2(2k+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)(k!)^2}>0.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}
This fixed-$k$ conclusion alone gives no threshold uniform in growing $k$.

\section{Exact infinite edge strips}
\subsection{The lower strip}
\begin{p8theorem}\label{p8:thm:low}
For every integer $n\ge4$ and $2\le k\le100$, $D_{n,k}\ge0$.
Within $2\le k\le N_n-2$, its only zeros in this strip are
\eqref{p8:eq:equalities}.
\end{p8theorem}
\begin{proof}
For each $k=2,\ldots,100$, write the polynomial of
Corollary~\ref{p8:cor:fixedk} in Newton's basis at $n=k+3$:
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:newton}
 D_{k+3+x,k}=\sum_{j=0}^{2k}c_{k,j}\binom{x}{j},
 \qquad c_{k,j}=\Delta^jD_{k+3,k}.
\end{equation}
Here $\Delta$ acts on the first index. The degree bound proves the
identity, rather than merely predicting it from extrapolation.
The exact computation in Appendix~\ref{p8:sec:code} establishes that all
\[
 \sum_{k=2}^{100}(2k+1)=10\,197
\]
integers $c_{k,j}$ are nonnegative. Hence \eqref{p8:eq:newton} is
nonnegative for every integer $x\ge0$.

The finite remainder $4\le n\le k+2$ is checked directly from
\eqref{p8:eq:mainrec}. It comprises $4\,950$ checks including
out-of-support centers, of which $4\,279$ are in the original admissible
domain. Two independently coded recurrence evaluations agree, and three
additional values beyond each interpolation interval agree with
\eqref{p8:eq:newton}. These are implementation checks; the polynomiality
proof justifies the infinite tail.

The computed $c_{k,0}$ is positive except for $k=3$. In that case
$c_{3,0}=0$ and $c_{3,1}>0$, so its only tail zero is $n=6$.
Enumeration of the finite remainder gives the other three pairs in
\eqref{p8:eq:equalities}. All equality claims are restricted to the
original admissible domain.
\end{proof}
The replay program also verifies the exact top coefficient
\[
 c_{k,2k}=\binom{2k}{k}-\binom{2k}{k-2},
\]
as follows independently from \eqref{p8:eq:leading}. It reproduces the
archived lower-strip JSON certificate with SHA-256
\begin{center}\small\ttfamily
ad3217b1c51edfdceb79419b85d2a21a412d\\
ba1e559f8f7cc2e2280e2a30c947.
\end{center}
The line break is typographical; the digest is one 64-character string.

\subsection{Convolution and reversed prefixes}
We use two elementary facts about nonnegative sequences. A sequence has
\emph{interval support} if its positive entries form an integer interval;
the zero sequence is allowed when discussing prefixes.

\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:convolution}
Convolution preserves log concavity and interval support for nonnegative
sequences. If $v,h$ have these properties, are supported on nonnegative
indices, and $h_0=1$, then for every $j\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:strictconv}
 (v*h)_j^2-(v*h)_{j-1}(v*h)_{j+1}
 \ge v_j^2-v_{j-1}v_{j+1}.
\end{equation}
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
For an interval-supported sequence $v$, adjacent log concavity implies
that the ratios $v_{j+1}/v_j$ decrease on its positive support. Multiplying
these ratio inequalities shows that every $2\times2$ minor of the
Toeplitz matrix $T(v)_{i,j}=v_{j-i}$ is nonnegative; minors involving
zeros have the same sign by interval support. Conversely its consecutive
row and column minors are the log-concavity inequalities. Thus log
concavity with interval support is equivalent to Toeplitz total
positivity of order two.

The convolution identity is $T(v*h)=T(v)T(h)$. Cauchy--Binet expresses
each $2\times2$ minor of the product as a sum of nonnegative products
of minors, proving log concavity. The support of a convolution is the
sum of the two support intervals. For rows $0,1$ and columns $j,j+1$,
the intermediate pair $j,j+1$ contributes exactly
\[
 (v_j^2-v_{j-1}v_{j+1})
 \det\begin{pmatrix}h_0&h_1\\h_{-1}&h_0\end{pmatrix}
 =v_j^2-v_{j-1}v_{j+1}.
\]
All remaining terms are nonnegative, proving \eqref{p8:eq:strictconv}.
For finitely many prescribed coefficients all matrices can be truncated
to a sufficiently large finite index set, so no infinite summation
issue occurs.
\end{proof}

Define the reversed polynomial
\[
 B_n(q)=q^{N_n}A_n(q^{-1})=\sum_{d\ge0}r_{n,d}q^d.
\]
Reversal of \eqref{p8:eq:mainrec} gives
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:reverse}
 B_n=q^{n-1}B_{n-1}+[n-1]_{q^2}B_{n-2}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $N_n-N_{n-1}=n-1$, $N_n-N_{n-2}=2n-3$, and the top degree
of $q[n-1]_{q^2}$ is $2n-3$.

\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:propagate}
Fix $D\ge2$. Suppose the parity prefixes of $B_n$ through degree $D+2$
have interval support and are log concave for $4\le n\le D+3$.
Then the same assertions hold for every $n\ge4$.
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
When $n>D+3$, the first summand of \eqref{p8:eq:reverse} vanishes through
degree $D+2$. Each relevant parity prefix is therefore the corresponding
prefix of the convolution of the parity prefix of $B_{n-2}$ with an
all-ones sequence of length $n-1$. Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:convolution} applies;
truncation preserves both properties. The two terminal base rows start
the two parity classes of $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{p8theorem}\label{p8:thm:high}
For every $n\ge4$ and $2\le d\le100$,
\[
 r_{n,d}^2\ge r_{n,d-2}r_{n,d+2}.
\]
Equivalently, \eqref{p8:eq:dukes} holds for $2\le N_n-k\le100$.
Its admissible equality pairs are exactly \eqref{p8:eq:equalities}.
\end{p8theorem}
\begin{proof}
The exact base in Appendix~\ref{p8:sec:code} checks all $9\,900$ inequalities
with $4\le n\le103$, $2\le d\le100$, including out-of-support centers,
and both complete parity-prefix support conditions through degree $102$.
The even prefix has interior centers $2,4,\ldots,100$; the odd prefix
has interior centers $3,5,\ldots,99$. Thus all required interior
inequalities are present. Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:propagate} proves the infinite
claim.

For strictness, \eqref{p8:eq:strictconv} shows that after $n=103$ the defect
at deficiency $d$ is at least that at the same deficiency in row $n-2$.
All tested defects in terminal rows $102,103$ are strictly positive.
The only admissible base zeros are
\[
 (n,d)=(4,2),(4,4),(6,10),(6,12).
\]
Translating $k=N_n-d$ gives \eqref{p8:eq:equalities}.
\end{proof}

\begin{p8corollary}
If $n$ is the least row in which \eqref{p8:eq:dukes} fails, every failing
deficiency $d=N_n-k$ satisfies $d\ge n-3$.
\end{p8corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $d\le n-4$, the reversed recurrence through degree $d+2$ is the
convolution of row $n-2$ with the uniform kernel. That row is log
concave by minimality. Its parity sections have interval support:
this holds in rows $2,3$, and the recurrences
\[
 E_n=E_{n-1}+t[n-1]_tO_{n-2},\qquad
 O_n=O_{n-1}+[n-1]_tE_{n-2}
\]
preserve it. The odd summands start at degree zero; the even supports
start at zero and one and meet. Reversal preserves interval support.
Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:convolution} excludes a failure at such $d$.
\end{proof}

\section{Strict log concavity on fixed linear windows}\label{p8:sec:linear}
\begin{p8theorem}\label{p8:thm:linear}
Fix real numbers $0<a<b<\infty$ and an integer $h\ge1$. There exists
$n_0(a,b,h)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:linearclaim}
 a_{n,k}^2>a_{n,k-h}a_{n,k+h}
 \qquad(n\ge n_0(a,b,h),\ an\le k\le bn).
\end{equation}
The threshold is existential. In particular $h=1$ gives ordinary log
concavity and $h=2$ gives Dukes' parity log concavity in these windows.
\end{p8theorem}
The proof includes a global arc bound, not only a local saddle expansion.
The final determinant sign uses an exact Fourier factorization, avoiding
subtraction of two leading asymptotic equivalents.

\subsection{The exact expansion and its amplitude}
For $|q|<1$, put
\[
 w(q)=\frac{q}{1-q^2},\quad
 \Delta(q)=\sqrt{1+4w(q)},\quad
 L(q)=\frac{1+\Delta(q)}2,\quad M(q)=1-L(q),\; z(q)=\frac{M(q)}{L(q)}.
\]
Near any compact subinterval of $(0,1)$ the branches are fixed by
$\Delta(r)>0$. Then $L+M=1$, $LM=-w$ and $|z(r)|<1$.
Use $(a;t)_j=\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(1-at^i)$ and
$\pEightgbinom njt=(t;t)_n/((t;t)_j(t;t)_{n-j})$.

\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:qhermite}
On these neighborhoods,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:qhermite}
 A_n(q)=\sum_{j=0}^n\pEightgbinom nj{q^2}L(q)^{n-j}M(q)^j.
\end{equation}
If $I\Subset(0,1)$ is compact, there is a complex neighborhood of $I$
on which, uniformly,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:amplitude}
 A_n(q)=L(q)^n\bigl(F(q)+O(\tau^n)\bigr),\qquad 0<\tau<1,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:F}
 F(q)=\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{z(q)^j}{(q^2;q^2)_j}
     =\frac1{(z(q);q^2)_\infty},\qquad F(r)>0\quad(r\in I).
\end{equation}
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
The classical Rogers--Szeg\H{o}, equivalently finite $q$-Hermite,
polynomial
\[
 R_n(X,Y;t)=\sum_j\pEightgbinom njt X^{n-j}Y^j
\]
satisfies
\[
 R_n=(X+Y)R_{n-1}-XY(1-t^{n-1})R_{n-2}.
\]
This follows coefficientwise from Gaussian Pascal recursion and
\[
 (1-t^{n-1})\pEightgbinom{n-2}{j-1}t
 =(1-t^{n-j})\pEightgbinom{n-1}{j-1}t.
\]
Substitution $(X,Y,t)=(L,M,q^2)$ gives \eqref{p8:eq:mainrec} with the
correct initial values, proving \eqref{p8:eq:qhermite}. See also the
classical finite expansion in \cite[Eq.~18.28.16]{p8:DLMF}.

Shrink the neighborhood so that $|q|\le r_0<1$ and $|z(q)|\le z_0<1$.
The positive number
\[
 p_0=\prod_{\ell\ge1}(1-r_0^{2\ell})
\]
bounds all denominator products away from zero. For $j\le n/2$,
\[
 \pEightgbinom nj{q^2}
 =\frac{\prod_{\ell=n-j+1}^n(1-q^{2\ell})}{(q^2;q^2)_j}
 =\frac{1+O(r_0^n)}{(q^2;q^2)_j}
\]
uniformly. All Gaussian binomials are uniformly bounded, by bounding
their numerator products with $\prod_{\ell\ge1}(1+r_0^{2\ell})$ and
their denominators with $p_0$. After division by $L^n$, the tails
$j>n/2$ in both sums are $O(z_0^{n/2})$. Summing the first error against
$|z|^j$ proves \eqref{p8:eq:amplitude}, for any
$\tau>\max(r_0,\sqrt{z_0})$ below one.

For $S(z)=\sum_{j\ge0}z^j/(t;t)_j$, the identity
$(1-z)S(z)=S(tz)$ gives the infinite product by iteration.
All products converge uniformly and are nonzero. On the real interval,
$z(r)<0$ and
\[
 F(r)=\prod_{i\ge0}(1+|z(r)|r^{2i})^{-1}>0.
\]
In particular $F$ has a positive minimum on $I$.
\end{proof}
On a slightly smaller complex neighborhood, Cauchy's estimates also
bound every fixed derivative of the error in \eqref{p8:eq:amplitude} by
$C_m\tau^n$. Analytic derivatives of $L,z,F$ are uniformly bounded there.

\subsection{A compact saddle interval}
Set
\[
 \nu(r)=r\frac{L'(r)}{L(r)},\qquad V(r)=r\nu'(r).
\]
Differentiating $L^2-L=w$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:nu}
 \nu(r)=\frac{1+r^2}{2(1-r^2)}
          \left(1-\frac1{\Delta(r)}\right).
\end{equation}
Both positive factors are strictly increasing on $(0,1)$, with positive
derivatives. Thus $V(r)>0$, while $\nu(r)\to0$ as $r\to0$ and
$\nu(r)\to\infty$ as $r\to1$. For $\alpha=k/n\in[a,b]$, the unique
saddle $r=\nu^{-1}(\alpha)$ lies in the compact interval
\[
 I=[\nu^{-1}(a),\nu^{-1}(b)]\Subset(0,1).
\]
On $I$, $V$ is bounded above and below by positive constants.

With $s=\log r$ and $\phi(s)=\log L(e^s)$, one has
$\phi'=\nu$ and $\phi''=V$. Uniform Taylor expansion in a common
complex neighborhood gives a fixed $\delta>0$ and $c>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:localbound}
 \Re\{\phi(s+i\theta)-\phi(s)-i\nu(r)\theta\}
 \le-c\theta^2\qquad(|\theta|\le\delta,\ r\in I).
\end{equation}
For bounded real $x$, uniformly in $r\in I$,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:gaussian}
 n\left\{\phi\left(s+\frac{ix}{\sqrt{nV(r)}}\right)-\phi(s)
    -\frac{i\nu(r)x}{\sqrt{nV(r)}}\right\}\longrightarrow-\frac{x^2}{2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The global complementary arc}
\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:arc}
For every fixed $\delta>0$, there are $C_\delta<\infty$ and
$\eta_\delta<1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:global}
 |A_n(re^{i\theta})|\le C_\delta L(r)^n\eta_\delta^n
 \quad(r\in I,\ \delta\le|\theta|\le\pi).
\end{equation}
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $q=re^{i\theta}$, the limiting transfer matrix is
\[
 T(q)=\begin{pmatrix}1&w(q)\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Each eigenvalue $\xi$ satisfies $\xi^2=\xi+w(q)$, hence
\[
 |\xi|^2\le|\xi|+|w(q)|\le|\xi|+w(r),
\]
so $|\xi|\le L(r)$. Equality in $|w(q)|\le w(r)$ is possible only at
$q=r$ or $q=-r$. At $q=-r$, equality in the first triangle inequality
would force $\xi$ to be negative real, whereas
$\xi^2=\xi-w(r)<0$ would be impossible. Thus the limiting spectral
radius is strictly below $L(r)$ on the complementary arc, including
its negative-real point.

To pass from limiting spectra to finite products, normalize the state:
\[
 Y_n=\begin{pmatrix}A_n(q)/L(r)^n\\A_{n-1}(q)/L(r)^{n-1}\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 Y_n=N_n(q,r)Y_{n-1},
\]
\[
 N_n(q,r)=\begin{pmatrix}
 L(r)^{-1}&q[n-1]_{q^2}L(r)^{-2}\\1&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Uniformly on the compact arc set, these matrices converge geometrically
to $N_\infty(q,r)$. The eigenvalues of $N_\infty$ are $\xi/L(r)$;
compactness bounds their moduli by a common $\rho<1$.

There is a uniform block length $\ell$ with
$\|N_\infty^\ell\|<1/2$ on the whole compact set. Indeed,
Cayley--Hamilton gives a uniform bound
$C(\ell+1)\rho^{\ell-1}$ for these powers. It remains valid at repeated
eigenvalues: the divided difference
\[
 \frac{\lambda_1^\ell-\lambda_2^\ell}{\lambda_1-\lambda_2}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\lambda_1^{\ell-1-j}\lambda_2^j
\]
has modulus at most $\ell\rho^{\ell-1}$, including its limiting value.
Because $\ell$ is fixed and $N_n\to N_\infty$ uniformly, every
sufficiently late product of $\ell$ consecutive actual matrices has
norm below $3/4$. The finitely many early factors and the leftover
factors are uniformly bounded. Multiplying blocks proves
\eqref{p8:eq:global}. This uses convergence of the matrices, not an
invalid inference about arbitrary products from spectral radii alone.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Weighted Fourier positivity and the determinant}
At the saddle for $k/n$, put
\[
 \chi(\theta)=\frac{A_n(re^{i\theta})}{F(r)L(r)^n}e^{-ik\theta}.
\]
It obeys $\chi(-\theta)=\overline{\chi(\theta)}$. Equations
\eqref{p8:eq:amplitude}, \eqref{p8:eq:localbound}, and \eqref{p8:eq:gaussian}
give Gaussian convergence under $\theta=x/\sqrt{nV(r)}$, uniformly
on bounded $x$ and in the prescribed window. On the small arc,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:chibound}
 |\chi(\theta)|\le C e^{-cn\theta^2}+C\tau^n.
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:arc} gives exponential smallness on the complementary arc.
Define
\begin{align*}
 U_h&=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}(1-\cos(h\theta))\Re\chi(\theta)\,d\theta,\\
 W_h&=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}(1+\cos(h\theta))\Re\chi(\theta)\,d\theta,
 \qquad G_0=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{nV(r)}}.
\end{align*}
Then, uniformly in the window,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:weighted}
 U_h=\frac{h^2G_0}{2nV(r)}(1+o(1)),\qquad
 W_h=2G_0(1+o(1)).
\end{equation}
For completeness, in the first assertion use
$1-\cos(h\theta)\le h^2\theta^2/2$ after Gaussian rescaling.
Its normalized integrand is dominated by $C x^2e^{-c'x^2}$.
First truncate to a common large bounded $x$ interval, then use
uniform Gaussian convergence there. The common Gaussian tail can be
made arbitrarily small. The exponential errors in
\eqref{p8:eq:chibound} and \eqref{p8:eq:global} still vanish after the
$n^{3/2}$ normalization. The same argument without the quadratic
weight proves the assertion for $W_h$. Both quantities are therefore
strictly positive for all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly in $k/n$.

Let
\[
 J_j=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\chi(\theta)e^{-ij\theta}\,d\theta,
 \quad C_h=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\chi(\theta)\cos(h\theta)\,d\theta,
 \quad is_h=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\chi(\theta)\sin(h\theta)\,d\theta.
\]
Conjugate symmetry makes $J_j,C_h,s_h$ real. Since
$J_h=C_h+s_h$ and $J_{-h}=C_h-s_h$, the exact identity is
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:fourierfactor}
 J_0^2-J_{-h}J_h=(J_0-C_h)(J_0+C_h)+s_h^2=U_hW_h+s_h^2>0.
\end{equation}
Finally Cauchy's coefficient formula gives
\[
 J_j=\frac{2\pi r^{k+j}a_{n,k+j}}{F(r)L(r)^n}.
\]
All normalization factors are positive, and the powers of $r$ cancel
in the neighboring product. This proves Theorem~\ref{p8:thm:linear}.
For sufficiently large $n$, fixed $a,b,h$ also ensure
$k-h\ge0$ and $k+h\le N_n$.

The compactness is essential. No estimates above are uniform as the
saddle approaches $1$ or $0$. In particular the theorem does not cover
the quadratic regime $k=\Theta(n^2)$, arbitrary $k/n\to\infty$, or a
transition with $k/n\to0$ and $k\to\infty$.

\section{Conditional likelihood ratio preservation}\label{p8:sec:lr}
This section records a proved auxiliary theorem, not an induction proof
of log concavity. Fix a scalar $c>0$, and put $U_r(t)=[r]_t$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:scalar}
 \begin{split}
 E_n&=E_{n-1}+ctU_{n-1}O_{n-2},\\
 O_n&=O_{n-1}+U_{n-1}E_{n-2},
 \end{split}
 \qquad E_0=E_1=1,\quad O_0=O_1=0.
\end{equation}
At $c=1$ these are \eqref{p8:eq:parity}. In this section $c$ is numerical,
not an indeterminate in a coefficientwise ordered ring.

For nonzero nonnegative interval-supported sequences, write
$a\le_{\pEightLR}b$ when
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:LR}
 a_i b_j-a_j b_i\ge0\qquad\hbox{for every }i<j.
\end{equation}
This is the full likelihood-ratio order. Adjacent minors alone do not
suffice on arbitrary separated supports. With ordered support endpoints,
adjacent ratio comparisons on the positive overlap do imply
\eqref{p8:eq:LR}: multiplication gives the distant comparisons, and the
zeros outside the overlap have the required signs.

We use the following elementary consequences of \eqref{p8:eq:LR}.
The order is transitive and is unchanged by positive rescaling.
Convolution by a log-concave interval-supported kernel preserves it:
apply Cauchy--Binet to the two-row matrix with rows $a,b$ and the
Toeplitz kernel. Thus if $a\le_{\pEightLR}b$, $h\le_{\pEightLR}k$ and all four
sequences are log concave with interval support, then
$a*h\le_{\pEightLR}b*k$, by two successive convolutions and transitivity.
If each $a_i\le_{\pEightLR}b_j$, then their positively weighted sums have
the same comparison, by bilinearity of each minor.
Also $U_r\le_{\pEightLR}U_s$ for $r\le s$ and
$\delta_0\le_{\pEightLR}tU_rU_s$, so
$a\le_{\pEightLR}tU_rU_s a$ for log-concave $a$.
All nonzero rows in \eqref{p8:eq:scalar} have interval support, by the
same support induction used after Theorem~\ref{p8:thm:high}.

Define the auxiliary assertions
\begin{align*}
 T_n:\quad&E_{n-1}\le_{\pEightLR}E_n,\quad O_{n-1}\le_{\pEightLR}O_n,\\
 S_n:\quad&E_n\le_{\pEightLR}tU_{n+1}O_n,\quad O_n\le_{\pEightLR}U_{n+1}E_n,\\
 R_m:\quad&E_m\le_{\pEightLR}tU_mU_{m+2}E_{m-1},\quad
 O_m\le_{\pEightLR}tU_mU_{m+2}O_{m-1}.
\end{align*}

\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:bridge}
For $m\ge4$, $S_{m-2}$, $T_{m-1}$, and log concavity of rows $m-2,m-1$
imply $R_m$. No log concavity of row $m$ is needed.
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
In $E_m=E_{m-1}+ctU_{m-1}O_{m-2}$, the first summand is
LR-below $tU_mU_{m+2}E_{m-1}$ by the preceding facts. For the second,
\[
 tU_{m-1}O_{m-2}
 \le_{\pEightLR}tU_{m-1}^2E_{m-2}
 \le_{\pEightLR}tU_mU_{m+2}E_{m-1}.
\]
The first comparison uses $S_{m-2}$; the second uses $T_{m-1}$ and
enlargement of the two uniform kernels. Positive rescaling and summation
give the result. For the odd equation the corresponding comparison is
\[
 U_{m-1}E_{m-2}
 \le_{\pEightLR}tU_{m-1}^2O_{m-2}
 \le_{\pEightLR}tU_mU_{m+2}O_{m-1},
\]
and the first summand is handled identically.
\end{proof}

\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:smooth}
For $m\ge4$, assume $S_{m-1},S_m,T_m,R_m$ and log concavity of rows
$m-1,m$. Then $S_{m+1}$ holds.
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $U=U_m$, $V=U_{m+2}$. Compare
\[
 E_{m+1}=E_m+ctUO_{m-1},\qquad
 tVO_{m+1}=tVO_m+tVU E_{m-1}.
\]
Each left summand is LR-below each right summand. For $E_m$, the
comparisons follow from $S_m$ with kernel enlargement, and $R_m$,
respectively. For $ctUO_{m-1}$, they follow from $T_m$ and $U\le_{\pEightLR}V$,
and from $S_{m-1}$ enlarged to $V$ and convolved by $tU$.
Summation proves the even assertion.

For the odd assertion compare
\[
 O_{m+1}=O_m+UE_{m-1},\qquad
 VE_{m+1}=VE_m+ctVUO_{m-1}.
\]
The same four comparisons follow, respectively, from $S_m$, $R_m$,
$T_m$, and $S_{m-1}$ with kernel enlargement. The scalar $c$ does not
affect LR order.
\end{proof}

\begin{p8lemma}\label{p8:lem:rowpreserve}
If $S_m,T_m$ hold and rows $m-1,m$ are log concave, then $T_{m+2}$ holds.
\end{p8lemma}
\begin{proof}
In
\[
 E_{m+1}=E_m+ctU_mO_{m-1},\qquad
 E_{m+2}=E_{m+1}+ctU_{m+1}O_m,
\]
both summands of $E_{m+1}$ are LR-below $ctU_{m+1}O_m$, by $S_m$
and by $T_m$ with kernel enlargement. Thus
$E_{m+1}\le_{\pEightLR}E_{m+2}$. The odd argument is identical.
\end{proof}

\begin{p8theorem}\label{p8:thm:conditional}
Fix $c>0$ and $N\ge4$. If $E_j,O_j$ are log concave for
$2\le j\le N-1$, then $S_j,T_j$ hold for $3\le j\le N$.
\end{p8theorem}
\begin{proof}
The base rows are
\[
 E_2=E_3=1,\quad O_2=1,\quad O_3=2+t,
 \qquad E_4=1+c(t+t^2+t^3),\quad O_4=3+2t+t^2.
\]
The nonzero adjacent base minors are listed below; omitted minors are
zero. All base pairs have ordered support endpoints, so these checks
establish their full LR orders.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Assertion&Even comparison&Odd comparison\\
\midrule
$T_3$&none&$1$\\
$T_4$&$c$&$1,1$\\
$S_2$&$1$&$1$\\
$S_3$&$2$&$1,1$\\
$S_4$&$3,2c,c,6c$&$1+3c,1+3c,1+3c$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For example, the supports in the even $S_4$ comparison are $[0,3]$
and $[1,7]$. The other comparisons either have a point mass on the
left, or begin together at zero with ordered right endpoints.

For $n\ge5$, $T_n$ follows from Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:rowpreserve} at $m=n-2$.
Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:bridge} gives $R_{n-1}$ from $S_{n-3},T_{n-2}$ and
log concavity through row $n-2$. Lemma~\ref{p8:lem:smooth} then gives
$S_n$ from earlier auxiliary assertions and log concavity through
row $n-1$. Neither conclusion uses log concavity of row $n$.
\end{proof}
Thus the auxiliary comparisons are available one row ahead under the
log-concavity induction hypothesis. They do not themselves imply
log concavity of that new row. For $c<1$, even row $4$ need not be log
concave, so the premise must not be suppressed. Nor does the proof
automatically give coefficientwise statements in $\pEightR[u]$: adjacent
coefficientwise Tur\'an inequalities do not imply all Toeplitz minors
by a numerical ratio argument.

\section{Exact obstructions to stronger induction claims}\label{p8:sec:obstructions}
For sequences $a,b$, put
\[
 D_j(a)=a_j^2-a_{j-1}a_{j+1},\qquad
 M_j(a,b)=2a_jb_j-a_{j-1}b_{j+1}-a_{j+1}b_{j-1}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:mixed}
 D_j(a+cb)=D_j(a)+cM_j(a,b)+c^2D_j(b).
\end{equation}
The following negative quantities refute stronger proposed properties,
not the original conjecture.

\subsection{Actual recurrence summands need not be synchronized}
At $n=6$, the equation $O_6=O_5+[5]_tE_4$ has summand triples
$(4,3,2)$ and $(1,2,3)$ at index $j=1$. Their mixed term is
\[
 2\cdot3\cdot2-4\cdot3-2\cdot1=-2.
\]
The two self-defects are each $1$, so the total is exactly zero.
A proof must account for this cancellation.

There are also infinitely many endpoint failures of same-row mixed
nonnegativity. For odd $n\ge3$, \eqref{p8:eq:reverse}, starting with
$B_3=1+2q^2+q^3$, gives
\[
 [q^0]B_n=1,\qquad[q^1]B_n=0,\qquad[q^3]B_n=1.
\]
If $n\equiv1\pmod4$, $N_n$ is even and
$M_{N_n/2-1}(E_n,O_n)=-1$.
If $n\equiv3\pmod4$, $N_n$ is odd and
$M_{(N_n-1)/2}(E_n,tO_n)=-1$.
These values follow by inserting the displayed terminal coefficients
and the zero coefficients beyond the support. Real-rootedness is also
unavailable: $E_4=1+t+t^2+t^3$ has roots $i,-i$.

\subsection{The available auxiliary cone does not preserve log concavity}
Take abstract inputs at $m=4$, $c=1$,
\[
 E_{m-1}=E_m=1,\qquad O_{m-1}=O_m=1+t=U_2.
\]
They are log concave with interval support. The row comparison $T_m$
holds by equality. The comparisons $S_{m-1},S_m$ hold because a point
mass at zero is LR-below the shifted convolutions and
$U_2\le_{\pEightLR}U_r$ for $r\ge2$. Both bridge comparisons $R_m$ follow
by convolution with $tU_mU_{m+2}$. Nevertheless the update gives
\[
 E_{m+1}=1+tU_4U_2=1+t+2t^2+2t^3+2t^4+t^5,
 \qquad D_1(E_{m+1})=-1.
\]
In \eqref{p8:eq:mixed}, $a=1$, $b=tU_4U_2$ have
$D_1(a)=0$, $M_1(a,b)=-2$, $D_1(b)=1$.
These are not rows of the actual recurrence from its prescribed initial
conditions. They prove that the presently available abstract cone
needs additional recurrence-history information or a quantitative
mixed-curvature bound.

\subsection{Refinement does not remove every obstruction}
Let $F_{n,m}(t)$ be defined by
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:fixedm}
 Z_n(x,q):=\sum_{\pi^2=1}x^{\pEightfix(\pi)}q^{\pEightinv(\pi)}
   =\sum_m x^{n-2m}q^mF_{n,m}(q^2).
\end{equation}
Then
\[
 F_{n,m}=F_{n-1,m}+[n-1]_tF_{n-2,m-1}.
\]
At $(n,m)=(5,2)$ the summands are $[3]_t$ and $[4]_t(2+t)$;
their mixed defect at index $3$ is $-1$.
Writing $F_{n,m,s}=[t^s]F_{n,m}(t)$, another exact minor is
\[
 F_{6,2,4}F_{5,1,4}-F_{5,2,4}F_{6,1,4}
 =7\cdot0-1\cdot1=-1.
\]
Thus joint MTP2 in row size and number of transpositions is false,
so a general MTP2 marginalization argument cannot be invoked.

For the scalar recurrence \eqref{p8:eq:scalar}, the relevant odd
coefficient pairs are
\[
 ([t^5]O_7,[t^6]O_7)=(1+18c,17c),\qquad
 ([t^5]O_6,[t^6]O_6)=(3c,2c).
\]
Their row-LR minor is $15c^2-2c$, negative for $0<c<2/15$.
This does not contradict Theorem~\ref{p8:thm:conditional}, whose earlier
log-concavity premise already fails there, or the original case $c=1$.

\section{Formal identities and their limitations}\label{p8:sec:formal}
\subsection{A positive diagonal convolution identity}
Let $P_k$ be from Theorem~\ref{p8:thm:poly}, and form
\[
 S(X,q)=\sum_{k\ge0}P_k(X)q^k.
\]
For each coefficient, \eqref{p8:eq:mainrec} holds for all sufficiently
large integers $X$, hence as a polynomial identity:
\[
 S(X,q)=S(X-1,q)+\frac{q}{1-q^2}S(X-2,q).
\]
Let $\lambda(q)\in\pEightR[[q]]$ be the unique series with constant term one
satisfying $\lambda^2-\lambda-q/(1-q^2)=0$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:formalS}
 S(X,q)=C(q)\lambda(q)^X,\qquad C(q)=S(0,q),
\end{equation}
where $\lambda^X=\exp(X\log\lambda)$ has polynomial coefficients in
$X$. To prove the identity, the first coefficient at which the two
sides could differ would be a polynomial $R(X)$ satisfying
$R(X)=R(X-1)$ and $R(0)=0$; it is therefore zero. Induction on the
coefficient index proves \eqref{p8:eq:formalS}.

Set $u=q/\lambda(q)$, let $q=Q(u)$ be its formal inverse, and put
$T(u)=\lambda(Q(u))=Q(u)/u$. Define
\[
 \mathcal B_r(u)=\sum_{k\ge0}a_{k+r,k}u^k\qquad(r\ge0).
\]
Formal residue substitution in \eqref{p8:eq:formalS} gives
\begin{align*}
 P_k(k+r)
 &=[q^k]C(q)\lambda(q)^{k+r}\\
 &=[u^k]C(Q(u))T(u)^r\frac{uQ'(u)}{Q(u)}.
\end{align*}
Taking $r=0$ identifies the common prefactor, so
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:diagonal}
 \mathcal B_r=\mathcal B_0T^r,\qquad
 T=1+\frac{u}{1-u^2T^2},\qquad Q=u+\frac{u^2}{1-Q^2}.
\end{equation}
The equation $T=1+\sum_{j\ge0}u^{2j+1}T^{2j}$ recursively determines
a unique solution with nonnegative coefficients. Its first terms are
\[
 T=1+u+u^3+2u^4+2u^5+6u^6+13u^7+22u^8+54u^9+\cdots,
\]
while
\[
 \mathcal B_0=1+u^3+u^4+2u^5+10u^6+17u^7+39u^8+119u^9+\cdots.
\]
The generator $T$ is not log concave: its $u^2$ coefficient is zero
between two positive coefficients. Ordinary convolution closure
therefore does not prove Dukes' inequalities from \eqref{p8:eq:diagonal}.

For completeness the associated forest kernel has the exact formula,
for $r>0$, $n\ge r$, and $k=n-r$,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:forest}
 [u^n]Q(u)^r=\mathbf1_{k=0}+
 \sum_{\substack{1\le b\le\min(k,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)\\b\equiv k\ ({\rm mod}\ 2)}}
 \frac{r}{n-b}\binom{n-b}{b}
 \binom{(k+b)/2-1}{(k-b)/2}.
\end{equation}
To derive it, first solve $W=z(a+b/(1-W^2))$ with independent type
weights. Lagrange inversion at $m$ vertices contributes $r/m$;
choosing $b$ vertices of the second type contributes $\binom mb$, and
the even total outdegree $2s=m-r$ contributes $\binom{b+s-1}s$.
Give the two types weights $u,u^2$. Then $m=n-b$ and $s=(k-b)/2$,
which gives \eqref{p8:eq:forest}; the all-first-type case is $k=0$.
Equivalently,
\[
 a_{n,k}=[u^n]\mathcal B_0(u)Q(u)^{n-k}\qquad(n\ge k).
\]
The actual boundary factor matters. For the artificial factor
$1+16u^3$, the coefficients $[u^6](1+16u^3)Q^r$ at $r=2,4,6$ are
$(38,6,1)$, giving $6^2-38=-2$. This refutes arbitrary-boundary
closure, not the result for the genuine $\mathcal B_0$, whose
$u^3$ coefficient is $1$.

\subsection{Hard dimers and a continuant determinant}
The recurrence for \eqref{p8:eq:fixedm} is
$Z_n=xZ_{n-1}+q[n-1]_{q^2}Z_{n-2}$, with $Z_0=1$, $Z_1=x$.
Classify tilings of a path with $n$ vertices by their final tile, with
monomer weight $x$ and dimer weight $q[i]_t$ on edge $i$. The same
recurrence and bases prove
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:dimers}
 F_{n,m}(t)=
 \sum_{\substack{S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\\|S|=m, S\text{ has no consecutive elements}}}
 \prod_{i\in S}[i]_t.
\end{equation}
For general edge weights $w_i$, let $K_{a,b}$ be this path partition
polynomial on vertices $a,\ldots,b$, with an empty interval assigned
value one. It is also the tridiagonal determinant with diagonal $x$,
superdiagonal $1$, and subdiagonal $-w_i$. Then, for $n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:continuant}
 K_{1,n}K_{2,n-1}-K_{1,n-1}K_{2,n}
 =(-1)^n\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}w_i.
\end{equation}
Indeed the left side is $w_1$ at $n=2$. Expanding both long intervals
at the last vertex makes it $-w_{n-1}$ times the preceding left side,
proving the formula by induction. Its shifted spatial intervals and
alternating sign differ from the coefficient shifts in \eqref{p8:eq:dukes}.
An interval-uniform log-concavity claim is false: replacing the edge
sequence by $[a]_t,[a+1]_t,\ldots$ with $a\ge2$ gives the even
four-vertex polynomial $1+t[a]_t[a+2]_t$, whose first coefficients
are $(1,1,2)$.

\section{Bernstein and four step candidates remain unproved}\label{p8:sec:candidates}
The definitions in this section are algebraic; the infinite positivity
assertions are conjectural. For $p\in\{0,1\}$ put
$J=\lfloor(n-2p)/4\rfloor$. For a nonzero parity class define
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:H}
 H_{n,p}(t,u)=\sum_{j=0}^J
 t^j u^{J-j}(1+u)^jF_{n,p+2j}(t).
\end{equation}
Set $H_{0,1}=H_{1,1}=0$. The coefficient of $u^J$ is the original
parity polynomial. Equivalently this is the Bernstein homogenization
of the fixed-point variable $x^4$ under $x^4=u/(1+u)$.
The transformed recurrence is
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:Hrec}
 \begin{split}
 H_{n,0}&=c_{n,0}H_{n-1,0}+t(1+u)[n-1]_tH_{n-2,1},\\
 H_{n,1}&=c_{n,1}H_{n-1,1}+[n-1]_tH_{n-2,0},
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
where $c_{n,p}=u$ if $n\equiv2p\pmod4$, and $c_{n,p}=1$ otherwise.
To check this, the fixed-point term gains a factor $x^4$ exactly when
the residue of $n-2p$ wraps from $3$ to $0$; homogenization turns that
factor into $u$. Only the even transposition term increases $J$ by
one, giving its extra factor $1+u$ and its factor $t$.

Write $h_{n,p,k}(u)=[t^k]H_{n,p}(t,u)$. The candidate assertion is
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:bernstein}
 h_{n,p,k}^2-h_{n,p,k-1}h_{n,p,k+1}\in\pEightN[u].
\end{equation}
A proof would imply \eqref{p8:eq:dukes} by taking the leading
$u^{2J}$ coefficient. The completed exact finite screen through
$n=120$ found no negative coefficient in $287\,567$ Tur\'an polynomials,
comprising $13\,522\,422$ integer coefficients. This is not an infinite
certificate. The equivalent clean recurrence is \eqref{p8:eq:scalar}
with $c=1+u$, after coefficient reversal with degree padding.

\subsection{The exact four step identity}
For $n\ge5$, put $w_i=[n-i]_t$ for $1\le i\le4$ and
$r\equiv n-2p\pmod4$, $0\le r<4$. Define
\[
 \beta_0=t(1+u),\quad\beta_1=1,\qquad
 \alpha_r=\begin{cases}u&r=0,1,\\1&r=2,3,\end{cases}
\]
\[
 \gamma_r=\begin{cases}u&r=0,\\1&r=1,2,3,\end{cases}\qquad
 \delta_r=\begin{cases}u&r=0,1,2,\\1&r=3.\end{cases}
\]
Then the following is an exact identity:
\begin{equation}\label{p8:eq:fourstep}
 \begin{split}
 H_{n,p}={}&\bigl(u+t(1+u)w_1w_3\bigr)H_{n-4,p}\\
 &+\beta_p\alpha_r(w_1+w_2+w_3)H_{n-4,1-p}\\
 &+t(1+u)\gamma_r w_4(w_1+w_2)H_{n-5,p}\\
 &+\beta_p\delta_r w_4H_{n-5,1-p}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
To verify it, expand the untransformed recurrence four times:
\begin{align*}
 Z_n={}&\bigl(x^4+x^2q(w_1+w_2+w_3)+q^2w_1w_3\bigr)Z_{n-4}\\
 &+\bigl(x^3qw_4+xq^2w_4(w_1+w_2)\bigr)Z_{n-5}.
\end{align*}
Extract parity and apply \eqref{p8:eq:H}; the four residues give exactly
the displayed $\alpha_r,\gamma_r,\delta_r$.
Although \eqref{p8:eq:fourstep} proves
$H_{n,p}-uH_{n-4,p}\in\pEightN[t,u]$, its same-parity multiplier has first
three $t$ coefficients $(u,1+u,2+2u)$. Their Tur\'an difference is
$1-u^2$, not coefficientwise nonnegative. Entrywise positivity of the
four-step transfer therefore does not close the log-concavity argument.

\subsection{A sufficient strong minor invariant}
Define
\[
 S_{n,p;i,j}=h_{n,p,i}h_{n,p,j}-h_{n,p,i-1}h_{n,p,j+1}
 \qquad(0\le i\le j).
\]
The proposed invariant is
\begin{equation}\tag{FS}\label{p8:eq:FS}
 S_{n,p;i,j}-u^2S_{n-4,p;i,j}\in\pEightN[u]
 \qquad(n\ge4).
\end{equation}
\textbf{This assertion is unproved.} If true, it would imply all strong
minors are coefficientwise nonnegative: the base nonzero polynomials
for $n\le3$ are $1$ and $2+t$, and iteration at the same $i,j$ gives,
with $m=\lfloor n/4\rfloor$,
\[
 S_{n,p;i,j}
 =\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}u^{2\ell}
   (S_{n-4\ell,p;i,j}-u^2S_{n-4\ell-4,p;i,j})
   +u^{2m}S_{n\bmod4,p;i,j}.
\]
Taking $i=j$ and then the leading $u$ coefficient would prove Dukes'
conjecture. Strong minors are specified because Cauchy--Binet preserves
them coefficientwise; numerical adjacent-ratio reasoning is not a
substitute over a polynomial ring.

The completed screen through $n=30$ checked $306\,631$ strong-minor
increments and $4\,440\,082$ integer coefficients, finding none negative.
It is only finite evidence for \eqref{p8:eq:FS}. The analogous two-step
assertion is false: at $(n,p,i,j)=(6,1,1,1)$ the aligned increment is
$1-u^2$. No sign-reversing involution, coefficientwise positive
expansion, or invariant induction establishing \eqref{p8:eq:FS} is supplied.

\section{What remains unresolved}
The two certified strips and each eventual fixed linear window are
infinite statements, but they do not cover all of the bulk
$100<k<N_n-100$. The analytic estimates are compact-saddle estimates;
no numerical threshold or moving-saddle extension is inferred from them.

For the recurrence summands the exact unresolved sign is
\[
 D_j(a)+cM_j(a,b)+c^2D_j(b)\ge0.
\]
Prior log concavity controls the two self-defects, and
Section~\ref{p8:sec:lr} supplies LR and smoothing comparisons one row
ahead. Section~\ref{p8:sec:obstructions} shows why these properties do not
bound the negative mixed defect sufficiently. The exact identities in
Sections~\ref{p8:sec:formal} and \ref{p8:sec:candidates} do not supply the
missing cancellation. Every counterexample reported here concerns a
stronger auxiliary property or artificial input, not an actual
counterexample to \eqref{p8:eq:dukes}.

Accordingly, this manuscript records partial results rather than a
solution of the full conjecture. No claim of an exhaustive literature
status determination or priority is made.

\clearpage
\CollectedAppendix
\section{Self contained exact edge certificate replay}\label{p8:sec:code}
The following Python 3 program uses only the standard library and is
embedded verbatim in this single \texttt{.tex} file. It contains no
third-party imports, data inputs, or certificate dependencies. Copy the listing
to \texttt{dukes\_edge\_replay.py}, or extract it from the source with
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
python3 - <<'PY'
from pathlib import Path
s = Path('KEIO_Dukes_Partial_Results_2026.tex').read_text()
a = s.index('\n% BEGIN EXACT REPLAY\n')
a = s.index('\n', s.index(chr(92)+'begin{lstlisting}', a))+1
b = s.index(chr(92)+'end{lstlisting}', a)
Path('dukes_edge_replay.py').write_text(s[a:b])
PY
python3 dukes_edge_replay.py
\end{lstlisting}
All checks use explicit exceptions, so they remain enabled under
\texttt{python3 -O}. The program writes
\texttt{low\_inversion\_certificate\_100.json} in its current working
directory and checks the archived SHA-256 exactly. Its independently
computed reversed prefixes also verify the upper-strip base and the
strict terminal rows. The mathematical polynomiality and propagation
proofs above, not the finite computations alone, justify all $n$.

% BEGIN EXACT REPLAY
\begin{lstlisting}
import hashlib
import json
import math
from pathlib import Path

LIMIT = 100
DIGEST = ('ad3217b1c51edfdceb79419b85d2a21a412d'
          'ba1e559f8f7cc2e2280e2a30c947')
ZEROS = {(4, 2), (4, 4), (6, 3), (6, 5)}


def require(ok, message):
    if not ok:
        raise RuntimeError(message)


def forward(N, K, direct=False):
    rows = [[1] + [0]*K, [1] + [0]*K]
    for n in range(2, N+1):
        a, b = rows[-1], rows[-2]
        if direct:
            row = [a[k] + sum(b[k-1-2*j] for j in
                   range(min(n-2, (k-1)//2)+1))
                   for k in range(K+1)]
        else:
            row, sums = a.copy(), [0, 0]
            for k in range(K+1):
                if k >= 1:
                    sums[k % 2] += b[k-1]
                if k >= 2*n-1:
                    sums[k % 2] -= b[k-(2*n-1)]
                row[k] += sums[k % 2]
        rows.append(row)
    return rows


def newton(values):
    out = []
    while values:
        out.append(values[0])
        values = [b-a for a, b in zip(values, values[1:])]
    return out


def low_certificate():
    K = LIMIT
    Nmax = 3*K+3
    rows = forward(Nmax+3, K+2)
    require(rows == forward(Nmax+3, K+2, True),
            'Independent forward recurrences disagree')
    certificates = []
    small_checks = finite_all = newton_total = 0
    zeros = set()
    for k in range(2, K+1):
        N = k+3
        def D(n):
            return rows[n][k]**2-rows[n][k-2]*rows[n][k+2]
        values = [D(n) for n in range(N, N+2*k+1)]
        c = newton(values)
        require(len(c) == 2*k+1 and min(c) >= 0,
                f'Newton positivity failure at k={k}')
        require(c[-1] == math.comb(2*k, k)
                - math.comb(2*k, k-2), 'Leading coefficient')
        require((c[0] == 0) == (k == 3), 'Tail equality')
        if k == 3:
            require(c[1] > 0, 'Tail strictness')
            zeros.add((N, k))
        for x in range(2*k+1, 2*k+4):
            require(sum(v*math.comb(x, j)
                    for j, v in enumerate(c)) == D(N+x),
                    'Polynomial replay beyond interpolation')
        for n in range(4, N):
            require(D(n) >= 0, f'Finite lower base: {n},{k}')
            finite_all += 1
            if 2 <= k <= n*(n-1)//2-2:
                small_checks += 1
                if D(n) == 0:
                    zeros.add((n, k))
        newton_total += len(c)
        certificates.append({
            'k': k, 'base_n': N, 'degree_bound': 2*k,
            'newton_coefficients': c})
    require((newton_total, finite_all, small_checks)
            == (10197, 4950, 4279), 'Lower counts')
    require(zeros == ZEROS, 'Lower equality classification')
    artifact = {
        'method': 'exact nonnegative Newton-basis polynomial certificates',
        'claim': f'For every n>=4 and 2<=k<={K}, a[n,k]^2 >= a[n,k-2]*a[n,k+2].',
        'status': 'infinite n, bounded k; NOT the full Dukes conjecture',
        'maximum_k': K,
        'newton_coefficients': newton_total,
        'finite_remaining_in_domain_checks': small_checks,
        'two_recurrence_implementations_agree_through_n': Nmax+3,
        'certificates': certificates}
    data = (json.dumps(artifact, indent=2)+'\n').encode()
    digest = hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest()
    require(digest == DIGEST, 'Archived SHA-256 mismatch')
    Path('low_inversion_certificate_100.json').write_bytes(data)
    return {'newton_coefficients': newton_total,
            'finite_checks_all': finite_all,
            'finite_checks_admissible': small_checks,
            'equality_pairs': sorted(zeros), 'sha256': digest}


def upper_certificate():
    D = LIMIT
    K = D+2
    rows = [[1]+[0]*K, [1]+[0]*K]
    checks = 0
    zeros = set()
    # Independently use the reversed recurrence, not reversed
    # coefficients from the lower-strip implementation.
    for n in range(2, D+4):
        a, b = rows[-1], rows[-2]
        row = []
        for d in range(K+1):
            shifted = a[d-n+1] if d >= n-1 else 0
            row.append(shifted + sum(b[d-2*j] for j in
                       range(min(n-2, d//2)+1)))
        rows.append(row)
        if n < 4:
            continue
        for p in (0, 1):
            support = [j for j, x in enumerate(row[p::2])
                       if x > 0]
            require(not support or support ==
                    list(range(support[0], support[-1]+1)),
                    f'Upper prefix support: {n},{p}')
        for d in range(2, D+1):
            defect = row[d]**2-row[d-2]*row[d+2]
            require(defect >= 0, f'Upper base: {n},{d}')
            checks += 1
            if n in (D+2, D+3):
                require(defect > 0, 'Terminal strictness')
            Nn = n*(n-1)//2
            if d <= Nn-2 and defect == 0:
                zeros.add((n, Nn-d))
    require(checks == 9900, 'Upper count')
    require(zeros == ZEROS, 'Upper equality classification')
    # Cross-check full forward rows through n=35.
    full = [[1], [1]]
    for n in range(2, 36):
        a = [0]*(n*(n-1)//2+1)
        a[:len(full[-1])] = full[-1]
        for shift in range(1, 2*n-2, 2):
            for k, x in enumerate(full[-2]):
                a[k+shift] += x
        full.append(a)
        reverse = a[::-1]
        for d in range(K+1):
            expected = reverse[d] if d < len(reverse) else 0
            require(rows[n][d] == expected,
                    f'Forward/reverse cross-check: {n},{d}')
    return {'base_n_through': D+3, 'inequalities': checks,
            'terminal_rows_strict': [D+2, D+3],
            'equality_pairs': sorted(zeros)}


def main():
    report = {'lower_strip': low_certificate(),
              'upper_strip': upper_certificate(),
              'status': 'PASS: both infinite width-100 edge strips',
              'scope': 'NOT the full Dukes conjecture; no linear-window threshold'}
    print(json.dumps(report, indent=2))


if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
\end{lstlisting}
% END EXACT REPLAY

\subsection*{Expected checks and the role of computation}
A successful run reports $10\,197$ nonnegative Newton coefficients,
$4\,950$ lower finite checks ($4\,279$ admissible), the stated digest,
$9\,900$ upper finite inequalities, strict terminal rows $102,103$, and
the same four admissible equality pairs in both strips. It prints a
\texttt{PASS} only after every check succeeds. The fixed width $100$
is deliberate: no larger strip, empirical Bernstein screen, four-step
invariant, or numerical linear-window threshold is certified by this
program. The linear-window theorem is proved analytically in
Section~\ref{p8:sec:linear} and does not depend on numerical samples.
\clearpage
